== Introduction

Welcome to the ArchiCode Command Line Tool Documentation. ArchiCode is a powerful tool designed to assist architects in creating models and generating views using an efficient as-code approach. Drawing inspiration from both the C4 Model and the ArchiMate Standard, ArchiCode provides a data model and a command line interface to streamline your architectural workflow.

With ArchiCode, architects can easily design models and produce views while adhering to the principles of the C4 Model. Our built-in viewpoints handle various levels of abstraction, ensuring your architectural vision is accurately represented in your projects.

Incorporating concepts from the ArchiMate Standard, ArchiCode's model is rooted in the structural Aspect and spans the Application and Technology layers. This integration adds depth and clarity to your architectural creations.

This documentation will guide you through the features, domain, commands, and best practices for utilizing ArchiCode in your architectural projects. Let's explore how ArchiCode can elevate your architecture-as-code journey.

== Availability, License, and Deliverables

ArchiCode is an open-source software hosted on GitHub, fostering transparency and collaboration. It operates under the MIT license, granting users flexibility in its use and modification. The software is distributed as a native program for Linux and macOS on the release page, as a Maven Artifact and as an OCI Image (Docker Image), simplifying integration.

- Source Code: ArchiCode's source code is hosted on GitHub at https://github.com/tmorin/archicode, providing access for developers to explore, contribute, and collaborate.

- Licensing: ArchiCode is licensed under the permissive MIT license, enabling users to freely utilize, modify, and distribute the software.

- Deliverables: ArchiCode is conveniently available as a native program (one file for each of Linux amd64, Linux arm64 and macOS arm64), as a Maven Artifact and as an OCI Image, facilitating easy integration into development workflows. <<install>> tells how to get and check each of them.

- Access to Deliverables: The native programs, with their checksums, are on the release page at https://github.com/tmorin/archicode/releases. The Maven Artifact and the OCI Image can be accessed through the dedicated packages repository at https://github.com/tmorin/archicode/packages.

This accessibility, licensing, and practical distribution emphasize ArchiCode's commitment to fostering an open and collaborative architectural community.

[[install]]
=== Install

ArchiCode is one program, `archicode`, and the commands are the same whichever form of it you run. This section is about getting it and checking that it is the file the project built.

*The native program of a release.* The releases after 0.18.0 carry, on https://github.com/tmorin/archicode/releases, one file for each machine and a file of checksums:

[cols="2,3",options="header"]
|===
| File | For
| `archicode-<version>-linux-amd64` | Linux on an x86-64 processor
| `archicode-<version>-linux-arm64` | Linux on an ARM 64-bit processor
| `archicode-<version>-macos-arm64` | macOS on Apple silicon
| `SHA256SUMS` | the SHA-256 checksum of each of the three files above
|===

Each is a single file, built as a native image: nothing else has to be installed, no Java and no container runtime, and the editor (`archicode editor serve`, see <<serve-the-editor>>) is inside it. `views generate` writes PlantUML source files and draws no image, so the program needs neither PlantUML nor Graphviz either; turning a `.puml` file into a picture is done with PlantUML, as with any form of ArchiCode. On Linux, download the file for your machine and `SHA256SUMS` into one folder, then:

[source,shell]
----
sha256sum --check --ignore-missing SHA256SUMS
chmod +x archicode-<version>-linux-amd64
./archicode-<version>-linux-amd64 --version
----

The first line checks the files of the folder against their lines of `SHA256SUMS` and prints `archicode-<version>-linux-amd64: OK` (`--ignore-missing` leaves out the files you did not download); the last prints `archicode <version>`, and nothing needs a workspace for that. To use it as `archicode`, put it on your `PATH` under that name, for example `install -m 755 archicode-<version>-linux-amd64 ~/.local/bin/archicode`. On macOS, `shasum` replaces `sha256sum` and checks the lines it is given, so give it the line of the file you downloaded:

[source,shell]
----
grep archicode-<version>-macos-arm64 SHA256SUMS | shasum -a 256 --check
chmod +x archicode-<version>-macos-arm64
./archicode-<version>-macos-arm64 --version
----

*The macOS file is not notarized.* It is not signed with an Apple developer identity, so macOS Gatekeeper stops a file that a browser downloaded (the browser marks it as quarantined) with a message that Apple cannot check it for malicious software. Allow it once, either with `xattr -d com.apple.quarantine archicode-<version>-macos-arm64` or in System Settings, Privacy & Security, with *Open Anyway*. A file fetched with `curl` or `gh release download` is not marked and runs as it is. The checksum is how you know the file is the one the release published.

*Where it runs.* The Linux files are built on Ubuntu 24.04 and need glibc 2.34 or later (a system that uses another C library, such as Alpine, which uses musl, was not tried); the macOS file is built on macOS 15 and says it needs macOS 15.0 or later. There is no Windows file and no file for an Intel Mac yet: there, and anywhere else, use the JAR (Java 25) or the OCI Image, which runs the same JAR: `java -jar quarkus-run.jar --version` and `docker run --rm ghcr.io/tmorin/archicode --version` print the same line as the native programs do.

Each file of a release is run on its own kind of machine, as it is published, before the release is made public: its checksum, `--version`, `views generate` on an example of this manual and `editor serve` answering a request. That check is what the project does; it does not replace yours.

== Features

.Structured Application Component Design
ArchiCode provides a structured data model that empowers architects to design and define the intricate composition of application components.

.Precise Technology Node Modeling
Seamlessly define the structure of technology nodes within your architecture to ensure your technology strategies are accurately captured.

.Flexible Source Formats
ArchiCode adapts to your preferences by supporting multiple source formats: YAML, JSON and TOML for the manifests and for the workspace file (the editor reads YAML only, see <<editor-reads>>).

.Manifest-Style Source Code Organization
ArchiCode simplifies the management of complex architectures by embracing a Kubernetes-like manifest approach to organize your source code more efficiently.

.Automated View Generation
Leverage pre-defined viewpoints to automatically generate views that communicate your architectural concepts effectively.

.Intelligent Relationship Management
ArchiCode's viewpoints smartly manage relationships, providing a clear representation of how components interact within your architecture.

.Seamless CI/CD Integration
Integrate ArchiCode seamlessly into your CI/CD pipelines to ensure architectural integrity throughout your development lifecycle.

.Extensibility Through Plug-gable Engines
Customize and extend ArchiCode's functionality by plugging in renderer engines to tailor the tool to match your unique requirements.

.Diverse Viewpoint Providers
ArchiCode's extensibility goes further by integrating viewpoint providers to enhance your architectural perspectives with specialized viewpoints.

.States of an Architecture
A plateau is a state of the architecture, as it is or as it should be, written as a layer: the manifests that say `plateau: <id>` in their header, over the plateau they override, with patches (`REMOVE`, `SET`, `ADD`, `MERGE`) for what they change. ArchiCode computes what is new, changed and removed from one plateau to another and draws it, in the views and in the editor, with marks that never rely on colour. An element can say what it replaces (`replaces`), so that a move, a split or a merge is one change and not a removal plus an addition; the editor proposes the matches it finds and writes the hint in one click, and moves an element under another parent without breaking a reference (see <<plateaus-overview>>).

.Unraveling Clarity Through Facets
The facet feature in Archicode elegantly simplifies complex architectural views, offering tailored visualizations focused on specific model elements and relationships for enhanced clarity and comprehension.

This next parts will comprehensively guide you through ArchiCode's features, commands, and best practices, enabling you to harness the full potential of this tool in your architectural endeavors. Let's delve into the details and unlock the benefits ArchiCode has to offer.

[[domain]]
== Domain

The ArchiCode domain serves as the foundational framework for your architectural endeavors, encompassing essential elements that facilitate and enhance your architectural work:

- Workspace: ArchiCode's workspace provides the supportive backdrop for all your architecture projects, offering an organized and collaborative environment.

- Application Layer: Within the ArchiCode domain, the Application layer hosts a rich array of elements coming from the C4 Model, enabling you to meticulously design and model application components with precision and clarity.

- Technology Layer: Embracing the Technology layer of the ArchiCode domain empowers you to delve into the realm of deployment nodes, strategically configuring your architecture's technological aspects.

- Viewpoint and Views: Navigating within the ArchiCode domain grants you access to a spectrum of viewpoints and customizable views, facilitating effective architectural communication and visualization.

- Technical Configuration: The ArchiCode domain encompasses essential technical configurations, enabling you to tailor the tool to your specific architectural requirements.

As we delve into each facet of the ArchiCode domain, you'll gain a comprehensive understanding of how each element contributes to your architectural prowess. Let's embark on a guided exploration through the diverse dimensions of the ArchiCode domain.

[[workspace]]
=== Workspace

In ArchiCode, a workspace is a fundamental construct that orchestrates the foundation of your architectural endeavors. It acts as a cohesive hub, streamlining the definition and configuration of your architecture work. Key aspects of the workspace include:

- Plain Text Format: A workspace is represented as a plain text file, allowing you to choose between YAML, TOML, or JSON formats. This flexibility ensures that the workspace aligns seamlessly with your preferred configuration style.

- The Name of the File: the workspace file is called `.archicode.yaml`, and `archicode` looks for it in the current directory. The name begins with a dot, so it is a hidden file: `ls -a` lists it, and a file dialog shows it only when you ask for hidden files (in the file manager of your system, or in the dialog of a browser). It is never read as a manifest, in any folder. `-w` (`--workspace`) names another file, in YAML, TOML or JSON, for the command line (`archicode -w ci/workspace.toml views generate`); the editor, and `archicode editor serve`, open a folder that has a `.archicode.yaml`. Before 0.18 the file was called `workspace.yaml`: <<plateaus-upgrade>> says what to do.

- Centralized Definition and Configuration: The workspace serves as a central repository for defining and configuring your architecture work. This consolidation streamlines your workflow and provides a clear reference point for all aspects of your project.

- Enrichment with Manifests: Workspaces can be enriched through the inclusion of manifests. A manifest, also presented as a plain text file in YAML, JSON or TOML format, further refines and specifies architectural details within the workspace. The command line reads the three; the editor reads YAML only (see <<editor-reads>>).

- Manifest for Enhanced Organization: The concept of manifests introduces a structural layer that enhances source code organization. By encapsulating architectural components, manifests contribute to a cleaner and more comprehensible separation of concerns.

- Collaboration Support: Embracing the manifest concept not only refines source code organization but also provides enhanced collaboration capabilities. Manifests facilitate clearer communication among team members by offering a well-defined structure and purpose.

- Optional Name: a workspace can be given a name with `settings.name`. The editor shows it in place of the name of the folder (see <<editor-workspace-name>>); the command line does not need it. A workspace without a name stays valid.

- Optional Plateaus: the states of the architecture are layers, written as manifests that say `plateau: <id>` in their header and, for each plateau, a manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau` (see <<plateaus>>). Views and stories belong to no plateau (see <<plateau-untied>>). A workspace that does not write any plateau keeps the one set of manifests it always had.

[[duplicate-references]]
A reference is declared once. The reference of an element is its `id` placed under its parent, whether that parent is the element that contains it in its file or the `parent` of the manifest's header, so `shop.web` is the same reference for an element nested in `shop`'s file and for a manifest with `parent: shop` and `id: web`. When two declarations share a reference, in two files, in a file and in a manifest placed under its parent, or twice in one file, the command line stops with an error that names the reference and the files, and generates nothing; two identical copies are as much an error as two different ones, because nothing says which file is meant. The same goes for two views, or two stories, that share an `id`. A reference may exist in both layers (an application and a node of the same name): they are not the same element.

----
the reference `shop` is declared twice: in `manifests/a.yaml` and `manifests/b.yaml`
----

The editor reports the same thing, in the same words (see <<editor-folders>>). With plateaus the rule is the one of <<plateau-header>>: a reference declared in a plateau and again in a plateau it stands on is the error, and the same reference in two plateaus that do not stand on each other is not.

[[manifest-formats]]
==== Manifest formats and kinds

A manifest is a file of YAML (`.yaml`, `.yml`), JSON (`.json`) or TOML (`.toml`) with a `header` and a `content`. The command line reads the three the same way, so that a manifest written in TOML gives the same graph as its YAML twin. This is the TOML twin of `manifests/user.yaml` above:

.The TOML twin of `manifests/user.yaml`: `manifests/user.toml`
[source,toml]
----
[header]
kind = "archicode.morin.io/person"
version = "1"

[content]
id = "User"

[[content.relationships]]
destination = "ArchiCode.CommandLineInterface"
qualifiers = ["Console"]
----

TOML has no null: a key with nothing to say is not written, and is absent. An element nested in another one is a `[[content.elements]]` table (a list of tables, as `[[content.relationships]]` above). The editor reads YAML only: it lists the `.toml` and `.json` files it finds as files it does not read (see <<editor-reads>>).

[[manifest-kinds]]
*The kinds.* The `kind` of the `header` is one of ten, each written `archicode.morin.io/<kind>`:

[cols="1,3"]
|===
| `person`, `solution`, `system`, `container`, `component` | the elements of the application layer (see <<application-layer>>)
| `environment`, `node` | the elements of the technology layer
| `view`, `story` | the views and the stories (see <<viewpoints>> and <<stories>>); they belong to no plateau
| `plateau` | a plateau (see <<plateau-manifest>>)
|===

*The group is gone.* Since 0.17 there is no notion of group: `container-group`, `solution-group`, `system-group` and `group` are no kinds, in the command line and in the editor. Write the children of a former group in the `elements` of its parent. A nested element of `kind: group` is refused with the sentence below, which lists the kinds the parent holds:

----
error: `manifests/a.yaml` is not valid: unknown kind `group`, a group is no kind any more: write its children in the `elements` of its parent (known: container) at `elements[0]`
----

and so is a manifest whose header is one of the three old kinds, with the same words before the list of the kinds there are:

----
error: `manifests/a.yaml` is not valid: unrecognized `header.kind` "archicode.morin.io/system-group", a group is no kind any more: write its children in the `elements` of its parent (known: archicode.morin.io/component, archicode.morin.io/container, archicode.morin.io/environment, archicode.morin.io/node, archicode.morin.io/person, archicode.morin.io/plateau, archicode.morin.io/solution, archicode.morin.io/story, archicode.morin.io/system, archicode.morin.io/view) (line 2, column 9)
----

[[nesting]]
*What a parent holds.* A solution holds systems; a system holds containers; a container holds components; an environment and a node hold nodes; a person and a component hold nothing. An element declared under a parent that cannot hold its kind (`header.parent`: a system under a container) is an error in both tools, with or without plateaus, that names the file and says what the parent holds:

----
error: `manifests/grp.yaml` is not valid: unknown kind `system` under `ArchiCode.CommandLineInterface` (known: component) at `header.kind`
----

An `id` is a name without a dot, since the dot separates the levels of a reference: `a.b` is refused by both tools.

----
error: `manifests/dot.yaml` is not valid: the id `a.b` contains a dot, which separates the levels of a reference, at `content.id`
----

*What a layer holds at its top.* In a workspace that has plateaus, a manifest with no `header.parent` must be of a kind that its layer holds at its root: a person, a solution or a system in the application layer, an environment in the technology layer. A container, a component or a node is held by an element, never by a layer, and a patch could not select one that stands alone, so both tools refuse it with one line that names the file and the kind:

----
error: `manifests/root-container.yaml` is not valid: unknown kind `container` with no `header.parent` (known: person, solution, system) at `header.kind`
----

A workspace with no plateau is not concerned: such a root is indexed and drawn as it always was. The editor does not offer these kinds at the top level, and does not move an element there.

[[manifests-folders]]
==== Manifests in folders

`settings.manifests.paths` lists the folders the manifests are read from, `manifests` by default, and a path read as it is written names one folder: the files directly in it are the manifests, and the files of its sub-folders are not. To keep the manifests in folders of your own (by domain, by team), end a path with `+/**+`: the folder and every folder below it are read.

.The content of `.archicode.yaml` of a workspace whose manifests are in folders
[source,yaml]
----
include::examples/domain_manifest_folders/.archicode.yaml[]
----

.The files of the example `domain_manifest_folders`
----
.archicode.yaml
manifests/
  application/
    shop.yaml
    shop/warehouse.yaml
  people/customer.yaml
  technology/
    reference.yaml
    reference/server.yaml
----

The command line and the editor read the same files, in the same order, by these rules:

. A path that does not end in `+/**+` is read exactly as it always was: the files directly in the folder. `+./**+` is the folder of `.archicode.yaml` and everything below it. The workspace file is never a manifest, at any depth and whatever the path: it is hidden, so a `+/**+` path would skip it anyway, and a folder read without `+/**+` skips it too. A folder that does not exist reads nothing, with `+/**+` as without.
. The files are read in the order of their path from the folder of `.archicode.yaml`, written with `/` and compared by Unicode code point, whatever the file system or the language of the system: `B.yaml` comes before `a.yaml`, and `a.yaml` before `a/b.yaml`. A file that two paths find (`manifests` and `+manifests/**+`) is read once. The order tells which file an error names first and the order of the files in the editor; the views do not depend on it.
. Below a path that ends in `+/**+`, these are not read: a file or a folder whose name begins with a dot (`.git`, `.cache`); a symbolic link to a folder (a link is never followed, so a link that points to its own folder is no loop; a link to a file is read like the file); and the folders of the generated views, which can hold files that are no manifests (the comparison of two plateaus writes a `gap.json`). These are the folder that `settings.views.path` names, as it is written in `.archicode.yaml`, and the folders beside it whose name begins with that name and a dash: with `views`, the folders `views`, `views-to-be`, `views-to-be-vs-baseline` and `views-shop`; and any folder that holds both files a comparison writes, `gap.json` and `gap.md`, whatever `--output` named it and whichever command wrote it (a folder with only a `gap.json` is read, since a manifest may be called so). A folder that is deeper than `views.path`, or whose name only looks like it (`viewsx`, `views_extra`), is read. A path that names such a folder itself (`+.manifests/**+`, `+views-notes/**+`) reads it: the rule applies to what is found below a path, not to the path. A path that is a link to a folder is read, as it is without `+/**+`.
. Every `.yaml`, `.yml`, `.json` and `.toml` file that is found is a manifest, as in a folder read without `+/**+`: a YAML file that is not a manifest (a CI file, a `values.yaml`) is refused with its name, so keep such files out of the folders you name, or in a hidden folder. A TOML manifest is a manifest like the others (see <<manifest-formats>>). The editor reads the YAML ones and says which files it does not read (see <<editor-reads>>).
. A reference declared in two of the files that are found is the error of <<duplicate-references>>, in the same line, with the two files in the order above.
. `+**+` means something only at the end of a path, after a `/`. `+manifests/**/shop+`, `+manifests/**x+`, `+a**/b+` and `+**+` are refused with one line that names the path and says what is read, in the command line and in the editor (where the Settings screen refuses them too). A path that contains `..` or is absolute is refused as before, with `+/**+` or without. A folder whose name contains `+**+` can no longer be named by a path.

Incorporating workspaces and manifests into your architectural workflow within ArchiCode empowers you with an efficient and organized foundation for your projects. Let's explore further as we navigate through the various dimensions of the ArchiCode domain.

[[plateaus]]
==== Plateaus

An architecture goes through states: how it is today, how it should be next year. (<<plateaus-overview>> tells the story in one place, with a tutorial.) A _plateau_ is one of these states, and it is a _layer_: it starts from the plateau it overrides, adds the elements of its own, and changes what it inherits with a list of patches. The first plateau, the architecture you have, is the _root_; it is called `baseline`, it exists without anything naming it, and it overrides nothing.

[NOTE]
====
*There is no `settings.plateaus` any more.* A plateau used to be a complete folder listed under `settings.plateaus` in `.archicode.yaml`, created by `archicode plateau add` and compared with `--baseline`. The key, the command and the option no longer exist. A `.archicode.yaml` that still has the key is refused by every command, and by the editor, with this one line, and nothing is read or written:

----
error: `settings.plateaus` no longer exists: a plateau is a layer now. Remove the key, write `plateau: <id>` in the header of the manifests of each plateau, and declare each plateau in a manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau` (`id`, `name`, `override`: the plateau it builds on, `baseline` when absent, and `patches` for what it changes)
----

`--baseline` is a usage error (`Unknown options: '--baseline', ...`, exit code 2) and `archicode plateau add` too (`Unmatched arguments from index 1: 'add', ...`); `--against` and `archicode plateau list` replace them (see <<plateau-choose>>). No command converts a workspace: <<plateaus-upgrade>> says how to do it by hand.
====

.A workspace with a root and one layer: `.archicode.yaml`
[source,yaml]
----
include::examples/domain_plateaus/.archicode.yaml[]
----

.The folders of that workspace
----
- project_dir/
    - plateaus/
        - baseline/ <1>
            - customer.yaml
            - inventory.yaml
            - plateau.baseline.yaml
            - shop.yaml
            - warehouse.yaml
        - to-be/ <2>
            - billing.yaml
            - cart.yaml
            - checkout.yaml
            - inventory.yaml
            - payments.yaml
            - plateau.to-be.yaml <3>
            - ratings.yaml
    - .archicode.yaml
----
<1> The manifests of the root: no `plateau:` in their header. `plateau.baseline.yaml` is optional and only gives the root a name and a horizon.
<2> The manifests of the layer `to-be`: each says `plateau: "to-be"` in its header. Only what the layer adds; what it inherits from `baseline` is not copied.
<3> The manifest of the plateau `to-be`: its name, its horizon, the plateau it overrides and its patches.

The folders are only a habit, and the example (`src/doc/examples/domain_plateaus` in the source repository) names them after the plateaus to be easy to read. What puts a manifest in a plateau is the `plateau` key of its header, not the folder it is in. (The editor warns when the two disagree, see <<editor-files>>; the command line says nothing.)

[[plateau-header]]
*The header.* A manifest of an element can say which plateau it belongs to in its header:

[source,yaml]
----
header:
  kind: "archicode.morin.io/container"
  version: "1"
  plateau: "to-be"
  parent: "shop"
content:
  id: "ratings"
  name: "Ratings"
----

Without `plateau`, the manifest belongs to `baseline`, the implicit root. A plateau exists as soon as a header names it (a plateau that only headers name has no name, no horizon and overrides `baseline`: it is told with a warning, see below) or a manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau` declares it. The elements of a plateau are added to the ones of the plateau it overrides, so a plateau sees everything its ancestors declare. A reference is declared once in a plateau and in all the plateaus it stands on (see <<duplicate-references>>): the same reference in a plateau and in its ancestor is an error, and in two plateaus that do not stand on each other (two alternatives) it is no duplicate.

[[plateau-untied]]
*Views and stories belong to no plateau.* Only the elements, the patches and the plateaus are layers. A view and a story exist whatever plateau is drawn: the same view or story is the one of every plateau, nothing patches them, and each is declared once in the whole workspace (the error of <<duplicate-references>>, between plateaus that do not stand on each other as well). A `plateau:` in the header of a view or of a story is accepted and means nothing: no tool reads it, no tool says anything about it (it is no warning and no error, whatever the text is), and it does not make a workspace of plateaus (a plateau that only a story names is no plateau). A header carries the key for every kind, and that is all there is to it.

What a view or a story names is looked at without a plateau, once the layers are read: every element it names must exist in at least one plateau, else it is an error, whichever plateau is asked for (a workspace without plateaus is not checked, and a view is looked at when it is drawn). A name that another plateau has and the plateau drawn lacks is no mistake of the view: that view cannot be drawn in that plateau (see <<plateau-views-lacking>>).

[[plateau-manifest]]
*The manifest of a plateau.* A manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau` qualifies one plateau; it is optional, and there is at most one for a plateau.

[cols="1,3"]
|===
| `id` | Required. What `plateau:` in a header, `override`, `--plateau` and `--against` name. Lowercase letters, digits, `_` and `-` (no dot, no capital): the identifier is also used in folder names. `baseline` is the id of the root.
| `name` | Optional. The name shown to people; the `id` when absent.
| `horizon` | Optional. A free-text label for when the plateau is meant to hold, such as `2027-Q1`. ArchiCode does not interpret it, except to order sibling plateaus in the lists of the editor and to warn when it is earlier than the horizon of the parent, when both read as a period (see below).
| `override` | Optional. The one plateau this one is a layer over: `baseline` when absent. The plateaus form a tree whose root is `baseline`; two plateaus that override the same one are alternatives. The root overrides nothing.
| `patches` | Optional. What the plateau changes in what it inherits, applied in order (see <<plateau-patches>>).
|===

The manifest of the example, with all its patches, is in step 3 of the tutorial (see <<plateaus-overview>>).

*What is checked, and the lines you get.* The tree is checked when the manifests are read, and each mistake is one `error:` line that names the plateau and the file, or one `warning:` (see <<errors-and-warnings>>). A plateau is read as its chain, from the root: for each plateau, the elements that say `plateau: <id>` are added to the graph, the patches of the plateau are applied, and the result is checked (a relationship, a `uses` entry, a hosted application and a parent must exist, and an element must be declared under a parent that can hold its kind, see <<nesting>>). The first plateau that is wrong ends the load, and the plateaus above it are not looked at. The views and the stories are checked once, after the layers (see <<plateau-untied>>), and so are the plateaus that the steps of a story name (see <<stories>>).

----
error: the plateau `to-be` (`plateaus/to-be/plateau.to-be.yaml`) overrides `asis`, which is not a plateau of this workspace (the plateaus are `baseline` and `to-be`)
error: the plateau `to-be` is declared twice: in `plateaus/to-be/again.yaml` and `plateaus/to-be/plateau.to-be.yaml`
error: the plateau id `To.Be` in `plateaus/to-be/plateau.to-be.yaml` is not valid: use lowercase letters, digits, `_` and `-`
error: the reference `customer` is declared in the plateau `to-be` (`plateaus/to-be/customer.yaml`) and again in its ancestor `baseline` (`plateaus/baseline/customer.yaml`)
error: in the plateau `to-be`, `shopp.cart` is declared under `shopp`, which does not exist
error: in the plateau `to-be`, `shop.web` has a relationship towards `shop.nowhere`, which does not exist, in `plateaus/to-be/shop.web.yaml` at `relationships[0].destination`
error: the view `shop_overview` (`manifests/view.shop_overview.yaml`) names `shopp`, which exists in no plateau
----

The first three are about the tree (an `override` that names nothing, a plateau declared by two manifests, an id that is not valid). Two more are told the same way: a plateau that overrides itself, directly or through others (`error: the plateau `to-be` overrides itself`), and a `baseline` manifest that overrides (`error: the plateau `baseline` (`plateaus/baseline/plateau.baseline.yaml`) is the root of the plateaus and cannot override `to-be``). The next three are about a plateau: a reference declared again in an ancestor, an element declared under a parent that does not exist, a relationship that leads nowhere. That last line, and its two cousins (an environment that `uses` an environment that is not there, a node that hosts an application that is not there), say the element that holds the reference, the file that declares it and the key of the reference in the element (`relationships[0].destination`, `uses[1]`, `applications[0]`: the place in its list); in a workspace without plateaus the same line is told without the plateau (``error: `shop.web` has a relationship towards `shop.nowhere`, which does not exist, in `manifests/shop.web.yaml` at `relationships[0].destination` ``), by `views generate` and by `query graph`, and the editor tells it in the same words. A mistake in a plateau names the plateau, so that the line says which layer to open. The last one is about no plateau: a view that names an element that exists in none.

*One cause, one line.* When one mistake has many consequences, the tools say the first one (by reference) and count the others. The elements a plateau declares under a parent that is not there are one sentence, whatever the parents are called (here a typo in the `parent` of three manifests): the first one told is a symptom of the missing layer, and a second wrong parent shows once the first is repaired. The elements that point at one reference that is not there are one sentence for each missing reference (here two relationships added towards `shop.nowhere`). The views and the stories that name something that is not there keep one line each, since they are not consequences of one another. When a manifest cannot be read, the command line stops at it, before any layer is built, with one line: the file and what is wrong. The editor draws what it can, and says that same line in place of the consequences the missing elements would leave behind.

----
error: in the plateau `to-be`, `shopp.billing` is declared under `shopp`, which does not exist (2 more elements of the plateau are declared under a parent that does not exist)
error: in the plateau `to-be`, `customer` has a relationship towards `shop.nowhere`, which does not exist, in `plateaus/to-be/customer.yaml` at `relationships[0].destination` (1 more element of the plateau points at it)
----

*Warnings.* These go on, and exit with the code 0:

----
warning: the plateau `to-b` exists only through the `plateau` key of the header of `plateaus/to-be/typo.yaml`: it has no manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau`, so it overrides `baseline` and has no name
warning: the plateaus `to-b` and `to-be` have ids one letter apart: check that one of them is not a typo
warning: the plateau `to-be` sets `description` of `shop.catalog` in patches 10 and 12: the last one wins
warning: the horizon `2026-Q1` of the plateau `to-be` is earlier than the horizon `2026-Q3` of the plateau it overrides, `baseline`
----

* A plateau that only headers name is told once, with the files. It is usually a typo in a `plateau:` key.
* Two plateaus whose ids are one letter apart (`to-b` and `to-be`) are told, so that a typo is seen, _only when one of the two has no manifest of kind plateau_. Two plateaus that have a manifest each (`p1` and `p2`, `to-be` and `to-bf`) are the author's choice and say nothing.
* Two `SET` of the same field of the same `target` in one plateau: the last one wins, and the warning gives both ranks. A `SET` with a `where` is a different one for each destination and label, and a patch with a `json-path` is not compared (what it selects is not known before the patches run).
* A `horizon` that is earlier than the one of the plateau it overrides. A horizon is a free label: two are compared only when both read as a period (`2026`, `2026-10`, `2026-10-18`, `2026-Q4`, `2026Q4`, `2026-H2`), and one is earlier when its whole period ends before the period above begins (`2026` under `2026-Q4` is not). Any other label is never compared.

[[plateau-patches]]
*The patches.* A layer changes what it inherits with the list `patches` of its manifest. A patch says what it does (`operator`), to what (a selector), and with what (`field`, `value`). The patches are applied in order, each one on the result of the ones before it, and a patch can reach an element that the layer brings with its own manifests (they are in the graph before the patches run). The kernel that applies them is the same one that draws the views and compares the plateaus, in the command line and in the editor.

[cols="1,3"]
|===
| `REMOVE` | The element goes, with what is in it and what points at it: the relationships towards it, its entry in a `uses` or an `applications` list. The node that hosted the element stays, even when it hosts nothing any more. Takes no `where` or `value`. What it took besides the element is told after the table of changes (see <<plateau-cascade>>). With a `field` it takes that field out of the selected elements and leaves the elements (below).
| `SET` | The field is replaced by `value`, a list or a map as a whole. With `where` (and `field: relationships`), `value` is a map and each of its keys replaces that key of the one relationship found.
| `ADD` | The list `field` gets `value` at its end, or each item if `value` is a list; a missing list is created. Nothing is deduplicated.
| `MERGE` | The keys of `value`, a map, are set in the map `field` (the tags, say); the other keys stay. One level only; a missing map is created.
|===

The selector is exactly one of these two:

[cols="1,3"]
|===
| `target` | The full reference of exactly one element (`shop.web`), in the application layer, or in the technology layer with `layer: technology`. A reference that does not exist is an error, and when the other layer has it, the error says to write `layer`.
| `json-path` | A JSONPath (RFC 9535) over the document `application.elements` and `technology.elements` of the plateau, which selects at least one node (an element or something in it). `expect: 3` asks for exactly three: a number of nodes that differs is an error, which turns a silent drift into a message.
|===

`field` is the field of the element that is changed (`name`, `description`, `qualifiers`, `tags`, `relationships`, `applications`, `uses`...); with a `json-path`, a patch with no `field` changes the selected nodes themselves. `where: {destination: "shop.catalog", label: "reads"}` finds one relationship by its destination and its label (a missing label is none); zero or several matches is an error. `id`, `parent` and `elements` cannot be changed: a patch never renames or moves an element, and an element that moved is told with `replaces` (see <<plateau-replaces>>). Views and stories belong to no plateau, so there is nothing to patch in them (see <<plateau-untied>>).

[source,yaml]
----
patches:
  - operator: SET            # one element, one field
    target: "shop.catalog"
    field: description
    value: "The books that are on sale."
  - operator: SET            # one relationship of an element, found by its destination and its label
    target: "shop.web"
    field: relationships
    where: {destination: "shop.catalog", label: "reads"}
    value: {label: "browses"}
  - operator: MERGE          # every container, and exactly nine of them
    json-path: "$.application..elements[?(@.kind == 'container')]"
    field: tags
    value: {zone: "north"}
    expect: 9
  - operator: REMOVE
    target: "shop.mailer"
  - operator: REMOVE         # a field, not the element: the inherited description is gone
    target: "shop.web"
    field: description
----

*Taking a field out.* A plateau says that something it inherits is gone with `REMOVE`, a `field` and the selector: the element stays, the field does not (a `SET` with no value cannot say it, since a missing value is no value). It works with a `target`, as above, and with a `json-path`, to take the field out of every element selected. Any field goes (`name`, `description`, `qualifiers`, `tags`, `relationships`, `applications`, `uses`, `replaces`) but `id`, `parent` and `elements`, which no patch changes, and `kind`, since an element always has one. A field the element does not have is already gone: that is no error, so a plateau still resolves when the plateau under it takes the same field out, and a bulk patch does not miss the elements that never had the field. A `where` or a `value` is refused. Nothing leaves the plateau, so the patch is not part of the cascade of the removals, and the comparison reads it as a change of the field of that element (`description: "The front of the shop." -> (none)`). A later `SET` of the same field, in this plateau or in one above, gives it back; a plateau that stands on this one sees it gone. The editor writes this patch when you clear a text that the plateau only inherits (see <<editor-writing>>).

A patch that cannot be applied stops the load, and the line names the plateau, the rank of the patch (from 1) and its selector, so that the patch can be found. These are the lines the commands print, on the example with one patch added each time:

----
error: plateau to-be: patch 10 (SET target shop.catalogue): selects nothing, there is no element shop.catalogue in the application layer
error: plateau to-be: patch 12 (REMOVE target shop.mailer): selects nothing, there is no element shop.mailer in the application layer
error: plateau to-be: patch 12 (REMOVE target shop.web): `kind` cannot be taken out: an element always has a kind
error: plateau to-be: patch 12 (REMOVE target shop.web): REMOVE takes no `value`: it takes a field out of what the selector selects
error: plateau to-be: patch 12 (SET json-path $.application..elements[?(@.kind == 'container')]): selects 9 nodes, 3 expected
error: plateau to-be: patch 12 (SET json-path $.application..elements[?(@.kind == 'component' && @.name == 'Nope')]): selects nothing, at least one node is expected
error: plateau to-be: patch 12 (SET target shop.web): shop.web has no relationship to shop.catalog with the label `nope`
error: plateau to-be: patch 12 (SET target shop.web): needs a `value`
error: plateau to-be: patch 12 (SET target shop.web): `id` cannot be changed: a patch never changes an id or a parent
error: `plateaus/to-be/plateau.to-be.yaml` is not valid: unknown key `values` (known: expect, field, json-path, layer, operator, target, value, where) at `patches[11].values`
error: `plateaus/to-be/plateau.to-be.yaml` is not valid: `patches[11]` has a `target` and a `json-path`: write one of them
----

The second one is the _stale patch_: an earlier patch (rank 1 here) had already removed `shop.mailer`, and a plateau that stands on this one cannot remove it again. Patches that are written for a plateau and not for the one it now overrides go stale when the plateaus above change; the error is how you learn it. The editor says the same sentences (see <<editor-band>>).

[[plateau-cascade]]
*What a `REMOVE` takes.* A `REMOVE` takes more than the element it selects, and that is not obvious from the patch. `archicode diff` and `archicode views generate` list it after the table of changes, in its own section, _cascade of the removals_ (the key `cascade` of `gap.json`, and a section of `gap.md`): the elements that went with the selected one (`removed`), and the entries taken out of elements that stay (`dropped`: a relationship, a `uses` or an `applications` entry). It is shown for the removals that are part of the difference between the two plateaus compared, and it is not part of the counts. In the example, the removal of the mailer takes the link from the web site to it, and the removal of the inventory takes its component and the link from `picking`:

[source,text]
----
cascade of the removals (what a REMOVE took besides the elements it selects, in the plateaus the target adds to the one it is compared with)
PLATEAU  PATCH                           EFFECT   LAYER        ELEMENT                DETAIL
to-be    1 REMOVE target shop.mailer     dropped  application  shop.web               relationships: shop.mailer (sends mails with)
to-be    6 REMOVE target shop.inventory  removed  application  shop.inventory.levels
to-be    6 REMOVE target shop.inventory  dropped  application  warehouse.picking      relationships: shop.inventory (counts with)
----

The editor shows the same rows in the _Cascade of the removals_ section of its Changes panel (see <<editor-band>>). A section that has nothing to say is not written, and the output of a plateau that removes nothing is what it always was.

[[plateau-choose]]
*Choosing a plateau on the command line.* The commands that load a workspace (`views generate`, `diff`, `query views`, `query graph`) take `--plateau <id>`. Without it, the plateau is the one `settings.current` names; else the last one when the plateaus form one line (each overrides the one before it); else the command stops and asks for a choice, since a tree that forks has no last plateau:

----
error: the plateaus of this workspace are not one line (`baseline` is overridden by `saas`, `to-be`): name the plateau to read with `--plateau`, or with `settings.current`
----

`--plateau` wins over `settings.current`. The editor does not choose as the command line does when no name is given and the plateaus form one line: with no address, no plateau remembered by the browser and no `settings.current`, it draws the root, `baseline`, where the command line draws the last plateau. Write `settings.current` (the editor has a field for it, see <<editor-current-plateau>>) to make the two agree. An unknown id is an error that lists the plateaus (`error: unknown plateau `nope`: the plateaus of this workspace are `baseline` and `to-be``). `archicode plateau list` lists the tree, one line per plateau, `baseline` first, then what overrides it, each plateau followed by what overrides it; plateaus that override the same one come in the order of their ids. `-` is for what is absent, and `SOURCE` says where the plateau comes from: its manifest, `headers only` (a plateau that only headers name, with its files), or `implicit` (the root that nothing names).

[source,shell]
----
archicode plateau list
----

----
PLATEAU   PARENT    SOURCE                                            NAME   HORIZON
baseline  -         manifest plateaus/baseline/plateau.baseline.yaml  As is  2026-Q3
to-be     baseline  manifest plateaus/to-be/plateau.to-be.yaml        To be  2027-Q1
----

`--against <id>` names the plateau to compare with. Without it, a plateau is compared with the plateau it overrides; the root overrides nothing, so `baseline` is compared with nothing unless `--against` names a plateau (`diff --plateau baseline` alone stops with `error: the plateau `baseline` is the root of the plateaus and nothing is before it: there is nothing to compare it with. Name a plateau with `--against``). `--against` takes any plateau of the tree, a sibling or the future included, and refuses the plateau itself (`error: `--against to-be` is the plateau that is compared: choose another plateau to compare it with`). The views of a plateau are written in `views-<plateau>`, and the views of its comparison in `views-<plateau>-vs-<against>` (and one folder per facet, as the `directory-name-template` of the facets says); a workspace with no plateau writes `views`, as it always did. With `--output out`, the folders are `out-<plateau>` and `out-<plateau>-vs-<against>`. See <<generate-views>> and <<diff>>.

`settings.manifests.paths` lists the folders of the manifests, for a workspace with plateaus as for one without. A layered workspace usually ends a path with `+/**+` (see <<manifests-folders>>), so that the folders of the plateaus are read.

IMPORTANT: *Breaking change* (see <<plateaus-upgrade>>). A path of `settings.manifests.paths` may no longer leave the folder of the workspace (`../shared`) nor be absolute (`/srv/manifests`, `C:\data`), so that one folder holds a whole workspace, and so that the editor, which only reaches the folder it opened, reads what the command line reads. A workspace that used such a path stops with an error that says which path and what to change: move the folder below `.archicode.yaml`, or move `.archicode.yaml` up to a folder that holds both, and change the path to match. A path that stays below the folder (`manifests`, `node_modules/<module>/manifests`) is not affected, and neither is a workspace without plateaus: it is read, and generates its views, exactly as before.

[[plateau-comparison]]
==== Comparing two plateaus

What a plateau _changes_ is the difference between it and another plateau: ArchiCode compares the two and tells what is new, what changed and what was removed, elements and relationships. The plateau it is compared with is, in this order, the one `--against` names, else the plateau it overrides. The root, `baseline`, overrides nothing, so it is compared with nothing unless `--against` names a plateau. Any plateau of the tree can be named, a sibling or a plateau of the future too (`archicode diff --plateau inhouse --against saas` compares two alternatives): the comparison is made on the two plateaus as they are resolved, whatever their relation.

Two commands show the comparison: `archicode diff` writes the table of changes (see <<diff>>), and `archicode views generate` draws the views of the comparison (see <<generate-views>>).

*What counts as a change.* An element is the same element in both plateaus when it has the same reference (a reference includes the path of the element, so an element that moves under another parent is a removed one and a new one, unless the new one says that it replaces the old one, see <<plateau-replaces>>). It is _new_ when its reference is only in the plateau, _removed_ when it is only in the other plateau, and _changed_ when something differs in the element itself or in the relationships it declares: its kind, its name, its description, its qualifiers, its tags (the `rendering-*` ones do not count), its deployment (`applications` and `uses`), or a relationship that was added, removed or changed. A child is judged on its own: a system whose only change is a new container is not _changed_, it _contains changes_. The table lists what changed in each element (`natures`) and tells a change of wording apart: an element whose description is the only thing that differs is _wording only_. A relationship is identified by its source, its destination and its label; it is _new_ or _removed_ when it is only on one side, and _changed_ when its qualifiers, its tags or, when it is the only one between the two elements, its label differ. The order of a list and the order of the keys of a file are no change.

*How the views say it.* The views of a comparison draw the union of the two plateaus (what was removed is drawn too), and every box and every link says its state with the same marks, never by colour alone (the last three are for an element that says what it replaces, see <<plateau-replaces>>, and are only in the legend of a view that has one):

[cols="1,1,2,2"]
|===
| State | Mark | Box | Link

| new | `+` | thick solid outline | thick solid line
| changed | `~` | dotted outline | dotted line
| removed | `−` | long dashes with narrow gaps, grey outline, grey and struck title | dashed grey line
| contains changes | `±` | thin dashed outline | (none)
| moved | `→` | long dashes with wide gaps, blue; the title ends with `(from <the reference it replaces>)` | (none)
| split | `⋔` | medium dashes, purple; the title ends with `(from <the reference it replaces>)` | (none)
| merged | `⊕` | short dashes far apart, light blue; the title ends with `(from <the references it replaces>)` | (none)
|===

The mark is written in front of the title of the box and of the label of the link. A box that did not change is drawn as it always was, and so is a link. `contains changes` is for a box that did not change by itself and holds something that did (a system whose container is new, for instance): the reader can see where to look inside. A link between two boxes that are drawn as one group (a view draws the relationships of the elements below it as one link) is new when all of them are, removed when all of them are, changed when they are mixed, and unchanged when none changed. A view that says at least one change has a legend that explains the marks.

The states are stereotypes of the boxes (`new`, `changed`, `removed`, `contains-changes`, `moved`, `split` and `merged`), so a `styles.by-tags` entry of the same name gives them your own look, as it does for every stereotype (see <<stereotypes-and-styles>>). ArchiCode brings a default style for a state only when the workspace has none, and the style you write wins property by property: write `new` with a `background-color` and the box keeps the thick outline of the default.

The default look of a state belongs to the boxes that _have_ the state, computed: it is never given to a box because of a word it carries. If you wrote `rendering-stereotypes: "new"` (or `changed`, `removed`, `contains-changes`, `moved`, `split`, `merged`) on an element that does not have that state, the box is drawn with your own `styles` entry named `new` and nothing else, no thick outline, no mark, no struck title, in a view that has something new as in one that has not, and the command says so (see <<plateau-state-tags>>).

[source,yaml]
----
styles:
  by-tags:
    new:
      background-color: "#DAF2E6"   # the outline stays the default one
    removed:
      line-color: red
----

A facet can read what changed: in a comparison, every element and every relationship has a `change` (`none`, `new`, `changed` or `removed`, and, for an element that says what it replaces, `moved`, `split` or `merged`) that a JSONPath selects like any other field, such as `$..relationships[?(@.change == 'removed')]`. The other facets work as they always did.

[[application-layer]]
=== Application Layer

// - In ArchiCode, the Application Layer matches the Application Layer of ArchiMate.
// - In ArchiMate the application layer is defined as "The Application Layer depicts application services that support the business, and the applications that realize them."
// - ArchiCode focuses exclusively on the structure of the Application Layer, i.e. the Structure aspect of ArchiMate.
// - In ArchiCode, the Application Layer matches the C4 Model with an additional element, the Solution, on top of the System element.

In ArchiCode, the Application Layer embodies a pivotal aspect of your architectural design, harmonizing with both the ArchiMate Standard and the C4 Model. Key insights into the Application Layer include:

- Correlation with ArchiMate: Drawing inspiration from the ArchiMate Standard, ArchiCode's Application Layer mirrors the Application Layer within the ArchiMate framework.

- Structural Focus: ArchiCode delves specifically into the structural realm of the Application Layer. It emphasizes the "Structure" aspect of ArchiMate, enabling architects to design and define the composition of application components.

- Alignment with C4 Model: Building upon the alignment with ArchiMate, ArchiCode's Application Layer extends its compatibility to the C4 Model. This alignment, inclusive of a unique "Solution" element, complements the foundational "System" element, enriching the architectural vocabulary.

==== Composition

// - The Application Layer is composed of Solutions and Systems.
// - A Solution is composed of Systems
// - A System is composed of Containers
// - A Container is composed of Components

- Solutions and Systems: At the apex of the Application Layer, you encounter "Solutions" and "Systems." A Solution encapsulates one or more Systems, establishing a higher-level conceptual grouping.

- Systems and Containers: Delving deeper, a "System" encompasses "Containers," delineating the delineating technological boundaries of your architecture. These Containers form the backbone of your application's operational flow.

- Containers and Components: Within each Container resides a multitude of "Components," embodying the granular building blocks of your architecture. These Components epitomize the specific functionalities that drive your application's behavior.

.Application Layer Composition
[plantuml, application_layer_composition]
----
@startuml
hide empty members
rectangle Workspace
rectangle ApplicationLayer as "Application Layer"
rectangle Solution
rectangle System
rectangle Container
rectangle Component
Workspace *--> "1" ApplicationLayer
ApplicationLayer *--> "*" Solution
ApplicationLayer *--> "*" System
Solution *--> "*" System
System *--> "*" Container
Container *--> "*" Component
@enduml
----

This hierarchical structure empowers you to systematically model and articulate the intricate relationships and dependencies between Solutions, Systems, Containers, and Components within your architectural design.

==== Solution

// - A Solution makes senses when the architecture work covers many systems.

In the context of the ArchiCode domain, the concept of "Solutions" takes on a pivotal role, particularly when orchestrating architectural endeavors that span multiple systems. Here's a closer look at the significance of Solutions:

- Multi-System Scope: Solutions come to the forefront when your architecture work extends its reach across multiple systems. They provide a coherent and organized approach to manage and address the complexities that arise from a diverse ecosystem of interconnected systems.

- Holistic View: By grouping related Systems under a common Solution, you achieve a holistic and top-level perspective. This organizational structure allows you to grasp the overarching design intent and align it seamlessly with the intricacies of your multi-system architecture.

- Modularity and Scalability: Solutions inherently promote modularity and scalability. They enable you to encapsulate specific sets of Systems, facilitating targeted enhancements, updates, and expansions while maintaining a clear demarcation of responsibilities.

- Efficient Management: Solutions offer an efficient management framework, streamlining the handling of interdependencies, resource allocation, and cross-cutting concerns across Systems. This, in turn, fosters improved coordination and resource utilization.

- Enhanced Communication: Solutions enhance communication by providing a concise reference point for discussing architectural decisions and rationale. This clarity aids in effective collaboration among stakeholders, ensuring everyone shares a common understanding of the architectural landscape.

Incorporating Solutions into your architectural repertoire within ArchiCode empowers you to navigate the complexities of multi-system architectures with finesse, coherence, and strategic foresight.

=== Technology Layer

// - In ArchiCode, the Technology Layer matches the Technology Layer of ArchiMate.
// - In ArchiMate the application layer is defined as "The Technology Layer comprises both information and operational technology. You can model, for example, processing, storage, and communication technology in support of the application world and Business Layers, and model operational or physical technology with facilities, physical equipment, materials, and distribution networks."
// - ArchiCode focuses exclusively on the structure of the Technology Layer, i.e. the Structure aspect of ArchiMate.
// - In ArchiCode, the Technology Layer matches the Deployment representations of the C4 Model, but there is only two elements, the Environment and the Node.

In the ArchiCode domain, the "Technology Layer" emerges as a pivotal facet that resonates with both the ArchiMate Standard and the Deployment representations within the C4 Model.Here's a comprehensive exploration of this layer:

- Correlation with ArchiMate: Mirroring the ArchiMate framework, ArchiCode's Technology Layer corresponds to the "Technology Layer" within ArchiMate.This alignment facilitates a seamless integration of architectural principles across domains.

- Structural Focus: ArchiCode, delves into the structural aspect of the Technology Layer.It exclusively emphasizes the "Structure" facet of ArchiMate, enabling architects to meticulously model and define the composition of technological elements that underpin your architecture.

- Equivalence with C4 Model: The Technology Layer in ArchiCode aligns with the Deployment representations found within the C4 Model. However, ArchiCode simplifies this representation by reducing to two foundational elements: "Environment" and "Node." These elements encapsulate the essence of the Technology Layer, providing a streamlined yet comprehensive view of technological aspects.

==== Composition

// - The Technology Layer is composed of Environments.
// - A Environment is composed of Nodes
// - A Node is composed of

Within the ArchiCode domain, the Technology Layer unfolds as a structured hierarchy, comprising essential elements that contribute to the technological fabric of your architecture. Let's delve into this composition:

- Environments and Nodes: At the forefront of the Technology Layer, you encounter "Environments" and "Nodes." An Environment encapsulates one or more Nodes, forming a high-level organizational unit that represents distinct technological contexts.
- Nodes and Sub-Nodes: Delving deeper, a "Node" encompasses both "Nodes" and "Sub-Nodes." This hierarchical arrangement enables the precise delineation of technological elements, allowing for finer-grained representation and definition.

.Technology Layer Composition
[plantuml, technology_layer_composition]
----
@startuml
hide empty members
rectangle Workspace
rectangle TechnologyLayer as "Technology Layer"
rectangle Environment
rectangle Node
Workspace *--> "1" TechnologyLayer
TechnologyLayer *--> "*" Environment
Environment *--> "*" Node
Node *--> "*" Node
@enduml
----

Within this framework, you gain the ability to systematically depict and elucidate the complex interactions, dependencies, and relationships that exist among Environments, Nodes, and Sub-Nodes within your technological architecture.

==== Environment

// - A Environment in ArchiCode is equivalent to an Environment in the Deployment representation of the C4 Model.

In the ArchiCode domain, the concept of an "Environment" resonates in parallel with its counterpart within the Deployment representation of the C4 Model.

.The workspace defining the Reference environment
[source,yaml]
----
include::examples/domain_technology/.archicode.yaml[]
----

==== Node

// - A Node in ArchiCode is equivalent to a Node in the Deployment representation of the C4 Model.

Within the ArchiCode domain, the concept of a "Node" aligns harmoniously with its counterpart within the Deployment representation of the C4 Model.

.The manifest of the Laptop node embedding the OperatingSystem and the TerminalSession nodes
[source,yaml]
----
include::examples/domain_technology/manifests/laptop.yaml[]
----

[[using-environments]]
==== Using other environments

An application rarely lives alone in its environment: the development environment of a platform talks to an identity provider, to a mail relay, to the integration environment of a partner. Those are environments too, each with the nodes that host the applications modeled for them. An environment says which ones it depends on with `uses`.

.The manifest of an environment that uses another one, and a node of a third one
[source,yaml]
----
header:
  kind: archicode.morin.io/environment
  version: "1"
content:
  id: dev
  name: Development
  uses:
    - integration
    - idp.production.openid
  elements:
    - kind: node
      id: cluster
      applications:
        - shop.api
----

`uses` is not a relationship and it draws nothing by itself. It tells where to look for what the applications of the environment depend on:

- When an application deployed in the environment, or anything inside it, has a relationship towards another application, ArchiCode looks where that other application is deployed. A node that lists the container `shop.app` in `applications` hosts the relationships of its components too: a relationship of `shop.app.payments` towards `billing` is a dependency of the node, where it used to give none. The destination counts the same way: a relationship of `backoffice` towards `shop.app.orders`, a component of the deployed container `shop.app`, is a dependency towards the node that hosts `shop.app` (it used to give none, because `shop.app.orders` is not itself listed in `applications`). A relationship that stays inside the deployed application gives nothing, and the destination must be a deployed application or inside one. If the environment hosts it, nothing leaves the environment.
- Otherwise, it is looked for in the environments, or the nodes, listed in `uses`, and nowhere else. An application deployed in several environments is reached in the ones the environment uses. That is how `dev` reaches the `integration` instance of an application, and not the `production` one.
- The dependency is then drawn, in the deep view of the technology layer, between the two deployed applications, with the label, the qualifiers and the tags of the relationship of the application. It folds with the nodes: when they are collapsed, the dependencies between them are grouped in one link.
- The overview and the detailed view of an environment, or of a node, show the same dependencies grouped on the nodes that host the applications: an environment is linked to the environments it uses.
- The view of an environment shows what it depends on, and the view of a used environment shows who depends on it.

An entry of `uses` is the reference of an environment, to use all of it, or of one of its nodes, to use a part of it. It has to exist, or the views are not generated.

As the dependencies are derived from the relationships of the applications, a facet that removes some of them removes the dependencies that rest on them. Through a facet, the deep view of an environment also draws the relationships between the applications deployed in it, with their label and qualifiers: a facet that keeps one flow (an authentication, the audit events) shows that flow as it is deployed, on the nodes that host the applications. Without a facet these relationships are not drawn, there would be too many. The overview and the detailed view lift them to the nodes the view is made of. These links between the nodes of one environment read, as the others do, the relationships of everything inside the deployed application, at both ends: a relationship of a component of a deployed container towards another deployed application is a link from the node of the container to the node of that application, and a relationship that stays inside one container draws nothing. They are drawn in the faceted views of the technology layer only, as before. A facet that sets `hide-unlinked: true` also leaves out, in these views, the elements none of its links goes through, the element the view is about staying; in the editor it is a check box of the facet, in the Settings. The entries of `uses` can also be selected by a facet, as any other part of the workspace.

=== Relationships

// - A Relationship links two Elements of the same Layer.
// - An Element of the Technology Layer can reference an Element of the Application Layer.
// - ArchiCode promotes natural in-depth relationships between Elements for accuracy, but smartly manage synthetic relationships at upper level to maintain clarity.

In the ArchiCode domain, relationships serve as essential conduits that navigate the intricate architectural landscape. Let's delve into the nuances of relationships and their pivotal role within this framework:

- Interconnection of Elements: A "Relationship" within ArchiCode emerges as a bridge that seamlessly links two "Elements" existing within a singular architectural "Layer." This interconnectedness facilitates a coherent representation of how architectural components interact and collaborate.

- Layer Interactions: Notably, an "Element" within the Technology Layer possesses the capability to reference an "Element" residing within the Application Layer. This cross-layer referencing enriches your architectural design by articulating dependencies that transcend traditional boundaries.

- Striking the Balance: ArchiCode takes a deliberate approach in fostering relationships. It promotes the establishment of intricate and in-depth relationships between Elements, enhancing accuracy and authenticity. Simultaneously, at higher levels, ArchiCode employs astute management of synthetic relationships to maintain clarity and comprehensibility.

.Relationship Composition
[plantuml, relationship_composition]
----
@startuml
hide empty members
rectangle Element
rectangle Relationship
Element *--> "*" Relationship
Relationship o--> "1" Element
@enduml
----

As you traverse the ArchiCode landscape, you'll discover that relationships are more than mere connections; they embody the intricate tapestry of your architecture. These relationships intertwine Elements, forging a harmonious narrative that elucidates dependencies, interactions, and the synergy that fuels your architectural vision. In this realm, relationships are not just links; they're the vital conduits that breathe life into your architectural masterpiece.

[[viewpoints]]
=== Viewpoints and Views

// - The purpose of ArchiCode is to finally generate views of the Application and Technology layers.
// - In ArchiCode, a View is primarily the aggregation of a Viewpoint and a Layer.
// - In ArchiCode, a Viewpoint is an opinionated set of pre-defined rules extracting and transforming data related to the Layer of View.

ArchiCode serves a fundamental purpose: the generation of comprehensive views that illuminate both the Application and Technology layers of your architectural design. This purpose aligns seamlessly with architectural principles and methodologies. Let's delve into the mechanics that facilitate this purpose within ArchiCode:

- View Composition in ArchiCode: Within the ArchiCode framework, a "View" emerges as a coherent amalgamation of two crucial components: a "Viewpoint" and a "Layer." This composition ensures that each view encapsulates a well-defined perspective on your architecture.

- Viewpoint Definition in ArchiCode: In the context of ArchiCode, a "Viewpoint" embodies a set of opinionated and pre-defined rules. These rules play a pivotal role in the extraction and transformation of data. This structuring ensures that views effectively communicate specific architectural aspects.

==== Composition

// - A workspace contains many Views.
// - A View is associated to one Viewpoint.
// - A View is associated to one Layer.

Within the ArchiCode workspace, the concept of "Views" plays a pivotal role in portraying architectural insights. Let's explore the characteristics of Views and their associations within the workspace:

- View and Viewpoint Association: In the realm of ArchiCode, every View is intrinsically linked to a designated "Viewpoint." This association ensures that each View is curated to encapsulate the viewpoint's specific perspective on the architectural content.

- View and Layer Association: Additionally, every View within ArchiCode is associated with a distinct "Layer." This connection anchors the View's focus to a specific architectural layer, allowing for a clear and structured portrayal of the intended architectural context.

.Viewpoints and Views Composition
[plantuml, viewpoint_views_composition]
----
@startuml
hide empty members
rectangle Workspace
rectangle View
rectangle Viewpoint
rectangle Layer
Workspace *--> "*" View
View o--> "1" Viewpoint
View o--> "1" Layer
@enduml
----

By incorporating Views within the ArchiCode workspace and meticulously associating them with relevant Viewpoints and Layers, you harness a powerful tool for effectively communicating architectural insights. As we continue our exploration of ArchiCode, you'll delve further into the mechanics of Views and their pivotal role in conveying the depth and breadth of your architectural design.

==== Views

// - ArchiCode emphases the dynamic generation of views.
// - ArchiCode leverage on pluggable Viewpoint Service to dynamically generate views according to the elements of both layers Application and Technology.
// - ArchiCode allows also ad-hoc definition of views.

ArchiCode places a strong emphasis on the dynamic generation of views, fostering a dynamic and adaptable approach to architectural visualization. Here's how ArchiCode leverages this dynamic view generation:

- Pluggable Viewpoint Service: ArchiCode harnesses a pluggable Viewpoint Service architecture, enabling the dynamic generation of views that seamlessly align with the elements of both the Application and Technology layers. This extensible framework empowers architects to tailor viewpoints according to their architectural needs.

- Diverse Architectural Elements: With ArchiCode, the dynamic view generation process taps into the rich spectrum of architectural elements within the Application and Technology layers. This ensures that views are not only comprehensive but also finely tuned to convey the intricacies of your design.

- Ad-Hoc View Definition: ArchiCode takes flexibility a step further by allowing architects to define ad-hoc views. This empowers you to create specialized viewpoints that cater to specific architectural inquiries or scenarios, providing an extra layer of customization.

.The workspace defined a View representing an __overview__ of the TerminalSession node
[source,yaml]
----
include::examples/domain_viewpoint_views/.archicode.yaml[]
----

A view that has an `id` is a named view. Without `--view`, `archicode views generate` draws the views it finds from the elements of the model (a named view is drawn when it is asked for); `archicode views generate --view terminal-session` (or `-i`) draws the views of that `id` alone, no other view with them, into `views/plantuml/technology/terminal-session.puml`. A view can be a manifest of its own, in the folders of the manifests (see <<stories>>); it belongs to no plateau, see <<plateau-untied>>.

[[map-viewpoint]]
*The map.* A view of the `map` viewpoint draws a whole layer instead of the surroundings of one element: `layer: application` (the default) or `layer: technology`, folded, so that only the top of the layer shows and a link joins the boxes its two ends land on. Three lists of references, in `properties`, shape it: `pinned` keeps only what touches these elements, `hidden` leaves these elements, what is inside them and their relationships out, and `opened` opens these boxes, so that their children show. A map of the technology layer has two more properties, booleans, that are true when they are absent: `applications: false` leaves out the applications deployed on the nodes, and `relationships: false` leaves out the links (see below). The `layer` of a view (and of a step that carries its own view) is read in any case by both tools: `layer: Technology` and `layer: TECHNOLOGY` are the technology layer, and anything else is the application layer; `application` and `technology` in lower case remain the form to write. The editor draws the same view on its page Applications (the application layer) and its page Environments (the technology layer, see <<editor-map>>), and both tools draw the same boxes (the example below has the boxes of each view pinned by a test). The command line draws a map when it is asked for it by its `id`, as for any named view:

.The content of `.archicode.yaml` of the example `domain_technology_map`: three maps of a technology layer
[source,yaml]
----
include::examples/domain_technology_map/.archicode.yaml[]
----

[source,shell]
----
archicode views generate --view datacentre-map-open
----

writes `views/plantuml/technology/datacentre-map-open.puml`. The command line does not read what the editor keeps in a map besides the three lists, the two booleans and `links` (`facet`, `hide-unlinked` and the texts of the diagram; see <<map-links>> for `links`). A list that names an element that does not exist is one `error:` line that names the view (`error: the view datacentre-map-database: dangling reference: datacentre.rack.dbx`); in the views of a facet, where the facet may have removed the element, the entry is dropped. A comparison of two plateaus gives a map the states of its boxes, as it does for the other viewpoints.

[[map-links]]
*Choosing the relationships a map draws.* A map of the application layer can name the relationships it draws, with a fourth property, `links`. Each entry is a mapping with a `source`, a `destination` and, optionally, a `label`:

[source,yaml]
----
properties:
  links:
    - source: customer
      destination: shop.web
      label: uses
    - source: shop.web
      destination: shop.catalog
----

The map then draws these relationships, the boxes of their two ends and what encloses them, and nothing else of the layer. `pinned`, `hidden` and `opened` keep their meaning: `hidden` still leaves out a relationship of or towards a hidden element, `pinned` still keeps the relationships that touch a pinned element (the pinned elements are boxes too), and an end inside a closed box lands on that box. A `links` that is absent, empty or has nothing after it draws the whole map, as before.

An entry names a relationship by what the `source` element declares: the `destination` and the `label` must be equal to the ones declared (the references are exact, an entry does not select what is inside the `source`). An entry with no `label` names the relationships that have no label; it is not a wildcard. Relationships that have the same `source`, `destination` and `label` and differ only by their qualifiers or tags cannot be told apart by an entry, so one entry draws them all. An entry of another shape (not a mapping, no text `source` or `destination`, a `label` that is not text, another key) is one `error:` line that says where it is, and so is an entry that names no relationship, with nothing written for the view:

----
error: the view orders_map: `links[1]` names a relationship from `shop.web` to `shop.catalog` with the label `reads`, which does not exist
----

(`with no label` when the entry has none; an unknown `source` or `destination` is `dangling reference` as for `pinned`.) The map of the technology layer reads no `links` and refuses them. The command line and the editor read `links`: the editor lists the relationships of the layer under the tree of its page Applications and draws the ticked ones (see <<editor-map-links>>).

In a workspace of plateaus, the entry is left out when the plateau drawn does not have the relationship, with `warning: the view `m` is drawn without the link `a -> b`, which the plateau `p` does not have`, and the view is skipped, with `warning: skip `m`: none of its links is in the plateau `p``, when none of its entries is there (see <<plateau-views-lacking>>). A typo in an entry is therefore only a warning in a workspace of plateaus, where it is an error in a workspace without. A relationship that only the plateau compared with has can be named in a comparison: it is drawn as removed.

[[map-highlight]]
*Highlighting a relationship.* A map of the application layer can also point at relationships among the ones it draws, with a fifth property, `highlight`. It is a list of the same entries as `links` (a `source`, a `destination` and, optionally, a `label`), matched the same way (exact references, a missing `label` names only the relationships that have none):

[source,yaml]
----
properties:
  links:
    - source: customer
      destination: shop.web
    - source: shop.web
      destination: shop.catalog
      label: reads
  highlight:
    - source: shop.web
      destination: shop.catalog
      label: reads
----

The highlight changes how links are drawn and nothing else: it adds no box and no link, and `highlight` without `links` points at links of the whole map.
