In PlantUML the highlighted link is the heaviest line of the view, in a vivid orange, with a black triangle before its label and the label in bold, so that the colour is never the only sign. As soon as one link is highlighted, the other links are drawn calm: thin, light grey, with a grey label. A link that carries a state of a comparison (new, changed, removed) keeps its colour and its dash and only takes the weight and the triangle when it is highlighted, and it is not made calm when it is not. A link that stands for several relationships (a grouped link, or one that lands on a closed box) is highlighted when one of them is. A view with no highlighted link is drawn as it always was, whatever the shape of its `highlight`: an entry that names a relationship the map leaves out (hidden, inside one closed box, or left out by `only changes`) marks nothing and is not an error.

The shape of an entry and the errors are the ones of `links`, with `highlight[n]` in the sentence, and a missing entry is `error: the view orders_map: `highlight[1]` names a relationship from `shop.web` to `shop.catalog` with the label `reads`, which does not exist`. The map of the technology layer refuses `highlight`, and the other viewpoints (`overview`, `detailed`, `deep`) ignore it. In a workspace of plateaus, an entry the plateau drawn does not have is left out with `warning: the view `m` is drawn without the highlight `a -> b`, which the plateau `p` does not have`; unlike `links`, the view is never skipped for it, since a highlight is not what the view is about. In a comparison an entry may name a relationship that only the plateau compared with had. The command line reads `highlight` (in `views generate` and `stories generate`), and so does the editor: it draws the same heavy link and calm links on its Applications page, in a saved view and in a story step, and plays them in the story player (see <<editor-map-links>>, <<editor-stories>> and <<editor-story-player>>). It writes `highlight` sorted, without a duplicate and after `links`, and follows the references in it when an element is renamed or moved.

[[map-technology]]
*What a map of the technology layer draws.* Besides the nodes and the environments, it draws the applications deployed on the nodes and the relationships:

* *The applications* are the `applications` that the nodes list, one box each, inside its node. Only an open node shows them (a node is open when it is in `opened`, or when it encloses a pinned element), and a node that is hidden shows none; when the map is pinned, they are drawn only inside what it is pinned on. A node that hosts applications can be opened, so it is no longer drawn as a closed box with nothing in it; an application box has nothing to open. A `pinned`, `hidden` or `opened` entry names a technology element: an application is not pinned, hidden or opened on its own (hide its node, or leave the applications out with `applications: false`).
* *The relationships* are the ones the elements of the layer declare, and the dependencies between environments that the deployed applications give: an application of one environment that depends on an application the environment does not host, found in an environment or a node that the first `uses`, is a link between the two application boxes when both are drawn, and between the boxes of the nodes that host them otherwise. These are the dependencies that the `overview`, `detailed` and `deep` viewpoints draw; the dependencies inside one environment stay out, as in those viewpoints. `uses` is a scope and no link of its own. The dependency is read from the relationships of the application that is deployed and from the ones of everything inside it (a relationship of a component of a deployed container counts); a relationship that ends inside the same application gives nothing. The destination is read the same way: a relationship that ends on a component of a deployed container is a dependency towards the node that hosts the container, and the destination must be a deployed application or inside one (see <<using-environments>>).

A saved map of the technology layer that says neither property draws both, so that a saved map of the technology layer draws more than it did in 0.14.0, where it drew neither the applications nor the dependencies between the environments. The properties are `applications` and `relationships`, `false` to leave one out; any other value is no choice, in both tools. They mean nothing in a map of the application layer. A comparison of two plateaus gives the states to the applications as it gives them to the other boxes.

[[plateau-views-lacking]]
*A view in a workspace of plateaus.* A view is read against one plateau at a time. A name that the plateau drawn does not have, and another plateau has, is not a mistake of the view, and the two tools say the same: an entry of `pinned`, `opened` or `hidden` is left out of the map (`warning: the view `m` is drawn without `x`, which the plateau `p` does not have`), and a view that cannot be drawn without the name (the `element` of an overview, a map none of whose `pinned` elements is there, or none of whose `links` is there: see <<map-links>>) cannot be drawn in that plateau. `archicode views generate --view <id>` skips such a view with a warning, and does not fail, since asking for a view in every plateau of a loop is legitimate:

----
warning: skip `x`: it names `shop.mailer`, which the plateau `to-be` does not have
----

The editor says it in the view (_The element “x” is not in the plateau “p”._). A name that exists in no plateau is the error of <<plateau-untied>>.

*A shipped example.* The layered Brightcart of the repository (`src/test/resources/brightcart/layered`, a fixture of the tests, not an example of this manual) keeps seven views and three stories in a folder, `shared/`, that no plateau owns, so that these cases are checked on it. `shop_overview` is an overview of `shop`, which the plateau `marketplace` renames `storefront`: `archicode views generate --plateau marketplace -i shop_overview` skips it with the warning above, and the three other plateaus draw it. `marketplace_overview` is drawn in one plateau only, and `applications_map` is drawn everywhere, without `shop` in `marketplace`, with a warning. A view is drawn by `views generate` only when it is named with `-i`; the plain command draws the built-in views alone.

*A view whose element a facet removes.* A view you ask for by its `id` (`views generate --view <id>`) that is about an element that a facet removes cannot be drawn in that facet. The command does not skip it, since you asked for it by name: it stops with one line that names the view, the element and the facet, exits with the code 1 and writes nothing, not even the folders of the facets before it. The check is made when `--view` is given, in the facets of the plateau and in those of its comparison, before the first view is drawn:

----
error: the view `saas_probe` cannot be drawn: it names `commerce-saas`, which the facet `no-externals` removes
----

The line is the one of `archicode views generate --plateau saas -i saas_probe` on a copy of the layered Brightcart with a view `saas_probe`, an `overview` of `commerce-saas`, which the facet `no-externals` of that workspace removes (the first facet that does, in the order of the file; the facets of the plateau come before those of its comparison). Name the view in a workspace where the facet keeps its element, or leave it out of the command. The same holds in a workspace that has no plateau. Without `--view` nothing is checked, since the views drawn are made from the elements the facet still has; a map whose lists name an element that a facet removes draws it without that element and says nothing.

By embracing dynamic view generation, leveraging pluggable Viewpoint Services, and facilitating ad-hoc view definitions, ArchiCode equips architects with a toolkit for capturing, communicating, and exploring architectural insights in a dynamic and tailored manner. As we navigate deeper into ArchiCode's capabilities, you'll uncover the rendering of views and how they enhance your architectural workflow.

[[stories]]
==== Stories

A story puts views in order, with a sentence for each, to tell how a part of the architecture works. It does not draw anything on its own: each step shows a view, and the editor draws that view live from the model, so a story stays true as the architecture moves.

Stories and views are manifests, like the elements: one file each, in the manifests folders, with the kinds `archicode.morin.io/story` and `archicode.morin.io/view`. The editor writes them for you (see <<editor-stories>>), but they are plain YAML:

.manifests/story.order_flow.yaml
[source,yaml]
----
header:
  kind: archicode.morin.io/story
  version: "1"
content:
  id: order_flow
  name: How an order travels
  description: From the shop to the payment provider.
  steps:
    - view: shop_overview          # a view that has a name: see below
      text: The shop receives the order.
    - viewpoint: map               # or a view written right in the step
      layer: application
      properties:
        pinned: [shop.payments]
        hidden: [mailer]
        opened: [shop]
        hide-unlinked: true
      text: It hands the payment over.
----

.manifests/view.shop_overview.yaml
[source,yaml]
----
header:
  kind: archicode.morin.io/view
  version: "1"
content:
  id: shop_overview
  name: Shop — Overview
  viewpoint: overview     # overview, detailed or deep: drawn by the command line as well
  layer: application
  properties:
    element: shop
    facet: flow           # optional: the editor draws the view through that facet
    descriptions: true    # the texts of the diagram, when they differ from its default (see the editor)
    wrap: true
----

A step either names a view by its `id`, or carries one: a `viewpoint`, a `layer` and `properties`, as a view has. A named view can be used by several stories and generated by the command line (its `id` is what `--view` takes); a view written in a step is only for that step, and has no file of its own. The text is plain text. A step on a view that is not declared is reported as a warning by the command line, and shown as missing by the editor. The command line does not draw the stories (see below): it reads them and checks them, and it generates the views of the workspace as it always did, the views of the `map` viewpoint included (see <<map-viewpoint>>).

A story belongs to no plateau, like a view (see <<plateau-untied>>): it is the same story whatever plateau is drawn, and it is declared once in the whole workspace. A `plateau:` in its header is accepted and means nothing, as it does in a view (see <<plateau-untied>>).

[[story-step-plateaus]]
*A step told at a plateau.* A step may say which plateau it is told at and which plateau it is compared with, with two optional keys, `at` and `against`, each the `id` of a plateau. They are not the `plateau:` of a header, which says that a manifest belongs to a plateau: here the story belongs to none and a step says where to look.

* Without `at`, the step is told at the plateau the reader has drawn. Without `against`, nothing is compared. A step that names neither is drawn as the reader had it, the plateau and the comparison.
* `against` without `at` is allowed: the step is told at the plateau drawn and compared with `against` (_what changes from `baseline` to where I am_). When `against` is the plateau drawn, nothing is compared, which is no mistake.
* A key with nothing after it, or an empty text (`at: ""`), names nothing.

.`plateaus/story.migration.yaml` of the example `domain_plateaus`
[source,yaml]
----
include::examples/domain_plateaus/plateaus/story.migration.yaml[]
----

Both tools check the keys when the manifests are read, one sentence for each, with the story, the file and the number of the step (from 1), after the layers and before the names the step carries:

----
error: the story `migration` (`plateaus/story.migration.yaml`) step 1 says `at: tobe`, which is not a plateau of this workspace (the plateaus are `baseline`, `to-be`)
error: the story `migration` (`plateaus/story.migration.yaml`) step 2 says `at: to-be` and `against: to-be`: a plateau is never compared with itself
error: the story `s` (`manifests/story.s.yaml`) step 1 says `at: to-be` but the workspace declares no plateau (a `plateau` key in a header, or a manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau`)
----

The last one is told for a workspace that has no plateau too. The command line checks the steps when it loads the workspace, and draws them with `archicode stories generate` (see <<stories-generate>>). The fork of the layered Brightcart ships a story that tells one (`fork_tour`: its steps are told at `baseline`, at `saas` and at `inhouse`, two plateaus that override `baseline` and do not stand on each other, and compared with `baseline`, with each other and with the plateaus `marketplace` stands on), next to `order_journey`, whose steps name no plateau, and `seller_journey`, whose steps are all told at `marketplace`. A name that the plateau of a step lacks is no mistake either: `stories generate` skips the step with a warning, and the editor, which plays the steps and draws their plateaus, tells it (see <<editor-stories>>).

Workspaces written before stories became manifests keep their views in `views:` of `.archicode.yaml`, and may have a `stories:` there too: both are still read. The editor offers to move them to manifests, which keeps `.archicode.yaml` small and free of the rewrites an editor save makes to it.

[[story-of-a-flow]]
*A story of a flow.* A story can show how something travels, one relationship more at each step, so that the reader sees where the flow has got to. Each step is a Map of the application layer with its own recipe: `properties.links` holds the relationships drawn so far, and the step's `highlight` key names the newest one only, so that one link is heavy and the others are calm. A step adds one entry to `links` and moves `highlight` to it. The step's key is the one place of its highlight: it replaces the highlight of a view, as above.

The layered Brightcart ships one, `order_flow`, with three steps at `baseline`: the path of an order from the customer to the payment provider, each step opening `shop` and `shop.app` so that the last one draws its link.

.`shared/story.order_flow.yaml` of the layered Brightcart
[source,yaml]
----
header:
  kind: "archicode.morin.io/story"
  version: "1"
content:
  id: "order_flow"
  name: "An order, link by link"
  description: "The path of an order through the shop, one relationship more at each step, the newest one highlighted."
  steps:
    - viewpoint: "map"
      layer: "application"
      properties:
        opened: ["shop", "shop.app"]
        links:
          - source: "customer"
            destination: "shop.web"
            label: "browses and buys with"
      text: "The shopper browses and buys with the web storefront."
      at: "baseline"
      highlight:
        - source: "customer"
          destination: "shop.web"
          label: "browses and buys with"
    - viewpoint: "map"
      layer: "application"
      properties:
        opened: ["shop", "shop.app"]
        links:
          - source: "customer"
            destination: "shop.web"
            label: "browses and buys with"
          - source: "shop.web"
            destination: "shop.app"
            label: "calls"
      text: "The storefront calls the application."
      at: "baseline"
      highlight:
        - source: "shop.web"
          destination: "shop.app"
          label: "calls"
    - viewpoint: "map"
      layer: "application"
      properties:
        opened: ["shop", "shop.app"]
        links:
          - source: "customer"
            destination: "shop.web"
            label: "browses and buys with"
          - source: "shop.web"
            destination: "shop.app"
            label: "calls"
          - source: "shop.app.payments"
            destination: "psp"
            label: "authorises payments with"
      text: "And the payments component authorises the payment with the provider."
      at: "baseline"
      highlight:
        - source: "shop.app.payments"
          destination: "psp"
          label: "authorises payments with"
----

The editor writes the same form. On the Applications page, tick the relationships so far, highlight the newest one and choose *Add to story...*: the new step carries the ticked relationships in `properties.links` and the highlight as its `highlight` key, and the field of the step edits that key. The editor writes the `links` and the `highlight` of a view or a step sorted by source, destination and label, without a duplicate, and `highlight` after `links`, so a file the editor saves reads the same whatever order the relationships were ticked in. *Play* plays the story in the player, one step at a time (see <<editor-story-player>>), and `archicode stories generate --story order_flow` draws one folder for each step, with the heavy link of each (see <<stories-generate>>).

==== Rendering

// - In ArchiCode, a View is rendered by a Renderer.
// - In ArchiCode, a Renderer is an opinionated set of pre-defined rules generating artifacts for the end user.
// - ArchiCode leverage on pluggable Renderer Service to generate views.

ArchiCode introduces a systematic approach to rendering views, enriching architectural communication through visualization. Here's how ArchiCode orchestrates the process of rendering views:

- View Rendering Mechanism: Within ArchiCode, each View is rendered by a designated "Renderer." This integral component takes the architectural model and translates it into tangible artifacts that resonate with the end user, facilitating a comprehensive understanding of the design.

- Renderer Definition: A "Renderer" within ArchiCode embodies a set of opinionated and pre-defined rules. These rules are specifically tailored to generate artifacts that eloquently present architectural insights, ensuring consistency and clarity in communication.

- Pluggable Renderer Service: ArchiCode harnesses a pluggable Renderer Service architecture, enabling seamless integration and utilization of diverse renderers. This extensibility empowers architects to choose the most suitable renderer for the intended audience or purpose.

By introducing a robust rendering mechanism, employing opinionated rules for artifact generation, and facilitating the pluggable Renderer Service, ArchiCode enhances architectural communication by transforming abstract architectural concepts into tangible representations.

[[stereotypes-and-styles]]
==== Stereotypes and styles

Every box of a diagram is drawn with a list of _stereotypes_.
The stereotypes of an element are, in this order, its `qualifiers` (each one whole: `Spring Boot` is one stereotype) and the words of its `rendering-stereotypes` tag.
The tag is a whitespace-separated list, so that `external changed` gives the two stereotypes `external` and `changed`, and a value of one word gives the one stereotype it always gave.
A stereotype named twice counts once.
The renderer then adds the kind of the element and the way the box is drawn.
The command line and the editor read the stereotypes the same way: they come from the same kernel.

The `styles` of the workspace give a stereotype its look.
`styles.by-tags` and `styles.by-qualifiers` map a stereotype to a style, and a box has the style of every stereotype it is drawn with.
The key is the stereotype itself, not the name of a tag: the keys of `by-tags` are the words of `rendering-stereotypes`, the keys of `by-qualifiers` are the qualifiers.
The two maps are equivalent for the renderer, which only sees stereotypes; use the one that says where the stereotype comes from.
A style sets any of `background-color`, `line-color`, `line-style`, `line-gap`, `line-thickness`, `round-corner`, `font-color`, `font-style` and `shadowing`.
`line-style` is the length of a dash (0 is a solid line) and `line-gap` the gap between two dashes: without `line-gap` the gap is as long as the dash, so that `line-style: 5` is a dash of 5 and a gap of 5 and `line-style: 16` with `line-gap: 3` is long dashes with narrow gaps (PlantUML's `LineStyle: 16-3`).
A `line-gap` means nothing without a `line-style`; when a style overrides the `line-style` of another one, the gap of the other style goes with the dash it replaces.

.Styling the boxes tagged `external` and `changed`
[source,yaml]
----
styles:
  by-tags:
    external:
      background-color: "lightgray"
    changed:
      line-color: "orangered"
      line-thickness: 3
  by-qualifiers:
    PostgreSQL:
      round-corner: 20
----

.An element drawn with the `external` and the `changed` styles
[source,yaml]
----
content:
  id: "mailer"
  name: "Mail Provider"
  tags:
    rendering-stereotypes: "external changed"
----

*In the editor.* The editor draws a box with the style of the stereotypes it is drawn with: `background-color` is the fill, `line-color`, `line-thickness`, `line-style` and `line-gap` the outline (derived from the fill when only the fill is given, see below), and `round-corner`, `font-color`, `font-style` and `shadowing` the rest.
A styled stereotype is also written on its box as a text badge with its name, so that a colour is never the only carrier.
The same badge follows the name of the element in the *Elements* tree and in the tree of the *Map*, and in the header of an element (after its kind, on *Details* and on the diagram tabs; when the header is narrow it shows the badges that fit and the name keeps its room), and the key under the diagram shows the badge of each styled stereotype drawn.
The badges are part of the SVG and PNG exported from a diagram, while the key is not; a badge is not the dot that marks an element as modified and not yet saved.

For the same stereotype, `by-tags` wins over `by-qualifiers`.
When a box has several styled stereotypes, a later one overrides an earlier one property by property: with `rendering-stereotypes: "external changed"`, the fill of `changed` wins, and the properties it does not set come from `external`.
The order is that of the stereotypes of the box, its qualifiers first and then the words of the tag.
The command line follows the same rule: it writes the stereotypes of a box last first, since PlantUML lets the first stereotype of a box win, property by property, whatever the order of the styles.
The `.puml` of a box carrying `external changed` therefore says `+<<changed>> <<external>>+`, and the same workspace always gives the same file, byte for byte.
The one difference: `shadowing` is a flag that is only ever switched on, so a style cannot switch off the shadow another style of the same box switched on (the editor lets `shadowing: false` of a later stereotype do it).

Colours are read as `#rgb`, `#rrggbb`, `rgb(r, g, b)`, `transparent` or a CSS colour name such as `lightgray`; a value the editor cannot read is ignored.
Numbers are whole numbers.

The fill is the colour written in `styles`, in both themes, as in the diagrams of the command line; `styles` has no separate dark colour.
A box with a `background-color` and no `line-color` is outlined with a line derived from its fill: the same colour, darker (each of its channels at 60 %, so the hue is kept), or lighter when the fill is so dark that darkening it would show nothing, as for black.
Without it the outline would be the grey of the theme, lost on the fill, and a box inside a box of the same fill, such as the systems of a solution that are both `external`, would vanish into it.
The derived line is as firm on a box that contains others as on one that does not.
The `line-color` of the style always wins, whatever the fill, and the fill itself is never changed; a box with no fill keeps the outline of the theme, and the outline of the box in focus or selected stays the accent colour.
The same line is drawn on the badge of the stereotype, and in the SVG and PNG exported from a diagram.
In the diagrams of the command line, PlantUML draws a box with the fill of its style and its own thin dark outline, so a box inside a box of the same fill is already told apart there: nothing is derived.
The editor chooses the colour of the text so that it reads on that fill, black or white with a contrast ratio of at least 4.5: the `font-color` of the style is kept when it reads well, and replaced otherwise.
Without a `background-color`, the box and its text keep the colours of the theme, and `font-color` is not used. Except that a box that contains others, with no `background-color` of its own, drawn inside one that has one, lets that fill show: its title is then black or white against that fill, in either theme, so that it never lands on a fill it cannot be read on.

Compared with the command line, `line-style` and `round-corner` are an approximation of what PlantUML draws.
The editor styles only the stereotypes of an element, its qualifiers and the words of `rendering-stereotypes`: it does not use the kind of the element, nor `atomic` and `composite`, as keys, and `styles.atomic` and `styles.composite` are not drawn.

[[technical-configuration]]
=== Technical Configuration

// - ArchiCode is shipped with an opinionated set of configuration.
// - The default configuration can be changed in the workspace.
// - The localization of the manifests can be changed.
// - When a relationship is created based on natural ones, the default label "uses" is configurable.
// - The generation of the views is also configurable. The output folder can be changed and the labels used in  the built-it viewpoints can be configurable too.

ArchiCode embraces configurability as a cornerstone of its design, affording you the ability to tailor the tool to align seamlessly with your architectural needs. Here's a comprehensive look at ArchiCode's configuration capabilities:

- Opinionated Default Configuration: Out of the box, ArchiCode ships with an opinionated and carefully crafted default configuration. This configuration sets the tone for a seamless and intuitive architectural experience.

- Workspace-Specific Customization: ArchiCode extends the flexibility of configuration to your workspace. The default configuration can be effortlessly customized, allowing you to fine-tune settings and preferences to match the unique demands of your architectural endeavor.

- Manifest Localization: ArchiCode acknowledges the importance of manifest localization. This localization can be dynamically modified, enabling you to adapt and align the tool's behavior with your project's localization requirements.

- Tailored View Generation: ArchiCode's configurability extends to the very essence of view generation. You have the ability to redefine the output folder for generated views, as well as customize the labels utilized in the built-in viewpoints. This level of customization ensures that the generated views align harmoniously with your architectural narrative.

The `settings` also accept a `name` for the workspace (see <<workspace>>) and the plateau that is `current` (see <<plateau-choose>>). They have no default, so the snippet below does not show them. There is no `settings.plateaus`: the plateaus are written in the manifests (see <<plateaus>>).

.The default settings of a workspace
[source,yaml]
----
include::examples/domain_configuration/default_settings.yaml[]
----

.The default configuration for styling
[source,yaml]
----
include::examples/domain_configuration/default_styles.yaml[]
----

.The default configuration for labels formatting
[source,yaml]
----
include::examples/domain_configuration/default_formatters.yaml[]
----

By affording you the capability to tailor the configuration of ArchiCode, the tool seamlessly integrates with your architectural process, providing a personalized and adaptable framework.

[[plateaus-overview]]
== States of an architecture (plateaus)

An architecture is never finished: it is how it runs today, how it should run next year, and what the year after may bring. ArchiCode calls each of these states a _plateau_ and tells you what changes from one to the next. This section is the story in one place, from the idea to a review of the changes; the rules are in <<plateaus,Plateaus>> and <<plateau-comparison,Comparing two plateaus>> (in the Domain section), the commands in <<plateau-choose,Choosing a plateau>>, <<diff,Compare plateaus>> and <<generate-views,Generate views>>, and the editor in <<editor-pages,The pages>>, <<editor-band,The band of plateaus>>, <<editor-plateaus,The Plateaus page>>, <<editor-writing,Writing in a plateau>> and <<editor-compare,Comparing two plateaus in the editor>>. What does not work yet is in <<plateau-limits,Limits of today>>.

IMPORTANT: *Breaking changes.* `settings.plateaus`, `archicode plateau add` and `--baseline` no longer exist: a plateau is a layer (see <<plateaus>>, and <<plateaus-upgrade>> to convert a workspace by hand). A path of `settings.manifests.paths` may no longer contain `..` nor be absolute, a reference declared twice is an error, and a plateau id is now lowercase letters, digits, `_` and `-`. A workspace that has no plateau and does neither is read and drawn exactly as before.

=== The idea

A plateau is a _layer_: the architecture in that state is the plateau it overrides, plus the elements its own manifests add, changed by its patches. You write what differs and nothing is copied, so nothing has to be kept in sync by hand: when the root changes, every plateau above it changes with it, and a patch that no longer fits tells you so. The difference between two plateaus is _computed_ from the references of the elements and the relationships, never typed: ArchiCode tells what is new, what changed and what was removed, and the views, the table of changes and the editor all draw the same answer.

One thing cannot be computed: that two elements are the same thing. An element that moves under another parent, or is renamed, has another reference, so it reads as one removed element and one new one. ArchiCode never guesses it; you say it, on the element of the more recent plateau, with `replaces`, and the comparison then reads a _moved_, _split_ or _merged_ element. It proposes the pairs that look like one thing said twice (the _candidates_) and applies none. The editor writes the hint when you accept a candidate.

=== The layout

The folder of the architecture holds one `.archicode.yaml`, with the settings, the styles and the facets that every plateau shares, the manifests of the root, and, for each other plateau, the manifests that say `plateau: <id>` in their header and a manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau` with its patches. The folders of the example `domain_plateaus` of this manual (`src/doc/examples/domain_plateaus` in the source repository) are drawn in <<plateaus>>; the tutorial below builds it.

=== The settings in short

[cols="1,3"]
|===
| `settings.manifests.paths` | The folders the manifests are read from. A layered workspace ends a path with `+/**+` to read the folders of its plateaus too (see <<manifests-folders>>).
| `settings.current` | The plateau that is drawn when none is asked for; the last one by default when the plateaus form one line. It must be a plateau of the tree. The editor writes it too (see <<editor-current-plateau>>), and opens the folder on it when nothing else says which plateau to draw.
| `settings.plateaus` | No longer exists (see the note in <<plateaus>>): a plateau is a `plateau:` header and a manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau`.
|===

A path of `manifests.paths` is relative to the folder of `.archicode.yaml`, below it (no `..`) and not absolute. Every rule is checked before anything is read, and each problem is reported. The details, with the messages, are in <<plateaus>>.

=== The states

What a plateau changes is told with four states, computed, on elements and on relationships (the fourth is for boxes only), and three more for the elements that say what they replace (see <<plateau-replaces>>):

[cols="1,1,3"]
|===
| State | Mark | Means

| `new` | `+` | the reference is only in the plateau
| `changed` | `~` | the reference is in both and something differs: a property, or a relationship the element declares (an element whose description is the only difference is _wording only_)
| `removed` | `−` | the reference is only in the other plateau, the one compared with
| `contains-changes` | `±` | the element did not change by itself, but something inside it did
| `moved` | `→` | the element says that it replaces one element of the other plateau (elements only)
| `split` | `⋔` | the element says that it replaces an element that another element replaces too (elements only)
| `merged` | `⊕` | the element says that it replaces several elements (elements only)
|===

Each state has a mark, a line style and a legend, so that nothing relies on colour: see <<plateau-comparison,Comparing two plateaus>> for the command line and <<editor-compare,Comparing two plateaus in the editor>> for the editor.

[[plateau-state-tags]]
*The states and the tags you wrote by hand.* The states are stereotypes of the boxes, the same ones that a `styles.by-tags` entry or a `rendering-stereotypes` tag names (see <<stereotypes-and-styles>>). If you used to mark the boxes of a target by hand with `rendering-stereotypes: "new"` or `"changed"`, nothing breaks, and the command line and the editor draw such a tag the same way. One rule decides it: _a box gets the default look of a state (its mark, its outline, its tint, its struck title) only when the comparison computes that state for it._

* A word you wrote that is the state the box has (a `new` tag on a new element) is one stereotype: the computed state is not written twice, it is the _last_ stereotype of the box, so its look overrides the look of the other stereotypes as a later stereotype always does (the generated `.puml` writes the stereotypes in reverse, so it reads first there). Your `styles.by-tags` entry named like the state wins, property by property, over the default look of ArchiCode: a `new` with only a `background-color` keeps the thick outline of the default.
* A word you wrote that is _not_ the state the box has (a `new` tag on an element that did not change, a `new` tag on an element that changed, any state word when no comparison is made) is only your stereotype (a `.puml` of a comparison writes it `written-new`, so that the default look of `new` cannot reach it). The box has the style you gave to that name in `styles.by-tags` or `styles.by-qualifiers`, if any, and nothing of the default look, whatever else the view shows. On an element that changed, the computed state, which is last, has the last word over it.
* The tags themselves are not read by the comparison (a `rendering-*` tag is never a change), so you can drop them when you like: the computed states say the same, and say it only where it is true.

The command line tells you where a word and the comparison disagree, one line per element and word, on the error output (not in the log, which hides warnings by default), and the files it writes and the table it prints are the same whether you read the lines or not. With the tag `rendering-stereotypes: "new"` added to `customer` in the example, which did not change, `archicode diff` says:

----
warning: the element `customer` carries `new` (`rendering-stereotypes` tag), but the comparison of `to-be` with `baseline` finds that it did not change and holds no change: it is drawn with your own `styles` entry named `new` (if any) and not with the default look of the state. The comparison computes the states, so the word can be dropped.
----

`archicode diff` and `archicode views generate` check the plateau they compare. `archicode views generate` of a plateau that is not compared says that a state word is a state of a comparison and that nothing is compared here. A workspace with no plateau has no comparison to disagree with, and is never told anything: there the words are your own stereotypes, as they always were. A word that agrees with the comparison is never reported. The editor draws the same: a hand-written word is a badge and a style, and the look of a state only where the state is computed. It tells you the same, in the same sentences, when a plateau is compared (*Compare with* on the band): in the panel of the element, under what changed, and in the *Hand-written states* list of the *Changes* panel (see <<editor-compare>>). Nothing is told when nothing is compared, since the comparison is a choice of the page.

[[plateau-replaces]]
=== Saying what an element replaces

A comparison finds an element by its reference. An element that moves under another parent, or is renamed, has another reference: the comparison reads it as one removed element and one new one, with nothing that links them. ArchiCode never guesses that two elements are the same thing; _you_ say it, on the element of the more recent plateau, with the key `replaces`: a list of the full references, in the plateau it is compared with (below, _the other plateau_: the one it overrides, or the one `--against` names), of what the element replaces. The key is written where the new element is declared, in its manifest. It is the same hint as the `moved` block of Terraform. The editor writes it when you accept a candidate (see <<editor-replaces>>), and you can edit it in the form of an element. The editor can move an element under another parent without breaking a reference, but a move does not say that the two references are one thing, and nor does a rename (see <<editor-move>>).

.The container `inventory` in the plateau `to-be` (`plateaus/to-be/inventory.yaml` of the example `domain_plateaus`)
[source,yaml]
----
include::examples/domain_plateaus/plateaus/to-be/inventory.yaml[]
----

The inventory used to be a container of the system `shop` and is now one of the system `warehouse`, so its reference went from `shop.inventory` to `warehouse.inventory`. It says what it replaces, and so does the component `levels` inside it.

The key is optional, and a tool that does not know it ignores it. The comparison turns the elements that say what they replace into three states, and the replaced elements are no longer _removed_:

* *moved*: the element replaces one element, which no other element replaces. It is in another place or has another name, and it is the same thing. Whatever else it changes, it keeps it: a name, a description and the relationships it declares are compared with the element it replaces, and the table says which (the `natures`), next to `moved`. A moved element is counted as `moved`, not also as `changed`.
* *split*: several elements replace the same element (`shop.orders` became `shop.cart` and `shop.checkout`). Each of them is `split`, and the table names the other ones.
* *merged*: the element replaces two elements or more (`shop.billing` replaces `shop.invoices` and `shop.receipts`), or an element that is in the other plateau and in the plateau at the same time. When an element is both split and merged, it is `merged`.

What the replaced elements held is not carried by itself: a container that moved takes none of its children with it, so each child needs its own `replaces` (`levels` above), or it is read as removed (the box of the replaced container is then drawn as the place it was removed from, with the words "replaced by"). What points at a replaced element follows it when exactly one element replaces it: a relationship to `shop.till` that now points at `stock.till` is not a change of the element that declares it, and nor is a node that deployed `shop.till` and now deploys `stock.till`. What points at a split element is not rewritten, because which half would own it is a guess. The relationships of the halves, and those that end on a half, are therefore not compared with the other plateau and read as new, whether the element had them before it was split or not; `archicode diff`, `gap.md` and `gap.json` say so on each of those rows (`of the split shop.report-a`) and count them in the header (see <<diff>>).

[[plateau-candidates]]
*Candidates.* When an element is only in the other plateau and another one of the same kind and the same layer is only in the plateau, and nothing replaces or is replaced by either of them, the comparison may find that they look like one thing that was said twice: the same name (ignoring the case), a description with most of its words in common, or the only two elements of that kind under the same parent (only when one of the two plateaus is the past of the other, that is, the plateau it overrides or one further down the same line, `baseline` being the past of all: two plateaus that build on the same one are alternatives, and sharing a parent says nothing between them). A name counts only when no other removed element and no other new element of that kind has it: a `Cache` in every service, or a `Message Broker` in every environment, would pair every removed one with every new one and say nothing about which is which, so such a name proposes nothing (the description and the parent can still). It lists them as candidates and it never applies one. To accept one, write `replaces` on the new element; to refuse it, do nothing. `archicode diff`, `gap.md` and `gap.json` list the candidates once the plateau uses `replaces` (a workspace that does not is written as it always was), each with its reasons. The candidates are not drawn in the views.

[[plateau-replaces-warnings]]
*Warnings.* The comparison does not fail on a `replaces` that it cannot use: it ignores it, and says so on the error output of `archicode diff` and of `archicode views generate` (not in the log, which hides warnings by default), one line per entry (the example has none; the sample below is from the invented warehouse that <<diff>> uses):

* a reference that is not an element of the other plateau (you may be comparing with another plateau than the one you wrote it for);
* a reference that is still an element of the plateau (what is there is not replaced, and an element cannot replace itself);
* a reference listed twice (it counts once);
* an entry that is not a reference (`- 12`, or an empty entry: it is ignored, and it is not read as the reference `12`);
* a `replaces` that is not a list (`replaces: "shop.mailer"`: it is ignored, and the element replaces nothing).

The command line gives the comparison the list as you wrote it, so these lines are the ones the comparison says (the editor refuses a `replaces` that is not a list with a message). A `replaces` that is well written says nothing, and so does an empty list.

----
warning: replaces of shop.report-b (application layer): shop.nowhere is not an element of the base plateau, ignored
----

`replaces` is read from the elements of the plateau that is compared; a `replaces` on an element of the other plateau is not read. A plateau that is not compared does not use it.

*The `replaces` of the layers are composed.* Each layer writes `replaces` relative to the plateau it overrides, so a move that took two steps (a plateau moves `shop.app.search` to `commerce.search`, the next one moves it again to `discovery.search`) names, in the second layer, `commerce.search`, which a plateau two layers up does not have. Between two plateaus several layers apart the comparison reads the entries through the layers in between, down to the plateau it is compared with, so the move is a move: `discovery.search` replaces `shop.app.search`. Only what the layers wrote is read, nothing is guessed. A move then a split is a split, a move then a merge is a merge; an element that a layer in between created and the next one moved replaces nothing (it is `new`) and its entry is no mistake; an element that a layer in between removed ends its own chain. The comparison with the plateau a plateau overrides is as it was: one layer, nothing to compose. On the layered Brightcart of the repository (`src/test/resources/brightcart/layered`), `archicode diff --plateau marketplace --against baseline` counts `+38 ~3 -6` elements, `11 moved, 2 split, 1 merged` and `+35 ~1 -9` relationships, lists `discovery.search` as `moved` from `shop.app.search`, and has no warning. Between two plateaus that do not stand on each other (`marketplace` against `saas`) only the layers of the plateau drawn since the plateau they have in common are read, and an entry that the plateau compared with still lacks is ignored with the usual warning, which names the reference the layers composed (`shop.app.search`, where the entry says `commerce.search`). *Write replaces* in the editor writes the entry you choose, one step. When the comparison is several layers apart and a layer in between removed the replaced element, the entry would be a mistake of its own layer: the editor refuses it and prints the sentence quoted in <<editor-replaces>>.

*What the command line shows.* `archicode diff` has the three states in its table, with the counts of `moved`, `split` and `merged` on a line of their own (`replacing`), and says in the details of each row what the element replaces (see <<diff>>). The views of the comparison draw them with the marks above (see <<plateau-comparison>>). The editor draws them too, lists the candidates, and writes `replaces` for you (see <<editor-replaces>>).

=== A tutorial: from one workspace to a review of the changes

The tutorial builds the example `domain_plateaus` of this manual, an invented bookshop, from the architecture it has today to a review of the changes in the editor, including the things that change shape. Its commands run in the folder of `.archicode.yaml`; the output quoted is the one the commands print.

*Step 1. Start from the architecture you have.* An existing workspace is already your first plateau: its manifests are the root, `baseline`, and nothing in them has to change, not even a header. To keep the next plateaus in folders beside them, move the manifests to a folder of their own and read it with a path that ends in `+/**+` (see <<manifests-folders>>); the folder does not have to be called `baseline`, the name is for the reader.

----
mkdir -p plateaus/baseline
mv manifests/* plateaus/baseline/
----

.`.archicode.yaml`
[source,yaml]
----
include::examples/domain_plateaus/.archicode.yaml[]
----

A manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau` is optional for the root. This one only gives it a name and a horizon, and the tools show them (`baseline` is its `id`, and cannot be another):

.`plateaus/baseline/plateau.baseline.yaml`
[source,yaml]
----
include::examples/domain_plateaus/plateaus/baseline/plateau.baseline.yaml[]
----

`archicode plateau list` shows the tree, which is a root for now:

[source,shell]
----
archicode plateau list
----

----
PLATEAU   PARENT  SOURCE                                            NAME   HORIZON
baseline  -       manifest plateaus/baseline/plateau.baseline.yaml  As is  2026-Q3
----

A workspace that names no plateau (no manifest of that kind, no `plateau:` in a header) is not a workspace of plateaus: it lists `baseline` alone, `implicit`, and generates its views in `views`, as ever. Once a plateau is named, even the root, the views of a plateau go to a folder named after it, here `views-baseline` (and `views-baseline-<facet>` for the facets, as the `directory-name-template` of the facets says). Move or update what reads the old folder.

.`plateaus/baseline/shop.yaml`
[source,yaml]
----
include::examples/domain_plateaus/plateaus/baseline/shop.yaml[]
----

*Step 2. Add the plateau of the future.* A plateau is two things: a manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau`, which says what it overrides and what it changes, and the manifests of the elements it adds, which say `plateau: "to-be"` in their header. Create the folder `plateaus/to-be`, and in it `plateau.to-be.yaml`, which for now only names the plateau (its `patches` come in step 3), and, for instance, the container `ratings`:

.`plateaus/to-be/plateau.to-be.yaml`, to begin with
[source,yaml]
----
header:
  kind: "archicode.morin.io/plateau"
  version: "1"
content:
  id: "to-be"
  name: "To be"
  horizon: "2027-Q1"
  override: "baseline"
----

The other container manifests of the layer (`payments.yaml`, `cart.yaml`, `checkout.yaml`, `billing.yaml`) and the manifests of the root that this page does not show (`customer.yaml`, `warehouse.yaml`) are in `src/doc/examples/domain_plateaus`: copy them from there to follow the steps.


.`plateaus/to-be/ratings.yaml`
[source,yaml]
----
include::examples/domain_plateaus/plateaus/to-be/ratings.yaml[]
----

Its `parent` is the system `shop`, which only the root declares: a layer can put an element under any element of the plateaus it stands on. The tree has a second plateau now:

----
PLATEAU   PARENT    SOURCE                                            NAME   HORIZON
baseline  -         manifest plateaus/baseline/plateau.baseline.yaml  As is  2026-Q3
to-be     baseline  manifest plateaus/to-be/plateau.to-be.yaml        To be  2027-Q1
----

The folder `plateaus/to-be` holds what the layer adds, nothing of what it inherits. The command line and the editor read it as the plateau `to-be` because of the headers, not because of the name of the folder.

*Step 3. Change what the layer inherits.* In the example, `shop.payments` is added, `shop.mailer` is removed, `shop.web` gets the qualifier `React` and its relationships change, a description is reworded, and four things change shape: the orders are split in two, the invoices and the receipts become one, the reviews are rewritten as the ratings, and the inventory moves from the shop to another system, the warehouse. What is added is manifests (`payments.yaml`, `cart.yaml`, `checkout.yaml`, `billing.yaml`, `ratings.yaml`, `inventory.yaml`); what is removed or changed is the `patches` of the plateau, which are applied in this order (see <<plateau-patches>>):

.`plateaus/to-be/plateau.to-be.yaml`
[source,yaml]
----
include::examples/domain_plateaus/plateaus/to-be/plateau.to-be.yaml[]
----

* Six `REMOVE`: what the new containers replace, and what is no longer done. A `REMOVE` takes the element with what is in it and what points at it, and `archicode diff` lists what it took besides the element (step 5).
* A `SET` of the qualifiers of `shop.web`; a `SET` with `where` that relabels the one relationship of `shop.web` towards `shop.catalog` (the same patch that the editor writes when you change a relationship); an `ADD` of the relationship towards `shop.payments`.
* A `SET` of the description of `shop.catalog`: the wording of the description is the only change of that element.
* An `ADD` of a relationship to `warehouse.picking`: what pointed at the inventory follows it. The `REMOVE` of `shop.inventory` took the link that `picking` had, and the inventory is a new container of the warehouse in `inventory.yaml`.

`shop.payments` still carries the tag `rendering-stereotypes: "new"` that its author wrote by hand: it works as before, and the comparison does not need it. In the comparison its word agrees with the state `new`, so nothing is said; when a hand-written word and the comparison disagree, the command says so on its error output (see <<plateau-state-tags>>).

A patch that no longer fits is an error that names the plateau, the rank of the patch and its selector. With a typo in the `target` of the description's `SET` (`shop.catalogue`):

----
error: plateau to-be: patch 10 (SET target shop.catalogue): selects nothing, there is no element shop.catalogue in the application layer
----

*Step 4. Say what moved, was split or merged.* Compare the two plateaus. Read by their references alone, `shop.orders`, `shop.invoices`, `shop.receipts`, `shop.reviews` and `shop.inventory` (with its child) are gone, and `cart`, `checkout`, `billing`, `ratings` and `warehouse.inventory` (with its child) are new, with nothing that links them. Some of them are the same things, and only you know which, so you say it with `replaces` on the elements of the more recent plateau (see <<plateau-replaces>>):

* `shop.orders` was *split*: `cart` and `checkout` both say `replaces: ["shop.orders"]`.
* `shop.invoices` and `shop.receipts` were *merged*: `billing` lists both in its `replaces`.
* `shop.inventory` *moved* to the warehouse. It is a manifest of its own, with the `parent` of its header, and `plateaus/to-be/inventory.yaml` says `parent: "warehouse"` and `replaces` on the container and on its child `levels`: a container that moves takes none of its children by itself.
* `shop.ratings` says nothing, on purpose: it will show a candidate in step 6.

.`plateaus/to-be/inventory.yaml`
[source,yaml]
----
include::examples/domain_plateaus/plateaus/to-be/inventory.yaml[]
----

In `plateaus/baseline`, `inventory.yaml` has the same content with `parent: "shop"` and no `replaces`, and `warehouse.yaml` holds the system `warehouse` with its container `picking`. What points at a moved element follows it, but only if you say it: `picking` counts with `shop.inventory` in the root, and the layer's last patch adds the link to `warehouse.inventory`, so that the comparison does not read it as a change of `picking`.

*Step 5. Ask what changed.* `archicode diff` compares the plateau that is drawn by default, the last one, with the plateau it overrides:

[source,shell]
----
archicode diff
----

[source,text]
----
comparing to-be (To be) with baseline (As is)
elements      +2 ~2 -2
replacing     2 moved, 2 split, 1 merged
relationships +1 ~1 -1
wording only  1 of the 2 changed elements

CHANGE   THING         LAYER        REFERENCE                                   NATURES                                                                     DETAILS
merged   element       application  shop.billing                                -                                                                           from shop.invoices, shop.receipts; name: "Billing"
split    element       application  shop.cart                                   -                                                                           from shop.orders; name: "Cart"; split with shop.checkout
changed  element       application  shop.catalog                                description (wording only)                                                  description: "The books on sale." -> "The books that are on sale."
split    element       application  shop.checkout                               -                                                                           from shop.orders; name: "Checkout"; split with shop.cart
removed  element       application  shop.mailer                                 -                                                                           name: "Mailer"
new      element       application  shop.payments                               -                                                                           name: "Payments"
new      element       application  shop.ratings                                -                                                                           name: "Ratings"
removed  element       application  shop.reviews                                -                                                                           name: "Reviews"
changed  element       application  shop.web                                    qualifiers,relationships-added,relationships-removed,relationships-changed  qualifiers: +React
moved    element       application  warehouse.inventory                         -                                                                           from shop.inventory; name: "Inventory"
moved    element       application  warehouse.inventory.levels                  -                                                                           from shop.inventory.levels; name: "Levels"
changed  relationship  application  shop.web -> shop.catalog (browses)          label                                                                       label: "reads" -> "browses"
removed  relationship  application  shop.web -> shop.mailer (sends mails with)  -
new      relationship  application  shop.web -> shop.payments (pays with)       -

candidates, not applied (each pair looks like one thing said twice; to accept one, write `replaces: [FROM]` on the element TO)
LAYER        FROM          TO            REASONS
application  shop.reviews  shop.ratings  description

cascade of the removals (what a REMOVE took besides the elements it selects, in the plateaus the target adds to the one it is compared with)
PLATEAU  PATCH                           EFFECT   LAYER        ELEMENT                DETAIL
to-be    1 REMOVE target shop.mailer     dropped  application  shop.web               relationships: shop.mailer (sends mails with)
to-be    6 REMOVE target shop.inventory  removed  application  shop.inventory.levels
to-be    6 REMOVE target shop.inventory  dropped  application  warehouse.picking      relationships: shop.inventory (counts with)
----

The NATURES column says what changed in an element that changed (here `qualifiers` and the relationships of `shop.web`, `description (wording only)` for the catalog), and the DETAILS column says from what and to what. Two elements changed, one only by its wording, and two moved, two were split and one merged: the table says in each row what changed or where the element came from (see <<diff>> for the options and the other formats). The replaced elements have no row and are not counted as removed, and `shop.mailer` and `shop.reviews` are, because nothing replaces them. The last section is the cascade of the `REMOVE` patches (see <<plateau-cascade>>): the removal of the mailer took the link of the web site towards it (patch 1), and the removal of the inventory took its component `levels` and the link of `picking` (patch 6). Nothing is written, and a difference is not an error, unless you ask for it to be: `archicode diff --fail-on removed,moved` prints the same table and exits with the code 3, because the example has removed and moved elements (a state the plateau does not hold gives 0; see <<diff>>). That is the line to put in a build that must not let a removal through.

*Step 6. Decide about the candidates.* The candidate section is a pair: `shop.reviews` is removed, `shop.ratings` is new, they are of the same kind in the same layer and their descriptions share most of their words, so the comparison thinks they may be one thing said twice (see <<plateau-candidates>>). It never applies a candidate. If it is the same thing, write `replaces: ["shop.reviews"]` on `ratings`: `ratings` becomes a moved element (`moved`, natures `name,description`, `from shop.reviews`), the counts read `elements +1 ~2 -1` and `replacing 3 moved, 2 split, 1 merged`, and the table lists a new, weaker candidate, `shop.mailer` to `shop.payments` with the reason `parent`: with the reviews and the ratings settled they are the only removed and the only new container of the shop. They are not the same thing, and the answer is to do nothing. If the reviews and the ratings are not the same thing either, do nothing, as this example does, and the output stays as it is. The editor writes the line for you (step 8).

*Step 7. Draw the comparison.* `views generate` writes the views of a plateau, and the views of the comparison next to them (with the plateau it overrides, or the one `--against` names):

[source,shell]
----
archicode views generate
----

----
$ ls
plateaus
views-to-be
views-to-be-vs-baseline
.archicode.yaml
$ ls views-to-be-vs-baseline
gap.json
gap.md
plantuml
----

(The `ls` shows what a clean folder shows; a folder that already had `manifests` or `views-baseline` still has them: they are not removed.) `views-to-be` holds the views of the plateau, as for any workspace. `views-to-be-vs-baseline` holds the views of the comparison, and `gap.md` and `gap.json`, the table of changes of step 5 in a file that a pull request can carry (with the cascade, the key `cascade` of the JSON). `archicode views generate --only-changes` draws, in `views-to-be-vs-baseline` only, what changed and the context faded: the views of `views-to-be` and the table stay as they are. Every box and every link of these views that changed says its state with a mark and a line style, and the views that have a change have a legend. The overview of the container `shop.web`:

.The overview of `shop.web` in `views-to-be-vs-baseline`
[plantuml, plateaus_shop_web_overview]
----
include::examples/domain_plateaus/views-to-be-vs-baseline/plantuml/application/shop_web-overview.puml[]
----

`shop.web` and `shop.catalog` are changed (dotted outline, `~`), `shop.payments` is new (thick outline, `+`), `shop.mailer` is removed (dashed, struck title, `−`), and the system `shop`, which did not change by itself, _contains changes_ (`±`). The links say it the same way: `+ pays with` is new, `− sends mails with` is removed, and the label of `shop.web` to `shop.catalog` changed, from `reads` to `browses`.

The detailed view of the system `shop` draws what was split and merged. The titles say where the boxes came from, `(from shop.orders)` and `(from shop.invoices, shop.receipts)`, and the legend has one line for each of the two states, with dashes that tell them from the others. `shop.ratings` is new and `shop.reviews` removed: the candidate is not drawn.

.The detailed view of `shop` in `views-to-be-vs-baseline`
[plantuml, plateaus_shop_detailed]
----
include::examples/domain_plateaus/views-to-be-vs-baseline/plantuml/application/shop-detailed.puml[]
----

The inventory is drawn where it is now, in the warehouse, with `→` and `(from shop.inventory)`. The view of its child `levels` shows the container and the component that moved together, each with the reference it came from.

.The overview of `levels` in `views-to-be-vs-baseline`
[plantuml, plateaus_levels_overview]
----
include::examples/domain_plateaus/views-to-be-vs-baseline/plantuml/application/warehouse_inventory_levels-overview.puml[]
----

*Step 8. Look at it in the editor.* `archicode editor serve` opens the folder in your browser (see <<serve-the-editor>>).

The editor opens the plateau of the address (`plateau=`), else the one this browser drew last in this folder, else the one `settings.current` names, else the root, `baseline`: here the root. The command line, without a name, draws the last plateau.

. The top bar has four buttons, *Timeline*, *Stories*, *Files* and *Settings*. *Timeline* opens *Applications* and has four pages, in a bar of their own: *Plateaus*, *Applications*, *Environments* and *Elements*. On those four pages, and on those only, the band under the top bar has two lists: *Plateau*, which draws a plateau in the whole editor, and *Compare with*, which shows the same tree of plateaus under _Nothing_ and _Past_. Choose *To be* in *Plateau* (see <<editor-band,The band of plateaus>>). The page *Plateaus* says the same as a tree: one entry for each plateau, with its name, its horizon and what it brings over the plateau it overrides (chips such as `+ 2 new`, `~ 2 changed` and `− 2 removed`), and it is where a plateau is written (see <<editor-plateaus,The Plateaus page>>).
. In *Compare with*, choose *Past (follows the plateau drawn)*: Applications shows the states, the new, changed, removed, moved, split and merged boxes, and the key under the drawing is the legend (see <<editor-compare,Comparing two plateaus in the editor>>). *Past* is the plateau *To be* overrides, here *As is*; choosing *As is* in the tree below it is read the same way. The tree lists every other plateau (the plateau drawn is greyed in it), so that a plateau that overrides the one drawn, or any other, is compared with by choosing it there.
. *Plateaus*, *Applications*, *Environments* and *Elements*, and *Stories* while a comparison is on, have the *Changes* panel on the right: the counts (`+ 2 new`, `~ 2 changed`, `− 2 removed`, `→ 2 moved`, `⋔ 2 split`, `⊕ 1 merged` for the elements), the candidates, the *Cascade of the removals* with the three rows of step 5, then every element and relationship that changed. They are the rows of step 5, in the same words. *Next change* and *Previous change* walk through them, one by one, and the lists *State* and *Layer* narrow what is read; a click on a row shows it (see <<editor-changes-panel>>).
. Select a changed element in *Elements*: the block *What changed* gives each property _Before_ and _After_ and its relationships that changed; for a moved, split or merged element it says what it replaces (see <<editor-what-changed>>). The page of the element says where each value comes from: for _Web site_, the name and the description are _inherited from baseline_ and the qualifiers are _set by patch 7 of to-be (SET target shop.web)_.
. *Changes only*, among the tools of a diagram, keeps what changed and the boxes needed to place it, and *Hide wording-only changes* sets the rewording aside (see <<editor-changes-only>>).
. Under the counts, the *Candidates* section proposes the pair of step 6, _Reviews_ and _Ratings_, with its reason, _close description_. *Write replaces* adds `replaces: [shop.reviews]` to `ratings` in the manifest that declares it, `plateaus/to-be/ratings.yaml`, and changes nothing else in the file; `ratings` is now `moved`, the counts read `→ 3 moved`, and *Save all changes* writes the file. With the clear pair resolved, the comparison proposes a weaker one: _Mailer_ and _Payments_ are now the only removed and the only new container under `shop`, which is the reason it gives (_the only two of their kind under the same parent_). They are not the same thing, so *Not related* is the answer (see <<editor-replaces>>).
. A change is written in the plateau that is drawn. In *To be*, select _Web site_, *Edit*, change its description and *Save all changes*: the description comes from `baseline`, so the editor adds one `SET` patch at the end of the `patches` of `plateaus/to-be/plateau.to-be.yaml` and leaves `plateaus/baseline/shop.yaml` as it was. The same edit made in *As is*, on a field that `to-be` inherits (the description of _Picking_), writes the root and, because `to-be` sees the change, asks first (_Writing in baseline changes other plateaus_, with the list of the plateaus) (see <<editor-writing>>).
. *Move* puts an element under another parent (or, for a kind that may be a root, at the top level), with what is inside it, and rewrites the references to it (see <<editor-move>>); it moves an element that the plateau drawn declares, such as _Ratings_ of `to-be`, and refuses one it inherits. *Move* writes no `replaces`.
. *Stories* lists the stories and the views of the workspace, whatever plateau is drawn. The story _From the shop to the new shop_ (`plateaus/story.migration.yaml`, the file shown in <<story-step-plateaus>>) tells it in four steps: *Play* opens the story in a window of its own that covers the editor and plays it like slides, and each step that says `at` and `against` is drawn at that plateau and with that comparison, so that the first step shows the shop of _As is_ and the second the shop of _To be_ with what changed marked; the slide says _Plateau To be · compared with As is_, and closing it leaves the editor as it was (see <<editor-stories>> and <<editor-story-player>>). *Show on diagram*, on a step, draws that step on the editing diagram instead, and the bar over it says _told at To be, compared with As is_.
. *Files* lists every file, in a group for each plateau: _As is_ with its five manifests, _To be_ with its seven, and, last, _No plateau_ with the story, which belongs to none (see <<editor-files>>).

A change is written in the plateau that is drawn; the comparison itself writes nothing (see <<editor-writing>>).


*Step 9. Go on.* To prepare the state after `to-be`, write another manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau` with `override: "to-be"` and the manifests that say `plateau: "later"`:

.`plateaus/later/plateau.later.yaml`
[source,yaml]
----
header:
  kind: "archicode.morin.io/plateau"
  version: "1"
content:
  id: "later"
  name: "Later"
  horizon: "2028-Q1"
  override: "to-be"
  patches:
    - operator: REMOVE
      target: "shop.checkout"
----

.`plateaus/later/search.yaml`
[source,yaml]
----
header:
  kind: "archicode.morin.io/container"
  version: "1"
  plateau: "later"
  parent: "shop"
content:
  id: "search"
  name: "Search"
  description: "Finds a book by its title."
----

The plateaus still form one line, so `later` is now the plateau that is drawn by default, and it is compared with the plateau it overrides:

[source,shell]
----
archicode diff
----

[source,text]
----
comparing later (Later) with to-be (To be)
elements      +1 ~0 -1
relationships +0 ~0 -0
wording only  0 of the 0 changed elements

CHANGE   THING    LAYER        REFERENCE      NATURES  DETAILS
removed  element  application  shop.checkout  -        name: "Checkout"
new      element  application  shop.search    -        name: "Search"
----

(The command also prints, on the error output, the warning of step 3 about the hand-written `new` of `shop.payments`: against `to-be` that element did not change, so the word is only the author's.) `archicode diff --plateau later --against baseline` compares the last plateau with the first one, and `archicode plateau list` shows the three. When two plateaus override the same one, the tree forks and the commands ask for the plateau with `--plateau` (see <<plateau-choose>>); the editor opens the plateau of the address, else the one you drew last, else the one `settings.current` names (which *Settings* can write, see <<editor-current-plateau>>), else the root.

*Step 10. Put it away.* A plateau can be deleted in the editor, from the page *Plateaus* (see <<editor-plateaus,The Plateaus page>>): *Delete* opens a dialog that lists the files that go, here `plateaus/later/plateau.later.yaml` and `plateaus/later/search.yaml`, and nothing else. Had another plateau overridden `later`, the dialog would also have said which plateaus go under its parent and what they lose. The files leave the disk at the next *Save all changes*. The command line deletes nothing: remove the files by hand, and the folders `views-later` and `views-later-vs-to-be` that `views generate` wrote, if you made them.

[[plateaus-upgrade]]
=== Upgrading: what breaks, and what does not

*From `settings.plateaus` to layers.* The key no longer exists, and no command converts a workspace: a `.archicode.yaml` that still has it is refused with the one line quoted in <<plateaus>>. To convert one by hand:

. Keep the manifests of your first plateau: they are the root, `baseline`, and need no header. Its id changes: `--plateau as-is`, `settings.current: as-is` and scripts that name it must say `baseline` (a `Kind: Plateau` manifest with `id: baseline` and `name: "As is"` keeps the name for people).
. For each later plateau, from the oldest, write a manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau` (its `id`, `name`, `horizon` and `override`: the previous plateau, or `baseline` for the first). The manifests of elements that are _new_ in that plateau get `plateau: "<id>"` in their header. What the plateau removes or changes among the elements it inherits becomes a patch (`REMOVE`, `SET`, `ADD` or `MERGE`, see <<plateau-patches>>). The copies of the unchanged manifests in the old complete folders are deleted: a manifest that declares again an element of a plateau below it is the error of <<plateau-header>>.
. Delete `settings.plateaus`, and write `manifests.paths` (a workspace with plateaus could not have one; the default is the folder `manifests`) with an entry that ends in `+/**+` for the folders that hold the plateaus (see <<manifests-folders>>).
. Run `archicode plateau list` and `archicode diff --plateau <id>` for each plateau: the counts should be the ones you had.

Four differences to know: the views of a plateau are written in `views-<plateau>` and the views of its comparison in `views-<plateau>-vs-<against>`; every plateau but the root is now compared with the plateau it overrides when its views are generated; a plateau id is lowercase letters, digits, `_` and `-` (a capital, or a dot as in `v1.2`, is refused: `error: the plateau id `v1.2` in ... is not valid`); and `--baseline` is `--against`. The `from` key of a plateau, which was reserved, and `archicode plateau add`, are gone.

*Earlier changes.* Two things that used to pass stop with an error that says what to change. Everything else about a workspace that has no plateau is unchanged: it is read, and generates its views in the same folders, byte for byte as before (checked on the views of this repository's workspaces and on a copy of a real workspace of 3,411 views).

. *A path of `settings.manifests.paths` with `..` or an absolute path.* `../shared`, `/srv/manifests` and `C:\data` are refused: one folder holds a whole workspace, so that the editor, which only reaches the folder it opened, reads what the command line reads. Move the folder below the folder of `.archicode.yaml`, or move `.archicode.yaml` up to a folder that holds both, and change the path to match. A path that stays below the folder (`manifests`, `node_modules/<module>/manifests`) is not affected.
. *A reference declared twice.* Two manifests, a manifest and an element nested in another one, or two elements of one file that share a reference used to pass silently, and which of them was drawn depended on chance. The command line now stops, names the reference and the two files, and writes nothing, and the editor reports the same in the same words (see <<duplicate-references>>). Remove one of the declarations, or give one of them another `id`. Two identical copies count too. This check applies to workspaces without plateaus as well; with plateaus, the same reference in a plateau and in its ancestor is the same error, and in two plateaus that do not stand on each other it is no duplicate, since they are alternatives.

Nothing is to be changed in the manifests themselves: their format is the one it always was, with two optional keys more, `plateau` in the header and `replaces` (see <<plateau-replaces>>), that an older version of ArchiCode ignores.

*Later changes.* Layers came with the editor reshaped; a few things moved after them, and what a workspace written for the first version of layers may meet is this:

. *A view or a story that says `plateau:`.* It is accepted and means nothing, with no message at all (see <<plateau-untied>>; the warning that earlier versions printed is gone). A workspace whose only `plateau:` is on a view or a story is no longer a workspace of plateaus, so `--plateau` and `settings.current` are errors for it, as for any workspace without plateaus. Two plateaus that each declared a view of one id, or a story of one id, are now one error: a view and a story are declared once.
. *Kinds.* An element declared with `header.parent` under a parent that cannot hold its kind (a system under a container) is an error, with or without patches, where it used to load in a plateau that had none (see <<nesting>>); an id with a dot is an error. The group kinds were refused here at the time; since 0.17 there is no group at all (see below).
. *Empty values and spellings.* A key with nothing after it and an item with no value in a list are absent, in both tools (`global-enabled:` with nothing is `true`; an empty `.archicode.yaml` is the default workspace; an item of `relationships` with no value draws no relationship in the editor either). The keys of `settings.facets` are `global-enabled`, `directory-name-template`, `hide-unlinked` and `json-path`; the camel-case spelling was still read then; since 0.17 it is refused (see below). The text of `header.parent`, `header.plateau` and `header.version` is the text as written: `parent: yes` is the reference `yes`.
. *The maps.* `archicode views generate --view <id>` draws a view of the `map` viewpoint, which it used to skip with a warning (see <<map-viewpoint>>).
. *The order of the warnings.* The facets are read in the order of the file, so the warnings that name them, and the creation of their folders, come in that order.
. *Folders of a comparison.* A folder that holds both `gap.json` and `gap.md` is left out of a `+/**+` read, whichever command wrote it (see <<manifests-folders>>).
. *The editor.* The pages are Plateaus, Applications, Environments, Elements, Stories, Files and Settings (see <<editor-pages>>); the band has two lists; Files has no choice of what it lists.

[[upgrade-0-20]]
*What changes for a reader who knows 0.20.0.* These are the changes after the release 0.20.0; they are in the release 0.21.0. Nothing breaks: the editor opens in a browser that has no folder picker, a Map can be drawn from the relationships it lists, a story can light up one relationship at a time, and a few mistakes are fixed or told in the same words by both tools. A workspace that loaded in 0.20.0 loads in 0.21.0 and the command line writes the same files for it (checked by running every command on every example and fixture of the repository against the jar of 0.19.0: the only differences are the sentence of 0.20.0 for a reference that names no element, see <<upgrade-0-19>>, and the files of the new story `order_flow`); the new keys `links` and `highlight` mean nothing until you write them. As for the blocks below, there is no release notes file in the repository: these blocks are the notes.

. *The editor works in Chrome, Firefox and Safari with nothing installed* (New, see <<editor-browser-workspace>>). Where the browser has no folder picker, or you do not want one, the editor keeps a workspace in the browser; one button on the presentation page opens the Brightcart sample; a workspace is exported to, and imported from, one YAML file with the same bytes. Nothing on your disk is read or written unless you pick a folder, and the editor served by `archicode editor serve` never touches the served folder while a browser workspace is open.
. *A Map can list the relationships it draws* (New, see <<map-links>>). `links` in the properties of a Map of the application layer is a list of `source`, `destination` and an optional `label`: the boxes drawn are the ends of those relationships with the boxes around them. `archicode views generate` draws it, and an entry that names no relationship is one `error:` line. A Map with no `links` is drawn as before.
. *A link can be highlighted, in a view and in a story step* (New, see <<map-highlight>> and <<story-of-a-flow>>). `highlight` draws the named links heavy, with a mark, and the others calm; a story step can say its own, so that a story lights up a flow one link after the other. `archicode stories generate` draws it, and the editor and the story player draw it too. The shipped example is the story `order_flow` of the Brightcart fixture.
. *The editor lists the relationships of a Map* (New, see <<editor-map-links>>). Under the tree of the page Applications, tick the relationships to draw, or click them on the diagram; the choice is saved as the `links` of the view, kept in the address, and followed by a rename or a move of an element.
. *The plateau tree is indented by depth for a chain of any length* (Fixed). The indentation stopped growing at the sixth level, so a long chain of plateaus read as siblings on the page Plateaus and in the two lists of the band.
. *The name field of a new browser workspace is one line high* (Fixed).
. *A view with no element, or one that names an element that does not exist, is said in the same words by the command line and the editor* (Changed message, see <<upgrade-0-19>> for the first of the two sentences).
. *The step 3 of the story `migration` of the example `domain_plateaus` draws something* (Fixed). It played an empty slide. The editor no longer logs an error on load when the browser asks for its icon.
