. *The manual's recipe for folding a plateau by hand is repaired* (see <<editor-plateau-delete>>). It now says to compose the `replaces` of the plateau above, and that two columns of the cascade of the removals change, the plateau and the number of the patch.
. *The editor page is built from source files* (Changed, no effect on a reader). The one file that is distributed is the same page; it is assembled, formatted and linted from about eighty parts.

[[upgrade-0-19]]
*What changes for a reader who knows 0.19.0.* These are the changes after the release 0.19.0; they are in the release 0.20.0. Nothing breaks: three things are new, one thing is fixed, five mistakes are told better (a sentence that names the file and the key, or one `error:` line where there was a stack trace), and every other command prints and writes what 0.19.0 printed and wrote, byte for byte (the views, the tables of `diff`, `query graph`, `query views`, the generated stories and the output of `query schemas workspace`; this was checked by running every command on every example and fixture the repository ships against the jar of 0.19.0). A script that reads the old text of the sentence of the second item below has to read the new one. As for the blocks below, there is no release notes file in the repository: these blocks are the notes.

. *`archicode query schemas manifest` describes the content of every kind* (New, see <<manifest-schema>>). It printed a schema of the envelope only, with the content as any value; it now carries the schema of the ten kinds side by side under `kinds` (keys, required keys, enums, shapes, what each element kind holds, whether it can be the top of a layer, the id rule and the reference form), the nested elements under `nested`, and it is derived from the classes that read the manifests. The output is still sorted. A tool that read the old output and ignored what it did not know is not affected; one that took the content for anything will find an object. The schema cannot say a dozen things the loader checks (a reference that resolves, a name declared twice, the tree of plateaus, a patch that selects nothing); the section lists them.
. *`archicode guide` is new* (see <<guide>>). It prints a guide for an agent, generated from the code, that needs no workspace and no network, and starts with `archicode guide <version>`.
. *`archicode init` is new* (see <<init>>). It writes a small workspace that loads and is drawn, adds `/views*/` to `.gitignore`, and overwrites nothing.
. *The editor's save keeps two layouts it used to write again* (see <<editor-elements>> and <<plateau-limits>>). A file that has a block scalar as an item of a list (`- |`, then its text) no longer makes any save, even of another key, write the whole file again in the editor's layout with every comment lost; and a block scalar on the second key of a map that is a list item (`note: |`) stays a block scalar when the first key of the item goes or another comes before it, where it was written as a quoted text. The file read the same before; what changes is the comments and the layout that are kept. Two shapes still fall back to the emitter's text: `- - |` on one line, and a folded block (`- >`) that reads differently once written again; they, and the two nesting cases listed with them, are in the limits.
. *A saved view or a story step with no `properties` is one `error:` line* (Fixed). It ended in a `NullPointerException` and a stack trace; it now says ``the view `v` has no element: write the reference of one in `properties.element` ``, exit 1, the same mistake as `properties: {}`. A map needs no element and is drawn.
. *A reference that names no element says where it is* (Changed message). A relationship `destination`, a `uses` entry or a hosted application that names an element which does not exist said `unable to find the element nowhere`; it now says which element holds it, in which file and at which key, for example ``error: `shop.web` has a relationship towards `nowhere`, which does not exist, in `manifests/shop.yaml` at `relationships[0].destination` ``, and the editor says the same sentence word for word (a workspace with plateaus adds ``in the plateau `p`, ``). It stays an error where it was one, so no command that succeeded fails.
. *`archicode views generate -e <engine>` with an engine that does not exist, and `-p <file>` with a file that does not exist, are one `error:` line each* (Fixed). They ended in a stack trace; they now say ``unknown rendering engine `nope` (known: plantuml)`` and that the file does not exist or cannot be accessed.
. *`archicode init` leaves nothing behind when it is stopped* (Fixed). A folder it could not write to (a `manifests` folder without the right to write) left `.archicode.yaml` behind, and the next `init` refused the folder as already a workspace; it now removes every file it created.
. *A mistake in one key of a manifest never ends in a stack trace* (Checked). A test copies every example and fixture, removes one key, empties one list or sets one key to `null`, and runs the commands: each result is a success or an `error:` line. The sweep found no other stack trace in the files the repository ships.

[[upgrade-0-18]]
*What changes for a reader who knows 0.18.0.* These are the changes after the release 0.18.0; the release that carries them has no number in this manual yet. One of them can stop a workspace that loaded before, a facet whose name cannot be the name of a folder, and it comes first; the others change a sentence, a saved file or a screen, or add something. As for the blocks below, there is no release notes file in the repository: these blocks are the notes. The `error:` lines are the ones the tools print.

. *A facet whose name is not a folder name is refused* (Breaking, for such a name). The name of a facet goes as it is written into the folders of its views, so a name that is empty, is `.` or `..`, holds `/`, `\`, a NUL or another control character or one of `< > : " | ? *`, or ends with a dot or a space is now refused by every command that loads the workspace and by the editor, with one `error:` line for each facet, exit code 1, before anything is written. A facet called `a/b` loaded in 0.18.0 and wrote its views in `views-a/b`, a folder inside a folder; it now stops with:
+
----
error: the facet name `a/b` cannot be the name of a folder: it holds `/`, which separates the folders of a path (write a plain word, such as `journey`)
----
+
Rename the facet in `.archicode.yaml`, and rename or delete the folder it wrote if you keep it. Every facet of the examples of this manual and of the repository's tests uses plain words, so their views are the ones they were, byte for byte. The rule and the names it does not look at are in <<facets>>.
. *A workspace file that cannot be read is one line, not a stack trace* (see <<errors-and-warnings>>). A `.archicode.yaml` that is there and that the file system refuses to read (a file without the right to read it) printed `error: unexpected java.io.FileNotFoundException: ... (Permission denied)` and a stack trace in 0.18.0; it is now `+error: the workspace file `...` cannot be read: permission denied+`, exit code 1. The other ways to have no workspace file, and the sentence for `workspace.yaml`, are word for word what they were.
. *The editor's card says what is wrong with the file* (see <<editor-open>>). For a `.archicode.yaml` that is read and found wrong (a key that does not exist, YAML that is not valid, a file that is not a mapping, `settings.plateaus`, a path or a facet name that is refused) the card _Can't open this workspace_ shows the sentence of the command line, where it said that the file could not be read and added a hint to check that it is valid YAML. It keeps those words for a file that cannot be had, and the list of the remembered folders notes only those.
. *The editor keeps the brackets* (see <<editor-elements>>). A list or a map that you wrote in brackets (`qualifiers: [a, b]`, `tags: {zone: north}`) and that a change reaches is saved in brackets, on its line, when it fits, where it was saved as a block. Nothing is lost either way: the file reads the same, and the cases that still become a block are listed there.
. *A story can be played* (see <<editor-stories>> and <<editor-story-player>>). *Play*, on the page of a story, opens the story in a window of its own that covers the editor and plays it like slides: the diagram of each step, drawn at the plateau and with the comparison the step names, its words, a list of the steps, *Previous* and *Next*, full screen, and the arrow keys, `Home`, `End` and `Esc`. The editor behind it does not change: when the window closes, the plateau, the comparison, the selection, the page and the address are what they were, and nothing is written. `play=` in the address keeps the window in a link. The bar over the editing diagram, which *Show on diagram* raises for one step, no longer has *Previous*, *Next* or a list of the steps: they are in the player. Nothing the command line writes changes: `archicode stories generate` and `views generate` draw what they drew.
. *The save keeps the quotes and the comments it can* (see <<editor-elements>> and <<plateau-limits>>). When you save a file, a value that comes back on a line that is written again (a map in brackets that becomes a list of maps, an item that moves, a list that gives back its item) keeps the quotes it had, and so does a key; a comment stays on the line of its own key or item; and removing the first of two items that read the same removes the first, with its comment, where the comment could stay on the second. A comment that a removed line left right under a block scalar (`key: |`) no longer makes the editor write the whole file again in its own layout, which lost every comment of the file. The file reads the same either way; what changes is that quotes and comments that were lost or put on the wrong item are kept.
. *`archicode --version`* is new (see <<version>>): one line, `archicode <version>`, on the top command only. Nothing else changes on the command line.
. *The release page carries native programs* (see <<install>>). The releases after 0.18.0 attach `archicode-<version>-linux-amd64`, `archicode-<version>-linux-arm64` and `archicode-<version>-macos-arm64` with a `SHA256SUMS` file, each run on its own kind of machine before the release is public. The Maven Artifact and the OCI Image are as they were. There is no file for Windows or for an Intel Mac yet.

[[upgrade-0-17]]
*What changes for a reader who knows 0.17.0.* This release has one change that stops every workspace, the name of the workspace file, so it comes first. There is no release notes file in the repository: this block and the ones that follow are the notes. The `error:` lines below are the ones the tools print.

. *The workspace file is `.archicode.yaml`* (Breaking). It was `workspace.yaml`. The new name is the default of `-w` for every command and the name the editor looks for, and there is no alias and no command that converts: rename the file once, with `git mv workspace.yaml .archicode.yaml`, or keep the old name and say so with `-w workspace.yaml`. A folder that has `workspace.yaml` and no `.archicode.yaml` is refused, by every command and by the editor, with this one line, exit code 1, before anything is loaded or written:
+
----
error: `workspace.yaml` is no longer the name of the workspace file: rename it to `.archicode.yaml` (`git mv workspace.yaml .archicode.yaml`), or tell the command line where it is with `-w workspace.yaml`
----
+
`archicode -w workspace.yaml views generate` works as it did, since the path is explicit. `archicode editor serve` is the exception: it opens a folder that has a `.archicode.yaml`, so `-w workspace.yaml` is refused with a usage error (exit code 2), `the editor opens a folder with a .archicode.yaml: /work/workspace.yaml is not named so.`, followed by the usage of the command; rename the file for the editor. The name begins with a dot, so the file is hidden: `ls -a` shows it, and a file dialog shows it when you ask for hidden files. It is never read as a manifest, in a folder read with `+/**+` or without it (see <<manifests-folders>>); a `workspace.yaml` left behind in a folder that a path ending in `+/**+` reads is read as a manifest and refused (`+error: `workspace.yaml` is not valid: unknown key `settings` (known: content, header) at `settings` (line 5, column 3)+`), so remove it. Change the scripts, the CI files and the Docker commands that name the old file; the views, the tables and everything else the commands write are byte for byte what they were (checked on the twenty workspaces of this repository).
. *`archicode stories generate` writes more* (see <<stories-generate>>). The folder of a step that compares two plateaus gets the table of changes, `gap.md` and `gap.json`, and the facets of the workspace are drawn beside each step, in `<NN>-<view>-<facet>` folders. The files that were written are the same, byte for byte, with one exception: `global-enabled: false` is now honoured, so a workspace with that setting and stories no longer gets the folders of the global views of its steps, only those of its facets. 0.17.0 drew them. A workspace that does not set it loses nothing.
. *More links in the technology views* (see <<using-environments>> and <<map-technology>>). A relationship whose destination is inside a deployed application (a component of a container that a node lists in `applications`) is now a dependency towards the node that hosts it, as a relationship whose source is inside already was; and the links between the nodes of one environment that a facet that keeps a flow draws read the relationships of everything inside the deployed applications. The overview, detailed and deep views of the technology layer and the Map of the technology layer gain the links, in the command line and in the editor. The views of a workspace that deploys no application with such relationships are the same, byte for byte, and so are the views of all the examples of this manual. In the layered Brightcart of the repository's tests, the technology views of the facets `journey`, `no-externals` and `without-cart` and of their comparisons gain links, all inside one environment.
. *The editor reads more like the command line* (see <<editor-elements>>). A tagged number is read as the command line reads it: `!!int 0x1F` is `31`, `!!int 1:30` stays the text `1:30`, `!!binary aGVsbG8=` stays the text `aGVsbG8=` (the page used to refuse them); `foo: .inf` under a key the header does not have is the unknown key, as in the command line; and a style written `line-gap: 0x1F`, `round-corner: 8.0` or `1.5` is drawn with the number the command line reads. The numbers of 0.17.0 are unchanged otherwise.
. *The save keeps more as written* (see <<editor-elements>>). An anchor, an alias or a tag inside square or curly brackets, a text that goes over several lines and a list item whose map starts under its dash are kept as written when you save a file, where the editor wrote the whole file again in its own layout and lost its comments. A few layouts still fall back (see <<plateau-limits>>).
. *The editor checks before it writes* (see <<editor-replaces>> and <<editor-plateau-delete>>). *Add replaces* is checked as *Write replaces* is; a merge that the layer cannot hold is refused as a whole, where its first pair used to be written alone; and the delete of a plateau that others override is also refused when a step of a story is compared `against` a plateau above that would lose what it draws and the plateau the step is told at does not keep it either, or when a saved view is drawn in such a plateau. A delete that was allowed and loses nothing is written as before. How to fold a plateau into the one above by hand is told in <<fold-by-hand>>.
. *Three rough edges*, fixed (see <<editor-compare>>, <<editor-rename>> and <<facets>>): the open key under the diagram keeps its 30 % cap when the window is resized, a write that fails after you said yes to the dialog of the plateaus gives the form back as typed, and a facet the kernel refuses shows its sentence as the error of the view instead of "facet removes this element".
. *The editor is quicker on a large pair of plateaus*: finding what has nothing to draw and resolving the patches take a quarter to a fifth of the time they took on the 591 elements of the test pair, and grow with the elements instead of faster (see <<plateau-limits>>). No output changes.

*What changes for a reader who knows 0.16.0.* This release removes things that were deprecated, so the first four items can stop a workspace that loaded before. Each prints one `error:` line, exit code 1, and writes nothing; the sentences below are the ones the tools print.

. *The group is gone* (Breaking). Write the children of a former group in the `elements` of its parent, which shortens each reference by one segment (`shop.web.edge.gateway` with a group `edge` becomes `shop.web.gateway`). The sentences, for `kind: group` and for the three old header kinds, are in <<manifest-kinds>>; the editor says the same words.
. *The camel-case keys of a facet are gone* (Breaking). `hideUnlinked`, `globalEnabled`, `directoryNameTemplate` and `jsonPath` must be written `hide-unlinked`, `global-enabled`, `directory-name-template` and `json-path`. The sentence names the key to write: `+unknown key `hideUnlinked`, write `hide-unlinked` (known: actions, hide-unlinked, name) at `settings.facets.customs[0].hideUnlinked` +`. `json_path` of a patch is still read.
. *The Jayway dialect of the facets is gone* (Breaking). `anyof`, `noneof`, `== null` and `!= null` are not JSONPath. The command line and the editor refuse the path with `+facet f: the facet path `...` uses `@.qualifiers anyof ['a']`, which is not JSONPath (RFC 9535) and is no longer read: write `@.qualifiers[?(@ == 'a')]` +`; the table of the four rewrites is in <<facets>>. The warning that used to rewrite them is gone with them.
. *The old addresses of the editor are gone* (Breaking). `#/map`, `#/elements`, `#/library`, `#/diagram`, `#/applications`, `#/plateaus`, `#/environments` and `#/timeline` alone open the default page, Applications, like any unknown address. Write `#/timeline/applications`, `#/timeline/elements`, `#/stories`, `#/stories/views`, `#/timeline/plateaus` and `#/timeline/environments` (see <<editor-pages>>). Update your bookmarks.
. *`archicode stories generate`* draws the steps of the stories (see <<stories-generate>>). `views generate` still does not.
. *More dependencies between environments.* A relationship declared by what is inside a deployed application (a component of a container that a node lists in `applications`) is now a dependency of the node, in the overview, the detailed and the deep viewpoints of the technology layer and in the Map of the technology layer (see <<using-environments>>). A workspace that deploys containers whose components declare the relationships gains links there; the layered Brightcart gains the dependencies of `app-vm` and `staging.vm` towards `partners` and `sandbox`. The views of every other example are written byte for byte as before.
. *Deleting a plateau in the middle.* A plateau that other plateaus override can be deleted in the editor, in one step; they go under its parent and the dialog lists what each loses (see <<editor-plateau-delete>>).
. *Write replaces refuses an entry its layer cannot hold*, when the comparison is several layers apart and a layer in between removed the replaced element: the button is off, with a sentence (see <<editor-replaces>>).
. *The key of a comparison is folded* under the diagram, with a *Key* button (see <<editor-compare>>), and a write the editor refuses, or an error while it reads the form, no longer empties the form: what you typed stays (see <<editor-rename>>).
. *The numbers.* The editor reads a plain scalar that YAML 1.1 reads as a number as the command line does, and saves it back as written: `id: 010` is the reference `8` in both tools, `parent: 0` and `version: 1` in a header are the text as written, `line-gap: 0x1F` is accepted for a whole number and `"0x1F"`, quoted, is refused, and `.inf` is the one line `+Malformed numeric value '.inf' at `content` +`. Two differences are left and pinned (see <<plateau-limits>>), and 0.18 reads the others like the command line (above). The command line is unchanged.

*What changes for a reader who knows 0.15.0.* Since that release, these things moved; the first can change a file that a workspace had, the second an error:

. *A comparison several layers apart reads the `replaces` of the layers in between.* `archicode diff --plateau <a> --against <b>` and the editor give a move that took two steps as a move, where they gave one element new and one removed with a warning; the table of the two plateaus, the counts and the files that `views generate` writes for the comparison change with it. The comparison of a plateau with the plateau it overrides is as it was (see <<plateau-replaces>>).
. *`views generate --view` on a view whose element a facet removes* is one `error:` line that names the view and the facet, with nothing written, where it was a bare `dangling reference` after some folders were written (see <<plateau-views-lacking>>).
. *A patch can take a field out* (`operator: REMOVE`, `target`, `field`), and the editor writes it when you clear an inherited text, where it refused (see <<plateau-patches>> and <<editor-writing>>). The patches that were written before are read as they were.
. *The editor*: the Remove buttons of a qualifier, an application and a use remove the item they sit on; cancelling the dialog of a rename or of a write that changes other plateaus gives the form back as typed; *Move* asks which views would break in the plateaus beside the one drawn, as a rename does; a saved file keeps its byte-order mark, and a comment after the last line of a moved block goes with it (see <<editor-rename>>, <<editor-move>>); and the forms of the centre no longer go past a window 360 pixels wide (see <<editor-small-screen>>).

*What changes for a reader who knows 0.14.0.* Since that release, these things moved; the first two can change a file or an error that a workspace had:

. *A saved Map of the technology layer draws more.* It draws the applications deployed on the open nodes and the dependencies between environments that they give, unless the view says `applications: false` or `relationships: false` (see <<map-technology>>). The views of a workspace that has no such map are written as they were, byte for byte, and so are the other viewpoints.
. *A root container, component or node in a workspace that has plateaus is an error*, with the same line in both tools (see <<nesting>>). A workspace with no plateau is read as it was.
. *The band*: *Compare with* shows the tree of the plateaus and has no entries _Future_ any more (see <<editor-band>>).
. *The editor* has the field *Current plateau* in *Settings*, `J` and `K` for the walk through the changes, the box of *Add to story...* that writes `at` and `against`, and a dialog before a rename that leaves a view broken in a plateau beside the one drawn (see <<editor-current-plateau>>, <<editor-keyboard>>, <<editor-stories>> and <<editor-rename>>).
. *The command line*: `archicode diff --width <columns>` wraps the text table (see <<diff>>), and a `plateau:` in the header of a view or a story says nothing (see <<plateau-untied>>).

One more thing changed for a workspace that has plateaus and that marks its boxes by hand with a state word (`rendering-stereotypes: "new"` or `"changed"`). A word that agrees with the comparison is drawn as before. A word on an element that does not have that state used to give the box the default look of the state in the views of the command line, and the editor did not: it now gives it nothing in both, and the command says so on its error output (see <<plateau-state-tags>>). The files that a workspace without a state word writes are the same, byte for byte.

[[plateau-limits]]
=== Limits of today

These are things that do not work yet, or not the way you might expect. Each is known, and the tools say so when they meet one. Those marked _not planned_ will not be built; the others are known and not scheduled.

*Plateaus.*

* *The editor does not fold a plateau into the one above* (not planned). Deleting a plateau that others override is one step and tells what the plateaus above lose, but no operation moves the patches and the elements of the deleted plateau into the plateau above, and the delete is refused when a step or a view would lose what it draws. Folding is done by hand, and <<fold-by-hand>> says how.
* *The editor does not move an element a plateau inherits* (a patch never changes an `id` or a `parent`): draw the plateau that declares it.
* *A comparison with the parent cannot stay on the parent.* On the band, the parent in the tree of *Compare with* is read as _Past (follows the plateau drawn)_, so the comparison moves to the new parent when another plateau is drawn. There is no fixed comparison with the parent.
* *Which plateau the editor and the command line draw first.* The editor draws the plateau of the address, then the one this browser drew last, then `settings.current` (which *Settings* writes, see <<editor-current-plateau>>), then the root; the command line draws the last plateau when the plateaus form one line and no `settings.current` says otherwise.
* *The editor and a large pair of plateaus.* Finding what has nothing to draw and resolving the patches grow with the number of elements, not faster: for the 591 elements and 839 changes of a test pair (`replaces-large-pair`), in the page, the first call takes about 0.08 second, where the 82 elements of the layered Brightcart take 10 milliseconds. What still grows faster is the unfolded Map, which builds the view again for each box it could open. No shipped workspace is that large.

*Reading and writing files.*

* *The save of the editor writes a file again in its own layout* in a few cases, with the comments lost and the aliases written as their values: an anchor or a tag on a key inside brackets (`{&k zone: north}`); a single pair in a list in brackets that also holds an anchor, an alias or a tag (`[&a one: two]`); a tagged text over several lines inside brackets; a list item whose map starts under its dash, nested inside another such item; and two block scalars that a list item holds in a form the save does not follow: `- - |` on one line (a block scalar that is the item of a list that is itself an item), and a folded block (`- >`) that the emitter would write with other line breaks, which read differently once written again. A list item that is a block scalar (`- |`, then its text) and a block scalar on the second key of a list item whose first key goes or comes are kept as written. The file is read and the model is what it says. The other layouts that were in this list in 0.17.0 are kept as written (see <<editor-elements>>).
* *A few comments are lost or move, and a list is wrapped in brackets, when the lists around them are nested again.* Two cases, found by a generated test of the save, are left: a comment on an item that is itself a list (`-` then its items, or `- [c, d]  # second row`) is lost, or taken by the item above, when the list around it is wrapped in another list or unwrapped; and a list that holds a list in brackets, wrapped in another list, is written in brackets as a whole. The value read is the same in each case; the layout, or a comment, is not what you wrote. (Up to 0.19.0 a block scalar that is a list item, and a block scalar on the second key of a map that is a list item, were in this list; they are kept now, see <<upgrade-0-19>>.) In every other layout the generated test of the save covers, it finds no quote or comment lost on a line that an edit did not touch.
* *A collection in brackets that a change reaches is sometimes saved as a block.* It stays in brackets, on its line, when it fits (see <<editor-elements>>); it becomes a block when it would be wider than the widest line of the file (100 to 120 columns), when a comment stands inside its brackets, when it is an empty `[]` or `{}` that the change fills, and when it has an anchor, an alias or a tag in it. A block in the file is never turned into brackets. The file reads the same either way; only its layout differs from what you wrote.
* *An anchor or a tag on a mapping key* (`&k name: x`) is read by the command line and refused by the editor, which does not open the file and says `an anchor or a tag on a key is not supported` (not planned: a splice on a key could lose text, for a form nobody writes). *The merge key `<<`* is no merge in either tool: it is read as a key named `<<`, which a manifest does not have, so both refuse it (not planned).
* *Differences in how the two tools read a number or a tag are left, and pinned* (a plain scalar that YAML 1.1 reads as a number, a tagged number and a style number are read alike, see <<plateaus-upgrade>>). The editor does not tell the line and the column of a mistake in a file: it names the place by its path in the file, as the command line does (see <<errors-and-warnings>>), because it reads a file into a tree that has no positions. A float that JavaScript prints another way than Java (`5.0`) is the text in a field of any shape: a `value` of a patch is the text `"5.0"` in the editor, a number for the command line (the qualifiers it sets are texts in both). A `!!binary` text in a field that is a whole number, a boolean or an enum is the wrong shape (found a string) for the editor, and `Cannot deserialize value of type ...` for the command line, which names a Java class. A few spellings that only Java defines differ in the sentence of the mistake (the digits of other scripts, a character that is not a base64 code point).

*The command line.*

* *`archicode diff` cannot compare with another version of the workspace in git* (a `--baseline-ref`; not planned). `--width`, `--fail-on` and `--only-changes` are built (see <<diff>> and <<generate-views>>). `views generate` writes no text table, so it has no `--width`.

*The editor on a phone* (not planned). Nobody has used the editor on a real phone: what the editor does in a window 360 pixels wide was measured in a desktop browser, and is told in <<editor-small-screen>>. That includes the player of a story, which was measured at such a window but not tried on a phone: the finger that moves its diagram, the picker of its list of the steps, full screen on a phone and the on-screen keyboard are not checked.

[[editor]]
== Editor

ArchiCode comes with an editor to browse, edit and draw a workspace without leaving the browser. It is a single page, `standalone.html`, that reads and writes the files of a workspace folder: nothing is uploaded anywhere, the page and the files stay on your machine.

[[editor-open]]
=== Opening the editor

There are two ways to open it:

- *Served by the command line.* `archicode editor serve` serves the editor and opens the folder of the `.archicode.yaml` in it, with nothing to pick. It works in any browser. See <<serve-the-editor>>.
- *From the published page.* The page published next to this documentation, at `/editor/` of the same site, works in Chrome, Edge, Brave, Opera, Firefox and Safari. With no folder to choose it keeps a workspace in the browser, and it still lets you pick a folder where the browser has the File System Access API (Chrome, Edge, Brave and Opera). To try it without a workspace of your own, open link:../editor/#/timeline/applications?plateau=marketplace&compare=past&sample=brightcart[the editor on the Brightcart sample]. See <<editor-browser-workspace>>.

The folder to open is the one that holds the workspace file, `.archicode.yaml`. You pick the folder, not the file, so the hidden name is no obstacle in the dialog of the browser. A folder that has a `workspace.yaml` and no `.archicode.yaml` is not opened: the editor gives the reason beside the folder, with the sentence the command line prints (see <<upgrade-0-17>>).

When a folder cannot be opened, the editor says so on a card, _Can't open this workspace_, and what it says depends on what went wrong with `.archicode.yaml`. If the file cannot be had at all (there is none, a folder stands in its place, or the browser or the server refuses to read it), the card says `.archicode.yaml could not be read in “folder”`, with the reason the browser gave and the hint to choose the root of the workspace. If the file is read and found wrong (a key that does not exist, YAML that is not valid, a file that is not a mapping, `settings.plateaus`, a path of `manifests.paths` or a facet name that is refused), the card shows the sentence the command line prints for it, as it is, without _could not be read_ and without the hint: the file was read, and the sentence says what to change. The sentence for `workspace.yaml` is shown as it is as well. In the list of the remembered folders, the note _Its .archicode.yaml could not be read the last time it was opened_ is given to a folder whose file could not be had, or is still named `workspace.yaml`, and not to one that failed for another reason.

[[editor-folders]]
The published page remembers, in the browser, every folder it opened, with the name its workspace gives itself (see <<editor-workspace-name>>). When the page opens again, it opens the last folder directly if the browser still lets it read it; otherwise it asks for a click (*Reopen*), because a browser only gives that permission back on a click. *Close folder*, at the top right, closes the folder and shows the list of the remembered folders: the closed one stays in it, and the next visit does not open it by itself. When no folder is open, the list shows each folder by the name of its workspace, with the name of the folder below it when they differ, most recent first: a click on one opens it, *Remove from the list* makes the browser forget it, and nothing is deleted on disk. Under each folder, the list says what a click will meet, as the browser answers without asking anything: _Opens without asking_, _Will ask for permission_ (the browser shows its prompt), _Blocked by the browser_, or _Could not be reached_ / _Could not be checked_ (the folder may have been moved or deleted). A folder picked again is not added twice, even by two tabs at the same moment: the browser then holds two records for it for an instant, and the editor merges them into one, keeping the name of its workspace and its place in the list. A folder that cannot be opened (the permission refused, the folder moved or deleted) stays in the list, which says why, until it is removed. Served by `archicode editor serve`, the editor has one folder, the one of the server: there is no list and no *Close folder*.

The editor loads `elkjs` (the diagram layout) and CodeMirror (the file editor) from a CDN when it opens. Without network access, it still works, with plainer diagrams and file editor.

In *Settings*, the paths of the manifests are one per line, and a path that ends in `+/**+` is read with the folders below it (see <<manifests-folders>>); a new element, view or story is written in the folder of the first path. The editor served by `archicode editor serve` does not list the hidden entries of a folder at all, `.archicode.yaml` of the folder itself excepted (it is served at the root only, not in a sub-folder), so a path that names a hidden folder reads nothing there, and does not list a link to a file that points out of the folder, which the command line reads. A browser that lets the page read a folder gives no way to tell a link from a folder: the page stops at a folder that is one it is inside of, but reads a link to another folder, which the command line does not.

[[editor-reads]]
The editor reads and writes YAML manifests (`.yaml` and `.yml`) only. The command line reads JSON and TOML manifests too (see <<manifest-formats>>): the editor reads neither, so what such a file declares is missing from its trees and diagrams, and relationships that point at it show as dangling. It says so, in *Files* (which names the files) and above the trees of *Elements* (which counts them).

A reference declared more than once is reported, not chosen: a red note above the trees of *Elements* and of *Files* gives the same message as the command line, each file that takes part is marked _duplicate_ in *Files* and says so when opened, and no diagram is drawn until each reference is declared once (a view or a story with a duplicated `id` is reported too, and does not stop the diagrams). Nothing else is blocked: the editor still edits and saves, so that the duplicate can be fixed in it.

[[editor-browser-workspace]]
=== A workspace kept in the browser

Where the browser has no folder picker, or when you prefer not to give the page a folder, the editor keeps a workspace in the browser. The card _Open a workspace_ then offers *Open the Brightcart sample*, *New workspace in this browser* and *Import a workspace file*, next to *Open workspace folder* when the browser can pick one. The workspaces kept in the browser are listed under _In this browser_, each with *Rename*, *Export* and *Delete*; a name is 1 to 80 characters and is not used by another workspace.

A browser workspace lives in the data of the site, on this device only: nothing is uploaded. Clearing the data of the site, a private window or a browser that frees space can remove it, so the editor asks the browser to keep it (once, at the first save) and the top bar says _In this browser_ and when the workspace was last exported. Nothing is stored until you click _Save all changes_. Keep a copy with *Export as a file*.

*The sample.* *Open the Brightcart sample* opens a copy of the layered Brightcart workspace, as the same workspace every time: the second click opens the one you already have, with your changes. The address `#/timeline/applications?plateau=marketplace&compare=past&sample=brightcart` does the same, and it is what the button of the presentation page uses (link:../editor/#/timeline/applications?plateau=marketplace&compare=past&sample=brightcart[the editor on the Brightcart sample]). A workspace made from the sample has *Reset to the sample...* in its *Workspace* menu: it names the workspace and what is lost (your changes), and replaces the files with the sample's, in one step.

*Export and import.* The *Workspace* menu of the top bar has *Export as a file*, which downloads one YAML file named after the workspace (`brightcart-bundle.yaml` for the sample): every YAML file of the workspace, each opened by a line `--- # file: <path>`. It waits while changes are not saved. A file the bundle cannot carry (a path that is absolute, hidden other than `.archicode.yaml`, leaves the folder or is not YAML, a file that is not UTF-8) is left out and listed. *Import a workspace file* on the card reads such a file and creates a new workspace from it, named after the file's `settings.name` or else the file; it never changes an existing workspace. It refuses a file over 10 MB, one that is not UTF-8 text, and a path with `..`, an absolute path, a hidden file other than `.archicode.yaml`, or a path given twice, and then creates nothing.

Served by `archicode editor serve`, the page has the same *Workspace* menu, with *Open the Brightcart sample* and, once a browser workspace is open, *Back to the served folder*. Nothing in that menu writes to the served folder.

*What is tested.* The flow above is tested end to end in Chromium, Firefox and WebKit as Playwright builds it. That WebKit is not Safari: the real Safari, Windows and macOS are not tested.

[[editor-pages]]
=== The perspectives and their pages

The top bar has four buttons: *Timeline*, *Stories*, *Files* and *Settings*. *Timeline* has four pages, in a bar of their own under the top bar: *Plateaus*, *Applications*, *Environments* and *Elements*. A click on *Timeline* opens *Applications*, a click on one of the others opens its first page, and the page that is on screen is the pressed one. On the four pages of Timeline, and on those only, the *band* shows the plateau that is drawn and the one it is compared with (see <<editor-band>>).

[cols="2,3,3"]
|===
| Page | What it is | Address

| Plateaus | the plateaus as a tree, each with what it changes over the plateau it overrides; the page where a plateau is made, edited and deleted (see <<editor-plateaus>>) | `#/timeline/plateaus` (and `#/plateaus`)
| Applications | the Map of the application layer, with no element selected (see <<editor-map>>) | `#/timeline/applications`
| Environments | the Map of the technology layer, with no element selected (see <<editor-map>>) | `#/timeline/environments`
| Elements | the trees of both layers, and the page and the diagrams of the element selected in them (see <<editor-elements>>); where an element is edited, created and deleted | `#/timeline/elements`
| Stories | the stories and the views of the workspace, the same whatever plateau is drawn (see <<editor-stories>>) | `#/stories` and `#/stories/views`
| Files | every manifest of the workspace, in a group for each plateau (see <<editor-files>>) | `#/files`
| Settings | the settings of the workspace (see <<technical-configuration>>) | `#/settings`
|===

Since 0.17 the pages answer to the addresses of the table above and to no other: `#/map`, `#/elements`, `#/library`, `#/library/stories`, `#/library/views`, `#/diagram`, `#/applications`, `#/plateaus`, `#/environments` and `#/timeline` alone are no routes any more, and open the default page, Applications, like any address the editor does not know. Replace them with `#/timeline/applications`, `#/timeline/elements`, `#/stories`, `#/stories/views`, `#/timeline/plateaus` and `#/timeline/environments`. The page writes its own address in place of the one that was typed, without adding an entry to the history of the browser. The parameters of an address (`ref`, `tab`, `pin`, `facet`, `story`, `view`, `plateau`...) are the ones they were.

*The address says the plateau.* A link with `plateau=<id>` draws that plateau, and `compare=` the comparison it names (see <<editor-changes-address>>); an address with no `plateau=` is the root, `baseline`, which the page never writes. This is also true once the editor is open: an address that is typed in the tab, followed, or reached by Back and Forward draws the plateau it names (an unknown plateau leaves the one that is drawn). A plateau that you choose, on the band or on the page Plateaus, is an entry of the history, so Back walks back through the plateaus you drew. A comparison chosen on the band changes no address, so Back between two comparisons of the same plateau does nothing.

*Environments* is a Map like Applications, on the technology layer: the tree of environments and nodes on the left, the pins, the eye, the chips, the Changes panel and *Name view...* are the ones of Applications. Unlike Applications, it draws the applications deployed on the nodes and the relationships between environments, and its tools can leave each of them out (see <<editor-map>>). The page *Elements* keeps the tree of both layers, and is where an environment or a node is edited and created, as an application element is. *Show on map*, on an element of either layer, opens the Map of that layer with the element pinned, and a row of the *Changes* panel about the other layer leaves the Map on screen for the Map of that layer.

*Settings* has no section for plateaus: a workspace of layers writes its plateaus in its manifests (see <<editor-plateaus>>), and has the paths of its manifests, as a workspace with no plateau does. It has one field about them, *Current plateau*, which is the key `settings.current` (see <<editor-current-plateau>>). The rest of this chapter says _the Map_ for what Applications and Environments draw, one for each layer. A workspace that writes no layer has the same perspectives and the same pages, and its band has one plateau. Its files are read, drawn and saved as they were.

[[editor-elements]]
=== Elements

The *Elements* page is the tree of both layers of the workspace on the left, and the selected element in the center. The center has four tabs:

- *Details*: the fields, qualifiers, tags and relationships of the element, with *Edit* to change them (change its id and every reference to it follows; *Move*, in the edit form, puts it under another parent, see <<editor-move>>), and *Delete* (the relationships of other elements that pointed at it are listed first, so that none is left dangling by surprise).
- *Overview*, *Detailed* and *Deep*: the viewpoints of the same names, drawn live from the model, as the command line generates them. *Detailed* is for an element that can contain others. Clicking a box in a diagram selects that element.

[[editor-rename]]
*Renaming an element in a workspace of plateaus.* Saving the form of an element with another `id` rewrites every reference to it. In a workspace that has plateaus the rewrite is made in the files of the plateau that is drawn and of the plateaus that stand on it, since the others do not see the element through it, and in the views and the stories of the workspace, which belong to no plateau and so follow everywhere. A plateau beside the one drawn (one that does not stand on it and does not override it) may still declare the element, or inherit it, under its old reference: a view or a step that names the new reference then cannot be drawn there any more. When that is so, saving the form first opens a dialog before anything is written. It names the plateaus that keep the old reference and the views and the steps of the stories (by story and number) that could no longer be drawn in them, and has two buttons: *Cancel*, which writes nothing, and *Rename anyway*, which writes what a rename always wrote. To keep every view whole, cancel, draw each of those plateaus and give the element the same new id there; once every plateau that has it uses the new reference, the views are drawn in all of them. The `links` and `highlight` of a Map view and of a story step name two references each, and a rename or a move rewrites them as it rewrites `pinned`, `hidden` and `opened` (the `label` never changes). Nothing is asked when no view or step is at risk, when the plateau drawn is `baseline` (every plateau stands on it), or in a workspace with no plateau.

*Cancelling.* *Cancel* (or Escape, or a click beside the dialog) writes nothing and gives the form back as you typed it: the new id, the name, the description, what was in the boxes that add a qualifier or a tag, the relationship rows and the `replaces`, and *Save all changes* is lit as before. The same goes for the dialog that lists the plateaus a write changes (_Writing in baseline changes other plateaus_, see <<editor-writing>>). Saying yes writes the form that was typed. A write that the editor refuses (`Not written: ...`), a write that fails after you said yes to the dialog of the plateaus, and an error while it reads the form give the form back as typed too, beside the sentence that tells why: nothing you typed is lost.

*Move* changes a reference too, and the views and the steps follow it in every plateau, so it asks the same question when you press the button of its dialog with a parent chosen and the move is allowed: the same list of plateaus that keep the old reference and of views and steps that could no longer be drawn there, in words of a move, with the buttons *Back to Move* and *Move anyway*. *Back to Move*, Escape and a click beside the dialog give the _Move_ dialog back with its parent and its search as they were, and write nothing, so that you can choose another parent. A move that no sibling plateau is concerned by (none keeps the old reference, or no view or step names it there), a move in `baseline` and a move in a workspace with no plateau are written at once, as they were.

The badges and colours of styled stereotypes, on the boxes and in the tree, are explained in <<stereotypes-and-styles>>.

Changes stay in memory, with a mark on the tree for each modified element, until *Save all changes* writes them. A file that changed on disk since the editor read it is not overwritten: the save is refused and says which one. A file that is saved keeps what the changes did not touch: its comments, its blank lines, the quotes of the values, its final line break and, for a file that starts with one, its UTF-8 byte-order mark (`.archicode.yaml` as well as the manifests; a browser never hands the mark to the page, so the editor reads the first three bytes of the file to know it, and puts the mark back when it writes; a file the editor creates has none). Only the lines that changed are rewritten, and a value written anew (a renamed id, a new parent, a reference that follows, a `replaces`) keeps the quoting of the line it replaces, the way its key is written and its trailing comment; a line that is added is written like the lines around it (double quotes, single quotes or none, as the file does), and plain when the file quotes nothing. A value that comes back on a line that is new (a map in brackets that becomes a list of maps, an item that moves, a list that gives back its item) keeps the quotes it had, and so does a key; a comment after a line stays on the line of its own key or item when that line is written again, and removing the first of two items that read the same removes the first, with its comment. A comment on a line of its own between two lines of an item that is removed stays where it was. A list or a map written in square or curly brackets (`replaces: [a, b]`, `tags: {zone: north}`, on one line or on several, nested, as an item of a list of maps) stays as written when the change does not reach it; when a change reaches it, it is written in brackets again, on the line where it started: `qualifiers: [a, b]` that gets a `c` is `qualifiers: [a, b, c]`, a map in curly brackets that gets a key stays a map in curly brackets, and so does an item of a list of maps (`- {destination: x}`). The comment after the closing bracket, the quotes of the values and of the keys, the words of the booleans (`on`, `yes`) and the spacing inside the brackets (`[ a ]` or `[a]`) are the ones you wrote, except for a value the change sets; a collection that you had over several lines goes on one line when it fits, and one that a change empties is `[]` or `{}`. It fits when its line, the comment included, is no wider than the widest line of the file, but at least 100 and at most 120 columns, or no wider than it already was. A collection stays a block, as the editor has always written it, in four cases: it no longer fits; it has a comment inside its brackets (the comments stay as lines of the block, a comment above a value above it, a comment after a value at the end of its line); it is an empty `[]` or `{}` that the change fills (the emitter's own spelling for nothing, which a new element or story has); or it has an anchor, an alias or a tag in it. A collection that is a block in the file is never turned into brackets. The same words (`yes`, `no`, `on`, `off`, `~`) and a key with no value (`description:`) stay as written in the lines the change does not reach, and a comment or a blank line above a line that is rewritten stays above it. A list written at the same column as its key (`tags:` then `- a` at the same column) is followed too: its items and comments stay, and an item that is added is written at the column the list uses; the comment after `[]`, `{}` or a key with no value stays on the line when a change fills the collection, and the comments inside empty brackets stay under the key. A manifest that uses a YAML anchor, an alias or a tag is read by the editor and by the command line the same way: an alias (`description: *n`) is the value of the anchor it points at (`name: &n "Bakery"`), not its name, and a tag qualifies a scalar (`!!str 12` is the text `12`; `!!int`, `!!float`, `!!bool` and `!!null` read it as such, any other tag as a text; a tagged number is read as the command line reads it: `!!int 0x1F` is `31`, `!!int 1:30` stays the text `1:30` and `!!binary aGVsbG8=` stays the text `aGVsbG8=`). A merge key (`+<<: *base+`) is not a merge: it is a key named `+<<+`. The anchor, the alias and the tag stay as written in the lines a change does not touch, and a line a change rewrites keeps its anchor and its tag; an alias stays an alias as long as the line still says what its anchor says, and is written as its value when a change makes them differ. An anchor, an alias or a tag inside square or curly brackets (`qualifiers: [&a oven, *a]`), a text that goes over several lines (tagged or not) and a list item whose map starts on the line under its dash (`-`, then its keys, with or without an anchor) stay as written as well: every line a change does not reach is the line of the file, byte for byte. A collection in brackets that a change reaches is written as a block that keeps its anchors, aliases and tags; an anchored value that a change edits keeps its anchor, and each alias of it is written as the value it had, since it would now say the new one. An anchor or a tag on a key (`&k name: x`) is read by the command line and refused by the editor, which does not open the file and says so (see <<plateau-limits>>). A few layouts are still written again entirely in the editor's own layout, with the comments lost and the aliases written as their values, and <<plateau-limits>> lists them; so is a file laid out in a way the editor does not write (an indentation other than two spaces, for example).

[[editor-map]]
=== Applications and Environments: the Map

The *Applications* page, which the editor called the *Map* until wave 014, draws the whole application layer, with no element selected, and the *Environments* page draws the whole technology layer the same way, with the same tools: one Map for each layer. The tree on the left of each lists the elements of its layer only (*Environments* in the title of the tree of the technology Map), the pins, hidden elements and open boxes of a layer are that Map's own (a reference can exist in both layers, and a pin of one means nothing in the other), and a technology element is the pin of the Map of its layer. A Map opens folded: only the top of the layer shows (the solutions and systems, or the environments), and a link is drawn between the boxes its two ends land on, so opening a box splits the links that reached it among its children.

In the tree on the left, each row has two buttons, which appear when the pointer is over the row or the row has the focus:

- *Pin* (the pin) keeps only what touches the element. Pin several to keep what touches any of them. With nothing pinned, the whole layer is drawn.
- *Hide* (the eye) leaves the element, what is inside it and their relationships out of the map.

The pinned and hidden elements are also shown as chips over the map, each removable, with *Clear all* once there are more than two. In the tree, rows of the elements the map does not draw are greyed. Folding the tree does not change the boxes of the map: use *Fold all* and *Unfold all* in the toolbar of the map, or the buttons of the boxes. *Hide unlinked* leaves out the boxes that no link reaches; it is in the toolbar of the diagrams of Elements too, where the element itself always stays. It is in the URL (`hide-unlinked=1`) and in a saved view. A saved view of a Map keeps the layer it draws, and the command line draws it too (see <<map-viewpoint>>).

[[editor-map-links]]
*Drawing some relationships only.* Under the tree of the page Applications, a section *Relationships* lists the relationships of the plateau drawn (and, in a comparison, those of the plateau compared with, so that a removed one can be ticked), once each, sorted by source, destination and label. Tick some to draw only them, with the boxes at their two ends and what encloses them, as the `links` of a Map do (see <<map-links>>); with nothing ticked the whole map is drawn. A field filters the list by source, destination, kind or text, a list limits it to a kind, *Ticked only* keeps the ticked rows, and *Clear* (or the button of the chip *Relationships: n* over the diagram) ticks nothing again. When a link of the diagram is selected, its panel lists the relationships it stands for, each with a check box, and, while nothing is ticked, a button *Draw only this link*. The choice is view state, not a change of the workspace: it is in the address (`rel=`, once for each relationship, a JSON list of the source, the destination and the label when there is one; a relationship that no model has is dropped), and a saved view of the Map keeps it as its `links`, as the other choices of the Map are kept, written as the command line reads them. The Map of the technology layer has no such section and refuses `links`. When an element that a ticked relationship names is renamed or moved, the entry follows it in the saved views and in the steps of the stories.

*Highlighting a relationship.* The panel of a selected link (see <<editor-inspection>>) has a *Highlight* box beside *Drawn*, for each relationship it lists, and the chip over the diagram says how many are highlighted (*Highlight: n*, with a cross that draws every relationship calmly again; *Clear all* does the same). A highlighted relationship is drawn heavy, with a `▶` before its caption and the caption in bold; the other links of the map are drawn calm, thinner, lighter and grey, so that the colour is not the only sign. The highlight is kept in the address as `hl=` (one parameter for each entry, as `rel=` is for the ticked relationships), and a relationship that the drawing does not show (hidden, inside a closed box, or left out by the ticked ones) marks nothing and stays in the list. The Map of the technology layer has no highlight, and *Keep as named view* keeps the highlight of the diagram as it keeps the ticked relationships (see <<editor-stories>>).

*What Environments draws besides the technology elements.* The Map of the technology layer also draws the applications deployed on the nodes, a box each inside its node, and the relationships: the ones the technology elements declare, and the dependencies between environments that the deployed applications give (an application of an environment that depends on an application hosted in an environment or a node that the first `uses`). Both are what the command line draws for the same view (see <<map-technology>>, which says exactly which). A node that hosts applications can be opened, with its chevron or *Unfold all*, and only an open node shows them, so the Map opens as folded as before. A click on an application box selects the application, in the application layer: the panel on the right is the one of that application. An application is not pinned, hidden or opened on its own: pin or hide the node. The group *Applications* and *Relationships* in the tools of the diagram turns each of the two parts off and on (a button is lit while its part is drawn): *Applications* off leaves the application boxes out and the links between the nodes that host them stay, *Relationships* off leaves every link out. The choice is in the URL, as what is left out only (`applications=0`, `relationships=0`), and it is what a saved Map view says in its `properties` (`applications: false`, `relationships: false`; the same two buttons, on the page of a saved view of the technology Map, edit the view like the texts do). It is a choice of the view, not of the model: it writes nothing to the workspace, and it is not kept when you go to another page. On the Map of the application layer there is no such group.

*Center* puts the selected box in the middle at a readable size (with nothing selected on the Map: the top of the map, centered across), never below 80%, and *Fit* shows the whole diagram, however small. Fit is in the URL (`frame=fit`) and Center, how a diagram opens, is a URL with no `frame`, so a link opens the diagram framed as you left it; a zoom or a move of your own is not part of the link. *PNG* and *SVG*, in the header, download the diagram.

[[editor-inspection]]
=== The inspection panel

Beside a diagram, on the right, a panel shows the data of the selected element without leaving the view: its name and styled stereotypes, its kind, its layer, its id and reference, its description, qualifiers and tags, and the file that declares it. It is there on the *Map* (Applications and Environments), on the *Overview*, *Detailed* and *Deep* tabs of Elements (where a click on a box moves to that element, and the panel follows) and on the page of a view in Stories (where a click on a box selects it on the page only, see <<editor-stories>>); it is not on *Details* and in the edit form, which show the same things themselves. Nothing is shown, and no room is kept, while nothing is selected. The panel is resized by dragging its edge (the width is remembered; a double click on the edge gives the default back), it goes away with *Maximize*, and on the Map its *x* button deselects the element. On the Map the panel says so when the selected element is not drawn (a pin, a hidden element or a folded box leaves it out), and when it is an element of the other layer, which the Map never draws (*This map draws the technology layer only.* on Environments, *The map draws applications only.* on Applications). A click does what it did before: on the Map it selects, in Elements it moves to the element.

A click on a link of the diagram, or on its caption, selects it instead, and the panel lists each relationship the link stands for: its label, its qualifiers and its tags (`tcp-port`, `doc-exchange`...), with the element that declares it (a button: it selects that element, in the layer of the drawing, as a click on its box does) and the one it points at. What is selected is the pair of boxes the link joins, so a link that groups several relationships lists them all (and says so), a link between folded boxes names the folded end and how many elements it hides (open it to see the relationships one by one), and a link to an unknown element says it points at nothing. The links of that pair are outlined. When several links join the same two boxes, the panel says how many, and marks (with the words _Clicked link_) the relationships of the one you clicked, unless they cannot be told from the others'. The links of a diagram are one stop of the Tab key, however many there are, and so are its boxes (Tab goes from one group to the other): once on a link, the arrow keys move to the next and the previous link, in the order the diagram draws them, and Home and End go to the first and the last. Enter or Space selects the link that has the focus. The stop is where the focus last was among the links, or else the selected link, or else the first one; for the boxes it is where the focus last was, or else the selected box, or else the one in focus of the diagram, or else the first one. *x* or Escape deselects: on the Map the panel goes, in Elements the panel of the element comes back. Selecting a link changes neither the perspective nor the tab, and in Elements the element stays the one being looked at (the tree, the tabs and the URL keep it). On the Map, if the panel that appears leaves the link behind its edge, the view moves by the least it takes to show it, once; a link already in view does not move the view. The selected link is in the URL, after the other parameters of the view (`link-from=customer&link-to=shop`: the two boxes of the drawing), so a copied URL, a reload and Back or Forward bring it back, and bring the view to it if it is not in sight. Selecting a link rewrites the current entry of the history, like a zoom, and does not add one. A link the drawing does not have when the URL is opened (it was renamed, or the boxes it joins are folded differently, which the URL does not carry) is dropped, and the URL with it. The selection ends as soon as the diagram it was made on is no longer the one on screen (another perspective, Details, another element, or a drawing that no longer has the link: a fold, a pin, a facet). A click on a box does what it did before and ends the link selection. A relationship the diagram does not draw (secondary links between deployed applications, unless a facet asks for them) cannot be selected.

Since the panel takes room from the center, the header of the diagram goes to its narrower states sooner (see <<editor-header>>): resize or close the panel to give it back.

[[editor-header]]
=== The header of a diagram

The header over a diagram, on the tabs of Elements and on the Map, adapts to the width of the center panel, which is the window minus the left panel, so dragging the left panel changes it. From wide to narrow:

- *Wide*: every control shows its words.
- *Narrower*: *Show on map* (or *Open in Elements* on the Map), *Name view...*, *Add to story...* and *Maximize* show only their icon; the tooltip says what each one does.
- *Narrower still*: *Show on map* or *Open in Elements*, the *Story* buttons and the *Download* buttons (PNG, SVG) move into the menu of a "⋯" button, which closes with Escape or a click elsewhere. The facet list and *Maximize* stay in the header. The menu items work exactly like the buttons they replace.
- *Very narrow*: the header takes two rows.
- *The key of a comparison* is one row that scrolls along under 700 pixels (see <<editor-compare>>).

[[editor-small-screen]]
=== The editor on a small screen

In a window narrower than 40 rem (640 pixels at the usual size) the regions of the editor are stacked one under the other, and the page scrolls from one to the next. It was measured in a desktop browser (the browser pane of the development tool), at 360 by 800 pixels, in the light and in the dark theme, on the layered Brightcart and on the example `domain_application`, with a script that walks every page and form and compares the right edge of every box with the width of the window, and the screenshots of the pages were looked at:

[cols="2,1",options="header"]
|===
| What | Measured
| the top bar | 105 pixels high
| the band of the plateaus (the pages of Plateaus, Applications, Environments and Elements) | 80 pixels, two lists of one line each
| the tabs of the pages | 39 pixels
| the tree / the center / the inspection | up to 240 / 640 / up to 400 pixels (30, 80 and 50 percent of the height of an 800 pixel window)
| the width of the document, on Plateaus, Applications, Environments, Elements, Stories, Files and Settings | 360 pixels on all seven
| the widest control of Settings, the list _Current plateau_ included | ends at 332 pixels
| the story player (below), in a window 360 pixels wide, dark scheme only | the page is 360 pixels wide, no horizontal scroll
|===

The section *Relationships* of the page Applications (see <<editor-map-links>>) wraps its filter, its kind list and *Ticked only* in the width of the tree, which is stacked above the diagram in this layout, and its long references wrap inside the rows. The controls of the highlight (the *Highlight* boxes, their chip, and the *Highlight* fields of a saved view and of a story step, see <<editor-map-links>> and <<editor-stories>>) came with run 0200 and are not in the table above.

Nothing is wider than the window, on the seven pages, on 52 states of the Brightcart in each theme (the forms of ten elements in view, edit, _Move_ and delete, three stories, seven views) and on 20 of `domain_application`; the diagram is drawn to scale and scrolls in its own box, and the code editor of Files scrolls inside itself, as they do on a large screen. Four places did go past the window inside the scroll of the center and are fixed: the row that adds a tag, the four buttons in the header of an element, the list of saved views of a story, and the lists of the references in _Confirm delete_. The list of the places not offered in the dialog of _Move_ has text wider than its box and scrolls inside it.

The walk row of the *Changes* panel (see <<editor-changes-panel>>) is sticky at this width too: *Previous change*, *Next change*, the position and *Show all* stay under the tabs while the list scrolls, and a press brings the selected row into view with the nearest scroll (`block: nearest`) that shows it. The row's height was measured at 360 by 640 pixels, as a share of the panel's height, against the 35 percent limit of the design, and the figure is in the record of the change (run 0201). Safari was not tested, so whether the row sticks the same way there is not proved.

The story player (see <<editor-story-player>>) is not a stacked layout either: it is a window of its own, as high and as wide as the browser window, and nothing in it makes the page scroll sideways. Its bar (the title, "Step n of N", the list of the steps, *Previous*, *Next*, *Full screen* and *Close*) wraps onto as many lines as it needs (four at 360 pixels), the plateau and the comparison are one line under it, the diagram takes the room that is left and is moved with a finger or the mouse inside its own box, the status line and the folded key under a comparison are one row that scrolls along, and the words of the step scroll in their own box. It was looked at in a desktop browser (the browser pane of the development tool), on the example `domain_plateaus`, in a window 360 pixels wide in the dark scheme, and the page was as wide as the window (`scrollWidth` 360), the diagram, the status and the words readable, zoom, *Fit*, *Center* and the wheel moving the drawing, and the open key capped and scrolling inside itself; the wide window in the light scheme was looked at as well, the same way (the first build of the player, before the cap on the key went from 30 to 24 percent of the height). The player is tested with a fake layout in the automated tests, which do not see pixels.

What this does not say: whether the order of the stacked regions and their heights suit a phone, whether the buttons are easy to touch, and how the on-screen keyboard behaves. They are a judgment on a real device, which nobody has made and which is not planned (see <<plateau-limits>>). The same goes for the player: nobody has tried a finger on its diagram, its list of the steps (a native list, so that the phone shows its own picker), *Full screen* on a phone, a screen reader on the announcement of a step, or the player in a folder picked in the browser (the File System Access path; a served folder was tried). *Full screen* itself was not seen working in a real browser: the pane used for the look refuses the request, so the grant is tested in the automated tests only.

[[editor-texts]]
=== The texts of a diagram

Four buttons in the toolbar of a diagram, in the group *Show*, choose what the boxes and the links say. A lit button is a text that is drawn:

- *Labels*: the caption of each link, with its qualifiers in brackets. A long caption does not run over the boxes next to it: it is broken at the spaces into lines of at most 220 pixels (about forty characters at the size of the diagram), centred on the link, and the layout leaves room for all the lines. A word that is wider than a line is cut with an ellipsis, as a name is. The qualifiers are kept in one piece as far as possible, at the end of the last line when they fit and on a line of their own when they do not: the label gives way to them rather than the other way round. They are cut only past two lines without *Wrap*, or when a single qualifier is wider than a line (then its closing bracket is kept). Without *Wrap* a caption has at most four lines, and an ellipsis replaces the end of the label. The caption is broken whether *Wrap* is lit or not, and the tooltip and the accessible name of the link always have all of it. The command line breaks the captions of its PlantUML views too, in narrower lines (PlantUML's own `wrapWidth` of 200).
- *Qualifiers*: the qualifiers of each element, after its kind, as the command line writes them: `[container: rest, public]`.
- *Descriptions*: the description of each element that does not contain others, under its kind, up to three lines.
- *Wrap*: long names, qualifiers and descriptions are broken over several lines, and the box gets taller, instead of being cut with an ellipsis. A box that contains others cuts the qualifiers of its header at any rate. The captions of the links, which are always broken, then show all their lines instead of four at most.

The diagram of an element starts with the labels and the qualifiers, as the command line draws them. The Map starts with none of them, to stay readable. Only a choice that differs from that is remembered: it is in the URL (`labels=0`, `qualifiers=1`, `descriptions=1`, `wrap=1`), and in a saved view (see <<editor-stories>>), so that a link or a step of a story draws the same texts. The tooltip of a box always has the qualifiers and the whole description, and the SVG that is downloaded is the diagram as it is on screen.

[[editor-stories]]
=== Stories: stories and views

The *Stories* perspective, which the editor called the *Library* until wave 014, keeps what the workspace says about itself, on two sides: *Stories* (see <<stories>>) and *Views*, the named diagrams that the stories are made of. Each side has its list on the left and its page in the center, and they lead to one another: the view of a step is a link to that view, and a view lists the steps that show it. The URL says which side is open (`#/stories` and `#/stories/views`; the former `#/library/stories` and `#/library/views` still open them). The page lists the same stories and the same views whatever plateau is drawn: a story and a view belong to no plateau (see <<plateau-untied>>), so there is no list per plateau and no note about other plateaus, and a story or a view is edited, renamed and deleted from any plateau. A new story or view is a manifest in the first folder of `manifests.paths`, with no `plateau:` in its header, in a workspace of plateaus as in one without. A diagram on the Applications page, on the Environments page or in Elements has two buttons in its header:

- *Name view...* keeps the diagram as a view, in a file of its own (`view.<id>.yaml`): the element and the viewpoint (Elements), or what is pinned, hidden and open, and the layer (Map), with the facet, *Hide unlinked*, the relationships ticked and highlighted of a Map of the application layer (`links` and `highlight`, see <<editor-map-links>>) and the texts it draws (and, for the Map of the technology layer, `applications: false` and `relationships: false` when one of them is turned off). It keeps the recipe, not a picture: the view is drawn again from the model each time. A named view can be put in several stories, and generated by the command line.
- *Add to story...* puts the diagram in a story, as a step after the one being told, or at the end. A dialog asks which story (a list to pick from, or the name of a new one: a name that is already a story's is that story). The step carries its own view (no extra file), unless the diagram is one that was named: then it points at it. In a workspace that has plateaus the dialog has a box that keeps the plateaus in the step: _Tell the step at `to-be`, compared with `baseline`_. It is checked when a comparison is on screen, and not otherwise. Checked, the new step is written with `at` (the plateau drawn) and `against` (the plateau compared with), as the plateau ids they are now: a comparison with the _Past_, which follows the plateau drawn, is written as the plateau that it is at that moment, and the dialog says so. Unchecked, the step has no plateau key and is drawn as the reader has it, as before. Whatever the box, a highlight of the diagram is written as the step's own `highlight` key, when there is one, and the relationships ticked stay in the recipe's `properties.links` (see <<story-of-a-flow>>). A comparison that has no plateau to name (the plateau drawn itself, a plateau that is gone) leaves `against` out, and the dialog says why; plateaus that cannot be drawn offer no box. A workspace with no plateau has no box.

*Show on diagram*, on a step of a story on the Stories side, draws that step on the editing diagram, on the page that shows its view, and raises a bar over the diagram that says where you are: the title of the story, "Step n of N", the plateau and the comparison the step names, and the words of the step, to read. *Edit text* turns the words into a box to type in (it opens by itself right after *Add to story...*, so that you can write as you go), *Edit story* goes to the story, *Back to this step* draws the saved view again when you moved away from it, and *Leave story* closes the bar. The bar has no *Previous*, *Next* or list of the steps: press the *Show on diagram* of another step, or play the story (below). A step that cannot be drawn (its element was deleted) says why in the bar, which stays, over the diagram that was there. The story is in the URL (`story=` and `step=`), so a link or Back lands on the same step.

[[editor-story-player]]
*Playing a story.* *Play*, in the header of a story on the Stories side (off for a story that has no step), opens the story at its first step in a window of its own, on top of the editor, to be read like slides. The window covers the browser window and the editor behind it cannot be reached while it is open: the focus stays inside (Tab and Shift+Tab go round its controls), and closing it gives the focus back to the *Play* that opened it. It is a dialog with the story as its name, and each step is said to a screen reader when it changes, through a live region that nothing redraws: "Step n of N", then the name of the view, then _cannot be shown_ and the reason when it cannot, and, for a step that highlights a relationship, _Highlighted_ with its two ends and its label (`. Highlighted: <source> to <destination>, <label>.`).

The slide holds, in a bar, the title of the story, "Step n of N" with the name of the view, the list of the steps (an entry for each, its number and a title; a story of one step has no list), *Previous* and *Next*, *Full screen* and *Close*. In a workspace that has plateaus a line under it says the plateau the step is drawn at and, when it has one, the plateau it is compared with: it is said, not chosen, and when the step names none it says _(from the editor)_, since a step that names none is drawn at the plateau, and with the comparison, that the editor had when *Play* was pressed, which stay that way for as long as the window is open. Under the line comes the diagram, which can be zoomed (the buttons, or Ctrl or Cmd with the wheel, around the pointer), moved (a drag, or the wheel) and put back by *Fit* and *Center*, but not clicked: a click on a box or a link selects nothing and opens no panel. A step opens at a readable size, from the top of its drawing. Under the diagram are the status line, the key of the comparison when one is drawn (folded, *Key* opens it and a long one scrolls inside itself) and the words of the step. A step that cannot be drawn (its element is not in its plateau, its plateau is none, it is compared with itself) says why where the diagram would be, and the way on stays open: the list, *Previous* and *Next* still work, and the entry of that step in the list says _cannot be shown_ and the reason. The bar of *Show on diagram* says the same with _cannot be shown_.

Each step is drawn from the model of its own plateau, on the side, with its highlight: the step's own `highlight` replaces the view's, and an empty one switches it off, so that the heavy link and the calm links are those of the step (see <<story-of-a-flow>>).
