The player reads the editor (the model, the story, the plateau and comparison it had when *Play* was pressed) and writes nothing to it: when the window closes, the plateau drawn, the comparison, the selection, the page, the address and the files are what they were, and nothing is marked as changed. The *Changes only* and wording switches of the editor are not read; a comparison is drawn with all its boxes.

*Full screen* asks the browser to give the whole screen to the window. A browser that has no full screen does not show the button; one that refuses leaves the window as it is and says _The browser did not give full screen._ under the bar; closing the window leaves full screen.

The keys are the arrow keys: Left and Right show the previous and the next step, `Home` and `End` the first and the last, and `Esc` closes the window, from the list of the steps too (the list owns the arrows, `Home` and `End`, and lets `Esc` through). Up and Down are not taken, so that nothing competes with the scroll of a long text. No other key acts while a control that types or owns the arrows has the focus (a field, a text box, a list), while a dialog, the help or the menu of the header is open (then `Esc` is theirs), or with Ctrl, Alt, Meta or Shift held; `J` and `K` of the changes do nothing in the window, which has no list of changes.

The window is in the address, as `play=<story>&step=n` after the keys of the editor, `n` counted from 0 as `step=` is. Opening it is an entry of the history; choosing another step is not (the entry is changed in place), so Back closes the window and Forward opens it again at the step it was left on; *Close* and `Esc` go Back when the history holds that entry, so that Forward brings it back. A link with `play=` opens it when the folder is loaded, for someone who has the folder open (`archicode editor serve`, or a folder picked in the browser), at the plateau the address names; it is then no entry of the history, and closing it writes the address in place. The `story=` and `step=` of the bar are not in the address while the window is open, so there is one `step=`. A story that is not there, a story with no step, a step that is missing, is not a number or does not exist, and a workspace that has no story leave the window closed, and the page writes the address it really is. It is not a page that exists outside the editor: the editor reads a folder, so a link opens nothing for someone who has none, and there is no export of a story as a document. The player reads; to change a story, *Edit story*, the page of a story and the story editor are as they were.

*A step told at a plateau.* A step that says `at` or `against` (see <<story-step-plateaus>>) is drawn at its plateau and with its comparison in the player (above), without the editor changing. With *Show on diagram* it draws its plateau and its comparison before it draws its view, in the whole editor: the band, the *Changes* panel and the diagram are the ones of that plateau, and it is the step, not the plateau, that is an entry of the history. The bar says _told at To be, compared with As is_. With *Show on diagram*, a step that names none is drawn as the reader had it when the story began; a plateau or a comparison that the reader chooses on the band in the middle of the story is the new home of the steps that name none. *Back to this step* is offered when the plateau or the comparison on screen is not the step's, and *Leave story* closes the bar and changes nothing: the diagram stays as it is. A name that the plateau of the step lacks is no mistake of the story: the step says _The element “x” is not in the plateau “p”._ and cannot be drawn. A step whose plateau is none, or cannot be drawn, changes nothing, says why in the bar, and stays.

On the Stories side, a story is open as soon as there is one, and edited in place: its name, its text, the order of its steps (*Up*, *Down*, *Remove*, which can be undone right away) and the words of each step. Each step also has a *Highlight* field, which edits its own `highlight` key: the relationships it highlights, as pills, and a list to add one. A note under it says that the key replaces the highlight of the view and that an empty list switches it off. Removing the last one leaves `highlight: []` only when the view the step names has a highlight to switch off, and otherwise deletes the key. In a workspace that has plateaus, each step has two lists, *Told at* (the plateau drawn, by default) and *Compared with* (nothing, by default): a choice that is the default takes the key out of the step, a plateau that is none is listed and flagged _(not a plateau)_ so that it is not lost, and *Keep as named view* keeps the two keys with the step. The step that is showing on the diagrams is marked. *Play* opens the story in the player, from its first step (see <<editor-story-player>>), and *Show on diagram* draws one step on the editing diagram. A step that carries its own view says so, and *Keep as named view* gives it a name and a file; a step whose view was deleted offers to point at another one. Renaming an element updates the views and steps that point at it. A change that is not saved yet is marked *modified*.

On the Views side, the page of a view that can be drawn draws it in the center, from the model, with the canvas and the tools of the Map and Elements: zoom, *Center*, *Fit*, *Hide unlinked*, the four texts (see <<editor-texts>>), and *PNG* and *SVG* in the header. The facet of the view is applied: it is drawn here, not picked. There is no folding on this page, since the open boxes of a Map view are part of the view. The zoom and the moves are the page's own and are not changes to the view, and the page changes nothing the Map and Elements show (selection, pins, tab, facet, texts, address). The header keeps *Open on diagram*, which draws the view on the Map of its layer or in Elements to explore it with their tools, *Rename* and *Delete*, and has *Maximize*, which hides the list of views and the right-hand panel (`M` maximizes, `Esc` restores; the Map is not touched).

The panel on the right, resized and hidden as the one of the Map (see <<editor-inspection>>), has the view first (while a plateau is compared, it has two tabs, *View* and *Changes*, see <<editor-changes-panel>>): what it draws and the file that keeps it, then a field for each attribute of the view, and the steps of the stories that show it, each a link to its story. Once something is selected, the inspection of the selection comes under it; the panel stays where it is when the top of the inspection is in sight, and otherwise scrolls by the least it takes to show its first lines, so that the fields of the view stay in sight as long as there is room. A click on a box, or Enter on the box that has the focus, selects the element and shows its panel, which has a *Show in Elements* button: it leaves the page and selects the element there, on the tab Elements was left on. A click on a link or on its caption shows the relationships it stands for, and the elements named in that panel select on the page too. The selected box is outlined, and *x* or `Esc` deselects. A box for a relationship that points at nothing cannot be clicked here. This selection is the page's own: it does not select anything on the Map or in Elements, it is forgotten when another view is shown, and it is not in the URL, which carries no more than `#/stories/views?view=<id>` (and the plateau).

[[editor-view-edit]]
A view is changed by its fields, and the diagram follows at once:

- *Viewpoint*: *Overview*, *Detailed*, *Deep* or *Map*. From an element viewpoint to the Map, the element becomes the pinned element, in the layer the view has (a technology element is the pin of the Map of the technology layer); from the Map to an element viewpoint, the first pinned element that still exists becomes the element, and the hidden and open elements are dropped; the layer stays. What the view showed of the texts is kept. *Cancel* gives the change up as long as it has not been saved.
- *Layer* (every viewpoint: a Map draws the layer it names) and *Element* (the element viewpoints): the element is typed, with the references of the layer as suggestions, and applied with `Enter` or by leaving the field. A reference that is not an element of the layer is refused, with the reason under the field, and stays there to be corrected. Changing the layer keeps the element when the other layer has it, and otherwise leaves the view without one: it then says so and cannot be drawn until one is chosen; for a Map it keeps the entries of its three lists that exist in the other layer.
- *Pinned*, *Hidden* and *Open* (the Map): each is a list of elements of the layer of the view (an element of the other layer is refused), with a cross to take one out and a field to add one (`Enter`). The drawing of this page is not edited itself, on purpose: a click on a box selects it. To choose pins by clicking, use *Open on diagram*.
- *Links* and *Highlight* (the Map of the application layer): each is a list of relationships of the layer, a pill for each entry and a select of the relationships not there yet, with a missing one marked as such. They are `properties.links` and `properties.highlight` (see <<editor-map-links>> and <<story-of-a-flow>>).
- *Facet*: the facets of the workspace, or none. *Hide unlinked*, *Labels*, *Qualifiers*, *Descriptions* and *Wrap* are boxes to tick (a Map of the technology layer has two more, *Applications* and *Relationships*, ticked when the property is absent: unticking one writes `false`, see <<map-technology>>), and they are the same as the buttons of the tools over the diagram. A text that is what that kind of diagram draws by default is not written to the file, as for any view.

Nothing is written to the disk by an edit. The view gets the mark *modified*, in the header and in the list, and loses it when what you did is put back, as soon as the file of the view would be the one on disk (the last one written); *Save all changes* writes the file of the view and no other (the comments and the layout of the file stay, as for any manifest; a property that an edit takes out and puts back again is written where the editor writes it, element, facet, *Hide unlinked*, then the texts), and *Cancel* gives every unsaved change up: the view, and the diagram with it, are the saved ones again. A view that an edit left impossible to draw (no element, nothing that was pinned exists) says why in the center, with the same fields to correct it, and can still be saved. Renaming an element in Elements still rewrites the views that point at it; the steps of the stories point at the view, so they show the edited view, saved or not.

A view that cannot be drawn (its element was deleted, nothing that was pinned exists any more, the viewpoint is unknown) keeps its message, what it draws, its fields and its steps in the center of the page, with no diagram and no panel.

A story is a file (`story.<id>.yaml`) in the first manifests folder, and so is each named view. They wait in memory, like the other changes, with a mark on the file, for *Save all changes*, and *Cancel* gives them up: only the files that changed are written. When an older workspace still has views or stories in `.archicode.yaml`, Stories says so, on both sides, with *Move to manifests*, which works whatever plateau is drawn.

[[editor-facets]]
=== Facets

The facets of the workspace (see <<facets>>) are in a list in the header of the diagrams: the diagram is then drawn as the command line generates it for that facet, with the relationships the facet removes left out, and the elements it removes, with the relationships that pointed at them. In the tree, the elements the facet leaves with nothing to draw are greyed. A facet that the kernel refuses (an old path of the previous engine, an invalid JSONPath) shows its sentence as the error of the view. The facets themselves are edited in the *Settings* perspective, along with the other settings of the workspace (see <<technical-configuration>>). *Add facet* there refuses a name that cannot be the name of a folder (`a/b`, `..`, a name that ends with a dot), with the reason under the field, as the command line refuses it when it loads the workspace (see <<facets>>).

[[editor-files]]
=== Files

*Files* lists every manifest of the workspace on the left, and the text of the one that is open in the center, which can be edited (see <<editor-reads>> for what the editor reads). Whatever plateau is drawn, the list holds every file: there is no choice of what it shows. A workspace that has layers lists them in groups.

*A group for each plateau.* The heading of a group says the plateau, its name and the number of its files. The groups come in the order of the tree, the root first and then each plateau under the one it overrides. The plateau of a manifest is the one its header names (`plateau: saas`), `baseline` when the header names none, and, for a manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau`, the plateau it declares. A group that holds no file has no heading. A workspace with no layer lists its files as it always did, with no group and nothing to fold.

*Fold and unfold.* A click on the heading of a group folds and unfolds it (Enter and Space do the same), and *Fold all* and *Unfold all* do every group. A folded group still tells, in its heading, that one of its files has an _error_ or is _modified_. Opening a file, by its row, by an address or by a link of a message, unfolds its group; a fold that you make afterwards is kept. The folds are the page's own: they last as long as the editor stays open on the same folder, they are not written in the workspace, and they do not make a file _modified_.

*Files that belong to no plateau.* The last group, *No plateau*, holds the files that belong to no plateau: the views and the stories (they are declared in no plateau, see <<plateau-untied>>) and a file the editor could not read, which is always listed, with its error. `.archicode.yaml` is not in the list: it is the page *Settings*.

*A plateau that disagrees with the folder.* The folder of a manifest does not matter to the tools: the plateau is what the header says. The folders of a project are often named after its plateaus, though, and a file moved from one to the other with its header left alone is read as the plateau of its header, not of its folder. When a manifest sits in a folder that is named like another plateau of the workspace (the deepest such folder counts), its row has a _folder_ mark, a note above the groups counts such files (_N files sit in the folder of another plateau_), and its page has a warning: `a/x.yaml` says `plateau: b` in its header but sits in the folder `a`; `b/y.yaml` has no `plateau` key, so it belongs to `baseline`, but sits in the folder `b`. It is only a warning: nothing is refused, the command line says nothing, and the file is where you put it. A folder that is named like no plateau says nothing.

[[editor-workspace-name]]
=== Name of the workspace

A workspace can have a name, the optional `name` key of its `settings` (see <<workspace>>). *Settings* has a section, *Workspace*, with a *Name* field. Unlike the theme below, the name is a setting of the workspace: it is written to `.archicode.yaml`, so it counts as a change to save and lights *Save all changes*.

When the workspace has a name, the editor shows it at the top left of the window, in place of the name of the folder, in the title of the page, in the <<editor-folders,list of remembered folders>> and in the "Opening" messages, which use the name the workspace had when its folder was last opened; the name of the folder is then the tooltip of the name. Without a name, or with a blank one, the editor shows the name of the folder, as before, and an emptied *Name* is left out of the file.

[[editor-current-plateau]]
=== The current plateau

*Settings*, in its section *Workspace*, has a field *Current plateau* when the workspace has at least two plateaus (or when `.archicode.yaml` already says `current`): a list of the plateaus, in the order of the band, and _None_. It writes `settings.current` into `.archicode.yaml`, the plateau the command line draws when `--plateau` is not given, and the plateau the editor opens the folder on when nothing else says which: no address with `plateau=`, and no plateau that this browser drew last in this folder. A file that has no `settings` gets one with that line alone; _None_ takes the line out (and leaves `settings: {}` when it was the only one). A name that is no plateau is refused with the sentence of the load. Like the other fields of *Settings* it is a change to save with *Save all changes*, and it is disabled while the plateaus have mistakes (only what the file says and _None_ can be chosen then).

It does not redraw the plateau on screen: what you chose on the band is not undone by a setting for the next time. With _None_, the command line draws the last plateau when the plateaus form one line, and asks for `--plateau` otherwise, while the editor opens the root.

[[editor-band]]
=== The band of plateaus

Under the top bar, on the four pages of Timeline and nowhere else, the band chooses the plateau that is drawn and the one it is compared with. It is lean and read-only: two lists and, when something is wrong, one button. It writes nothing, and it has no tree, no counts and no sentence of its own (the tree and the counts are on the page Plateaus, see <<editor-plateaus>>). The plateau drawn and the comparison are the state of the editor, not of the band: Stories, Files and Settings have no band, and draw the plateau that was chosen. There is no plateau selector, no _Compare to_ list and no section of the Settings for plateaus. A workspace that writes no layer has one plateau, `baseline`. A workspace of layers (a `plateau` key in the header of a manifest, or a manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau`) has one for each plateau.

* *Plateau* draws a plateau in the whole editor. It lists every plateau in the order of the tree, `baseline` first and then what overrides it, each followed by what overrides it and indented by its depth, with its name and its horizon. A plateau that the command line refuses has a `!` in front of its name.
* *Compare with* says what the plateau drawn is compared with. It starts with _Nothing_ and _Past (follows the plateau drawn)_ (the plateau it overrides, whichever that is, so it changes when another plateau is drawn; the root has no past, so no such entry), then shows the same tree as *Plateau*, every plateau in the order of the tree and indented by its depth, with the plateau that is drawn greyed in its place so that it cannot be chosen and the hierarchy reads in both lists. A plateau of the tree is a fixed comparison, as `archicode diff --plateau <drawn> --against <other>` reads it: the parent, a child, a sibling or a cousin alike. There are no entries _Future_ any more: a plateau above the one drawn is chosen in the tree, and is read as the future of the plateau drawn. A list chooses and does not toggle: choosing again the plateau that is compared already does nothing. Choosing the parent in the tree is read as _Past (follows the plateau drawn)_, as the editor has always read a comparison with the parent: the comparison then moves with the plateau drawn. There is no way, on the band, to compare with the parent and have it stay when another plateau is drawn (see <<plateau-limits>>). An address with `compare=future` and several plateaus above the plateau drawn shows a disabled entry, _Choose a future plateau_, selected, with nothing selected in the tree, until one is chosen.
* *! N mistakes*, when there are some, opens the dialog _Mistakes in the plateaus_: why only `baseline` is drawn, the sentences of the plateau drawn and of the plateau compared with that the command line refuses (the sentences of the command line, see <<plateaus>>), the views and the stories that name elements no plateau has, the steps of the stories that name plateaus that are none (see <<plateau-untied>> and <<story-step-plateaus>>), and the sentence that nothing is written in a plateau that cannot be drawn.

*Everything | Changes only* is not on the band: it is among the tools of a diagram, and is shown while a plateau is compared (see <<editor-changes-only>>).

*The order of the siblings.* Plateaus that override the same plateau are placed by their `horizon`, when it reads as a date, and by their id otherwise. A horizon reads as a date when it is a year (`2027`), a month (`2027-03`), a day (`2027-03-18`), a quarter (`2027-Q4`) or a half (`2027-H2`); the date of a quarter or a half is the first day of the period. The plateaus whose horizon reads as a date come first, from the nearest to the farthest, those with the same date by id; the others follow, by id. The rule is the same every time the folder is opened. (`archicode plateau list` orders the siblings by id only, and the editor by horizon: the tree is the same.) (The tree does not need a horizon: it is the `override` of each plateau that places it, the horizon only orders the siblings. Any other text in a `horizon` is a label that no tool interprets.)

*Drawing a plateau.* A choice in *Plateau* draws that plateau, in the whole editor: the trees, Applications, Environments, the diagrams, Stories and the panel on the right show the plateau of the band. The plateau is its chain: the manifests that say `plateau: <id>` are added to the elements of the plateau it overrides, then its patches change them (`REMOVE`, `SET`, `ADD`, `MERGE`, applied by the same kernel as the command line), and every plateau of the chain is checked, with the sentences and in the order of the command line: a patch that selects nothing names the plateau, the rank of the patch and what it selected; a relationship, an entry of `uses` or an application hosted on a node that leads nowhere, a manifest whose `parent` is not there or cannot hold its kind, name the plateau. The views and the stories belong to no plateau: a name that they have and the plateau lacks is told in the view (see <<editor-stories>>), and a name that no plateau has is in the dialog of the mistakes. The choice reads nothing from the disk and loses nothing: the manifests stay as they are, and each plateau is resolved once and kept until a manifest changes. When the kernel refuses the patches of a plateau, that plateau cannot be drawn (the dialog says why, and `baseline` is one choice away); when only the checks fail, the plateau is drawn all the same, flagged `!`, with the sentences that the command line would stop on, so that the mistake can be repaired in it. The plateau that opens is the one the address names (`plateau=`), else the one this browser drew last in this folder, else the one `settings.current` names (a name that is no plateau is an error with the sentence of the command line; the field *Current plateau* of *Settings* writes it, see <<editor-current-plateau>>), else `baseline`. When the plateaus form one line and no `settings.current` says otherwise, the editor therefore opens the root while the command line, with no `--plateau`, draws the last plateau.

*Comparing.* *Compare with* compares the plateau that is drawn with another one. The comparison is the one of <<editor-compare>>: the Map, the diagrams, Stories and the *Changes* panel tell what is new, changed, removed, moved, split or merged, with the same counts and the same table as `archicode diff` for the same two plateaus; *Everything* and *Changes only* choose what the diagrams draw. With the past the plateau drawn is the more recent one, with the future it is the older one, and what only the other plateau has is read in the panel on the right, as a removed element is. The choice is remembered by this browser for this folder.

*Cascade.* Below the lists, the Changes panel has *Cascade of the removals* when a `REMOVE` of the more recent plateau took more than what it selects: the elements that went with the selected one, and the entries taken out of elements that stay (a relationship towards it, an entry of `uses` or of `applications`), among what the other plateau has, as `archicode diff` lists them after the table. *Write replaces* and *Not related* work on the pairs of any two plateaus; *Write replaces* writes `replaces` in the manifest that declares the new element, and refuses an element the plateau only inherits (it is declared in a plateau above, which the key would change).

*Where a value comes from.* In the panel of an element, each value (name, description, qualifiers, tags, relationships, replaces, uses, applications) says where it comes from: _declared in_ the plateau drawn, _inherited from_ another plateau (the one whose manifest declares the element), or _set by patch N_ of a plateau (with the operator and the selector of the patch, and "inherited" when the patch is one of a plateau above), or _taken out by patch N_ when a `REMOVE` with a `field` took the value out (see <<plateau-patches>>). A block *Origin* lists the plateau that declares the element and the patches that selected it.

*Writing.* The plateau that is drawn is the one a write goes to, and the page Plateaus says so (_writing in: Buy the commerce platform_). What an edit writes, and what is refused, is in <<editor-writing>>.

[[editor-writing]]
=== Writing in a plateau

A write goes to the plateau that is drawn, in the files of that plateau, and nowhere else. The page Plateaus says _writing in: <plateau>_ when the workspace has layers. A workspace that writes no layer saves as it always did.

[cols="2,3",options="header"]
|===
| In the plateau drawn | What is written
| a field of an element the plateau declares (its manifest says `plateau: <the plateau>`)
| the field, in the manifest that declares the element, as before
| a field of an element the plateau inherits (name, description, qualifiers, tags, relationships, applications, uses, replaces)
| a patch in the manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau` of the plateau, at the end of its `patches`: `SET` for a value (a list set whole too), `ADD` for items added at the end of a list, `MERGE` for tags that only gain or change keys, `SET` with `where` for one relationship changed (found by its destination and its label), `REMOVE` with a `field` for a text or a number that is cleared (below)
| the same field again
| the patch the plateau already has on that element and that field is changed (a `REMOVE` becomes a `SET` when you write a new text, a `SET` becomes a `REMOVE` when you clear it), and taken out when the field is back to what the plateau above gives it; one patch for the field, not one more for each edit. When the plateau has several patches on the field (a relationship added, another relabeled), none is rewritten: the last one takes an edit of its kind, and any other edit is a patch after it
| a value a patch of the plateau set
| that patch
| a new element (top level, or a child of an element the plateau inherits)
| a manifest whose header says `plateau: <the plateau>` (and `parent` for a child), in the folder of the manifests the plateau already has. A child of an element the plateau declares is written in the file of its parent, as before
| a new view or story (they belong to no plateau, whatever plateau is drawn)
| a manifest with no `plateau:` in its header, in the first folder of `manifests.paths`
| removing an element the plateau inherits
| a `REMOVE` at the end of the patches (with `layer: technology` for a technology element). The kernel takes what is in the element and what points at it; the manifests of the plateau above are not touched
| removing an element the plateau declares
| its manifests are deleted, as before, and the patches of the plateau that name it (their `target`, the relationship they find with `where` or add) are taken out with them
| a new plateau (_New plateau_, on the page Plateaus)
| a manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau` with its `id`, `name`, `horizon` and `override` (none when it overrides `baseline`)
| the name, horizon or parent of a plateau (_Edit plateau_)
| the same manifest; a plateau that exists through headers only gets one
| deleting a plateau (_Delete_)
| its manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau` and the manifests whose header says its id are deleted, after a dialog that lists them (see <<editor-plateaus>>)
|===

*Asking first.* A plateau that other plateaus stand on is not written without a word: when an edit changes the resolution of a plateau that overrides the one written, the editor lists those plateaus (the ones that inherit what was written and do not set it themselves) and writes only when you say yes. `baseline` is the plateau most often in that case. A plateau at the end of the chain, or an edit that no other plateau sees, is written at once.

*Only what was meant.* Every write is made on the manifests in memory by the same line-preserving save as ever (the lines the edit does not touch, their comments and quotes, stay as they were written; a list or map in brackets that an edit changes stays in brackets on its line when it fits, see <<editor-elements>>), and it is kept only if the plateaus are resolved again and all of this holds: the plateau written gives back the value that was edited; no plateau that resolved before is refused now, and no new mistake appears in the tree of the plateaus or in a plateau (a rename, a delete or a new parent that would make a patch of another plateau select nothing is refused, and says which plateau and which patch). A write that is refused is one sentence in the top bar, and nothing is changed: not a file, not a mark of what is modified. A new patch is always written at the end of the patches, never between two patches of yours, so that a comment above a patch stays above it.

*Clearing what the plateau inherits.* When you clear a name, a description or another text that the plateau only inherits, the editor writes a `REMOVE` patch with the `field`, at the end of the patches of the plateau drawn, after the question above if other plateaus stand on it. The Inspector then says where the value went (_taken out by patch 3 of saas (REMOVE target shop.web)_), and writing a text again changes that patch instead of adding one. A list or tags are emptied with a `SET` of an empty list or object, as before, and clearing a field that the plateau declares itself removes its line from the manifest, as before. If the plateau written does not give the field back gone, nothing is written.

*Refused, with one sentence.*

- Renaming (`id`) or moving an element the plateau inherits: a patch never changes an id or a parent; draw the plateau that declares the element. Renaming or moving an element the plateau declares works as it does in `baseline`; the `target` of patches, the relationships they add, and the `replaces` entries that name it follow the new reference.
- A field that a patch written by hand with a free `json-path` sets (for instance a `MERGE` of tags on every element that has a qualifier): the editor shows it, with its origin, and does not edit it field by field. Edit the patch in *Files*.
- A plateau cannot be drawn when the kernel refuses its patches: nothing is written in it until they are repaired in *Files*.

Not written by the editor: patches with a free `json-path` or an `expect` (they are written by hand in *Files*). A new plateau manifest is written next to the other manifests (`plateau.<id>.yaml` in the first folder of `manifests.paths`; for a plateau that exists through headers only, in the folder of its files). The text editor of *Files* writes the text of any file whatever plateau is drawn, and is the way to repair or write what the forms do not.

[[editor-plateaus]]
=== The Plateaus page

*Plateaus*, a page of the perspective Timeline (its address is `#/timeline/plateaus`), lists the plateaus as a tree: one entry for each, in the order of the tree, each indented by its depth. An entry says the name of the plateau, its id, its `horizon`, whether it is the one drawn or the one compared, and, in a line below, the plateau it overrides, where it is written (`described in inhouse/plateau.yaml`, or `exists only through the plateau key of a header` for a plateau that has no manifest of its own), how many patches it has and how many files belong to it. Below that, what it changes over the plateau it overrides, as the chips of the *Changes* panel, for the elements and for the relationships: new, changed, removed, and moved, split and merged when there are some. They are the counts of `archicode diff --plateau <id>`; a plateau that the command line refuses, or that overrides one it refuses, says it is not counted (a `!` marks it). The root overrides nothing, and says so. A workspace that writes no layer has the page too: one entry, `baseline`, and a note on what a plateau is. This page is the one place that lists the plateaus as a tree with their counts and the one place that writes a plateau: the band chooses and writes nothing. The indentation is not capped: each level of the tree is one step further in, for a chain of any length, and in a window narrower than 40 rem (see <<editor-small-screen>>) each level is half as far in, half a rem instead of a full step, so that a deep chain takes less of the width; the two lists of the band keep one em space per level.

The name of a plateau draws it, as a choice in the list *Plateau* of the band does. *Changes* draws the plateau and compares it with the plateau it overrides in one step, and the *Changes* panel opens on the right with the same lists as on the other pages; a click on one of its rows opens the element in *Elements*, since this page draws none (*Next change* and *Previous change* show the change they are at under the buttons, see <<editor-changes-panel>>).

*Writing a plateau.* *New plateau after this* (on an entry) and *New plateau* (above the list, for the plateau drawn, with _writing in: <plateau>_) make a plateau that overrides it; *Edit* (*Edit plateau* beside *New plateau*) changes its name, horizon or parent. The manifest is written as <<editor-writing>> says.

[[editor-plateau-delete]]
*Deleting a plateau.* *Delete* is on the entry of every plateau but the root, and *Delete plateau* beside *New plateau* for the plateau drawn. It opens a dialog that says what goes before anything does.

* *What can go.* Any plateau but the root. The dialog refuses, with one sentence and without a *Delete* button, the root (_every other plateau stands on it_), a workspace that has no plateau but `baseline`, plateaus that cannot be drawn, and a save in flight.
* *A plateau that others override.* It is deleted in the same one step. The plateaus that override it go under its parent: their `override` is rewritten, line by line, in their manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau` (the key is taken out when the new parent is the root). What the deleted plateau declared and patched is lost to every plateau that stands on it, and the dialog says it before anything is written: for each plateau above, its new parent, the elements it loses, the ones it gets back (the deleted plateau had removed them) and the ones that read differently. *Delete plateau* is the yes to those plateaus; a change to any other plateau is still asked. A comparison with the past of a plateau that overrode it follows it to the new parent.
* *What goes, and only that.* Its manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau` and every manifest whose header says its id, listed one by one (a file not saved yet is marked _new, not saved yet_, and a modified one says its changes go with it). A view or a story that says `plateau: <id>` belongs to no plateau: it stays, and the dialog says so. The files leave the disk at the next *Save all changes*, like any change, and *Cancel* gives the delete up with the others.
* *Files that the editor does not read.* A `.toml` or `.json` manifest, or a YAML file that the editor cannot read, may say `plateau: <id>`, or `override: <id>`, for the command line: the delete touches and rewrites none of them, the dialog lists them, and says that the command line still reads the plateau if one of them names it.
* *`settings.current`.* When it names the plateau (or `baseline`, when the last plateau goes), the key is taken out of `.archicode.yaml`, and the dialog says that the command line then draws the last plateau when the plateaus form one line, and asks for `--plateau` otherwise. A `current` that names another plateau, gone already, is left as it is.
* *What blocks it.* A step of a story that names the plateau in `at` or `against`, a view or a story step that names an element that only this plateau declares (it would exist in no plateau), a step told `at` a plateau above that would lose the element it draws, a step compared `against` a plateau above that would lose it when the plateau the step is told at does not keep it either, a saved view that is drawn in a plateau above and would lose its element (or, for a map, every element it is pinned on), and a plateau above that would no longer resolve (a patch that selects nothing, a relationship or a parent that is gone): the dialog lists the sentences, offers no *Delete*, and says to change them first, in Stories for the steps and the views, or to repair the plateau above or give it another parent with *Edit*. They are not cleared for you: a step told at a plateau is your statement of which state it shows. A step compared `against` a plateau above is not refused when the plateau it is told at (the plateau drawn when it says none) keeps what it draws: the comparison is drawn from that plateau, and an element the plateau compared with loses is then shown as new. The sentences are these (`tour` is a story, `beta` and `marketplace` stand above the plateau deleted, and `loyalty` is an element only the deleted plateau declares):
+
----
the story `tour` (`stories/story.tour.yaml`) step 1 is told at `beta`, which would no longer have `loyalty`
the story `tour` (`stories/story.tour.yaml`) step 1 is compared with `marketplace`, which would no longer have `loyalty`
the view `loyalty_overview` (`entries/view.loyalty.yaml`) is drawn in `beta`, `marketplace`, which would no longer have `loyalty`
----
+
A plateau that nothing of this kind concerns is deleted as before, byte for byte. On the layered Brightcart, `inhouse` cannot be deleted for that reason (`marketplace` patches what `inhouse` declares).
* [[fold-by-hand]]*Folding a plateau into the one above, by hand.* The editor does not move the patches and the elements of a plateau into the plateau above: that is a choice about your history, and no button makes it. When the refusal tells you the plateau above would lose what the plateau deleted gave it, and you want that plateau to keep it, fold by hand, in the files, with the plateau `to-be` and the plateau `later` above it (`later` overrides `to-be`):
+
. In every manifest whose header says `plateau: "to-be"`, write `plateau: "later"`. These are the elements `to-be` declares.
. In the manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau` of `later`, change `override: "to-be"` to the plateau `to-be` overrode (`"baseline"`, or take the line out when that is the root), and put the `patches` of `to-be` in front of those of `later`, in their order: `later` was written to be read over `to-be`, so its patches come last. A patch of `later` that refers to what `to-be` declared (a `REMOVE` of an element of `to-be`) still finds it, since it is now declared by `later` itself.
. Compose the `replaces` of `later`. Look in the manifests of `later` and in the `value` of its patches that `SET` a `replaces` for an entry that names an element `to-be` declared (say `shop.search`). Once `to-be` is gone, `later` is read over the plateau `to-be` overrode, and that entry names nothing. Replace it by what that element itself replaced (its `replaces`, in its manifest or set by a patch of `to-be`), or take it out when the element replaced nothing. An entry that names an element of the plateau below stays as it is. If you forget, `archicode diff` prints `warning: replaces of search (application layer): shop.search is not an element of the base plateau, ignored`, exits with 0, and reads the move as one new element and one removed element.
. Delete the manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau` of `to-be`, and any manifest that still names it, with your file manager or `git rm`.
. Change what still names `to-be`; `archicode` lists the ones you forgot, one `error:` line each (`the story `migration` (`plateaus/story.migration.yaml`) step 2 says `at: to-be`, which is not a plateau of this workspace (the plateaus are `baseline`, `later`)`). Each replacement is a choice. A step told `at: "to-be"` becomes `at: "later"`, but then it draws `later`, which is `to-be` and the layer above it: it can draw more, and a step that names an element `later` removes is skipped with a warning (`warning: skip migration/4: it names `shop`, which the plateau `later` does not have`). An `against: "to-be"` becomes the plateau `to-be` overrode, or the key is taken out: do not write `later` there, a step told at `later` and compared with `later` is one `error:` line (`... step 5 says `at: later` and `against: later`: a plateau is never compared with itself`). The `plateau` of a view or a story means nothing and may stay. If `settings.current` in `.archicode.yaml` is `to-be`, write `later`: otherwise the commands that read it stop with `error: `settings.current` names the plateau `to-be`, which is not a plateau of this workspace (the plateaus are `baseline`, `later`)`.
+
Check it with `archicode plateau list` (`to-be` is gone, `later` has the plateau `to-be` overrode as its parent), with `archicode diff --plateau later --against baseline` and with `archicode views generate --plateau later --against baseline`, before and after. Name `--against` both times: the default of `later` is `to-be` before and its new parent after, so two defaults compare two different pairs. When the steps are followed, the text above the table of `diff` and every `.puml` that `views generate` writes are the same, the table has the same rows, and two columns of the cascade of the removals differ: PLATEAU, where the rows of `to-be` now say `later`, and PATCH, since the patches of `later` are numbered after those of `to-be`; `gap.md` and `gap.json` differ in those two columns only. On a copy of the example `domain_plateaus` with the plateau `later` of the tutorial, only the column PLATEAU changes, because the one patch of `later` has no row in the cascade. A `replaces ... ignored` warning after the fold means that the third step was missed. `archicode query graph` is not a byte for byte check: the order of siblings that two files of the folded plateau declare can change. This is for a plateau with one plateau above it; with two, keep it: folding it into one would leave the other without what it declared. The folding loses what the plateau `to-be` was on its own: a comparison `--plateau to-be`, its views folder `views-to-be`, and the `against: "to-be"` of any step, which you changed above.
* *After it.* The plateau it overrode is drawn, with no comparison, when the plateau drawn was the one deleted; the plateau drawn stays, compared with nothing, when the one deleted was the one compared with. The folders of views that the command line generated (`views-<id>` and the folders of its comparisons) are not manifests: they stay where they are.

[[editor-compare]]
=== Comparing two plateaus

*Compare with*, on the band, compares the plateau that is drawn with another one (see <<editor-band>>): _Past_ is the plateau it overrides, and the tree lists every other plateau, the one that overrides the plateau drawn (the _future_ of the comparison), a sibling or a cousin included. With nothing compared, which is the default, the plateau is drawn alone, exactly as the editor always did. A comparison draws the plateau together with the other one: Applications, the diagrams of Elements and the page of a view in Stories tell what is new, what changed and what was removed (and, for the elements that say what they replace, what moved, was split or merged, see <<editor-replaces>>), with the same states as `archicode views generate` (see <<plateau-comparison>>). Like the plateau, the choice is remembered by this browser for this folder (not in the workspace), and it is ignored when the workspace no longer has that plateau. A plateau is never compared with itself. The address carries the plateau (`plateau=<id>`, when it is not `baseline`), and the comparison only next to the selection of a removed element (`compare=past`, `compare=future:<id>` or `compare=other:<id>`, see <<editor-changes-address>>); a link that has `compare=` draws that comparison, and one that has none leaves the comparison as it is.

The plateau compared with is _read_, not written: the editor resolves it from the manifests it has already read (there is no second folder to pick), keeps it apart from the plateau that is drawn, and an edit goes to the plateau drawn (see <<editor-writing>>). Its elements are not in the trees of the plateau drawn, and what only it has is listed in the *Changes* panel and read in the panel on the right. A plateau that cannot be resolved cannot be compared, and the dialog of the mistakes (the button of the band) says why. The one write that is made for the comparison is *Write replaces* (see <<editor-replaces>>).

The diagram is drawn from the union of the two plateaus, and the boxes are laid out on that union: what was removed is drawn where it was, so a box does not move when the comparison is turned on from the other plateau or when you choose the other plateau of the pair (when nothing is compared, the plateau is laid out alone, as before). The editor tells the states without relying on colour: each one has a mark in front of the title of the box and of the caption of the link, a line style, and a pill with its name on the box.

[cols="1,1,2,2"]
|===
| State | Mark | Box | Link

| new | `+` | thick solid outline, a pill `new`, a green tint | thick solid line
| changed | `~` | dotted outline, a pill `changed`, an orange tint | dotted line
| removed | `−` | long dashes with narrow gaps, grey outline, grey struck title, a pill `removed`, a grey tint | dashed grey line
| contains changes | `±` | thin dashed outline, a pill `contains-changes` | (none)
| moved | `→` | long dashes with wide gaps, a pill `moved`, a blue tint, `(from shop.till)` after the title | (none)
| split | `⋔` | medium dashes, a pill `split`, a purple tint, `(from shop.report)` after the title | (none)
| merged | `⊕` | short dashes far apart, a pill `merged`, a light blue tint, `(from stock.bin, stock.shelf)` after the title | (none)
|===

The last three states are for the elements that say what they replace, and exist only for a box: a relationship is never moved, split or merged. They are in the table above and in the key only when the drawing has a box in that state.

A box that did not change is drawn as it always was. A removed element is not in the plateau that is drawn, so it is not in the tree and a click on its box does not open it: it shows, in the panel on the right, what the other plateau had (name, kind, properties, the file it is in, the relationships it declared), read-only. A link that changed shows its mark even when the captions are off, as on the Map. The marks and lines are the ones of the command line (a dash and a gap of its own for `removed` 16-3, `moved` 12-12, `split` 8-8 and `merged` 3-9 in both, so that no two of them are alike); the colours are only a help, chosen to read in the light and the dark theme. *Red dashed* keeps its meaning of a dangling reference (an unknown element), and is never used for a removed one.

The look is a default, given to the stereotype of the same name as the state (`new`, `changed`, `removed`, `contains-changes`). A style of your own for that name in `styles` (see <<stereotypes-and-styles>>) wins property by property: your `line-color` replaces the colour of the line and keeps its style and thickness, your `background-color` fills the box and replaces the tint, your `font-color` replaces the grey of a removed title. The state is the last stereotype of a box, so it overrides the style of the others, as a later stereotype always does. A `new` or `changed` tag that you wrote by hand on an element that does not have that state is only your stereotype, with your own style if you gave it one, and the command line draws it the same way (see <<plateau-state-tags>>).

*The key is folded.* Under the diagram, the key starts as one row: a sample of the box and of the link, the mark, the name and the count of each state the drawing uses, and a *Key* button. The row wraps on a wide window and stays one row that scrolls along under 700 pixels. *Key* (`aria-expanded`) opens the rest, which says how each line is drawn and the note about references that point nowhere; the open key takes at most 30 % of the height of the diagram area and scrolls inside, and the cap follows a resize of the window (it is worked out again when the window changes size, so a key left open keeps within 30 % of a smaller window). Open or folded is not saved: it is not in the address or the workspace, and a new folder folds it again. Open, the key adds an entry for each state the drawing uses, with a sample of its box and its link, its mark, its name, how its line is drawn, and how many boxes and links of that state the drawing shows. The SVG and PNG downloads draw the same marks, lines, struck titles, pills and tints; like the other keys, the key itself is not part of the image.

[[editor-changes-panel]]
==== The Changes panel

While a comparison is on, the panel on the right of Plateaus, Applications, Environments and Elements is never empty. With nothing selected it is the *Changes* panel; with something selected it has two tabs, *Selected* (what is selected, as before) and *Changes* (with the number of rows). On Stories the panel is the one of the view, and while a comparison is on and can be drawn it has two tabs, *View* (what was there) and *Changes*; a story, or a view that cannot be drawn, had no panel and has the *Changes* panel alone.

The panel starts with the counts: elements and relationships, each _new_, _changed_ and _removed_, as chips that carry a mark and a word (`+ 38 new`, `~ 26 changed`, `− 5 removed`), so that they read without colour; the elements add `→ 1 moved`, `⋔ 2 split` and `⊕ 1 merged` when there are some. The same chip is the state on every row below. Two lists follow, *Elements* and *Relationships*, in the order a review reads them: what is new, then what changed, then what moved, was split or merged, then what was removed, and by reference inside each. A row has the chip, the name and the reference of the element (a relationship reads `source → destination “label”`) and, for something that changed, what changed in it in words: `name`, `description (wording only)`, `qualifiers`, `tags`, `deployment`, `relationships added`, `relationships removed`, `relationships changed`, and for a relationship `label`, `qualifiers`, `tags`. A relationship whose label changed is one row that says the label before and after. A row of a moved, split or merged element says where it came from (`from shop.till`, `from shop.report; split with shop.report-b`), as `archicode diff` does.

*Reading a long list.* A comparison of a large workspace has hundreds of changes (the pair `before` and `after-declared` of the fixture `replaces-large-pair` has 360). Under the counts, the panel says how many there are (`167 changes`) and has what it takes to read them without losing the place:

* *Previous change* and *Next change* walk through the list, in the order of the list (the elements, then the relationships). They stop at the ends and do not wrap, and the panel says where you are (`3 of 12`). With no change selected, *Next change* goes to the first and *Previous change* to the last. The walk selects the change as a click on its row does, and leaves the list on screen (the *Changes* tab stays). Under the buttons the panel shows the _What changed_ block of the change the walk is at (see <<editor-what-changed>>), so that the page Plateaus, and a story with no view drawn, where no box can be shown, still say what the change is; a click on a row there opens the element in *Elements*, as before.
* The walk row stays in view while the list scrolls. *Previous change*, *Next change*, the position (`3 of 12`) and *Show all* are one row under the tabs, sticky at the top of the panel, so a reader who walks with the mouse does not scroll back to the buttons after each press. A press brings the selected row into view with the nearest scroll (`block: nearest`) that shows it, below the sticky row: a row already in view does not move the list, and one that is below it moves the list just enough. At a window 360 pixels wide the row is measured as well, its height as a share of the panel's height at 360 by 640 pixels, against the 35 percent limit of the design; above that limit the rows would have a box of their own and the buttons would stay outside it. The figure of that measure is in the record of the change (run 0201), not in this manual. The row was tested in Chromium and Firefox. Safari was not tested, so whether it keeps the row in view in the same way is not proved.
* The lists *State* (all, then the states that some row has, each with its count) and *Layer* (both, application, technology) narrow what is listed: the line then reads `12 of 167 changes`, each group says `n of N`, and *Show all* clears both. `contains-changes` is not a choice: it is the state of a box that holds a change, never of a row. A filter that leaves out the change you are at moves you to the nearest one that is shown.
* The filters change the list only, never what the diagrams draw (*Changes only* does that, see <<editor-changes-only>>), nor the counts above them. They are the editor's, not the workspace's: they start again when the pair of plateaus compared changes. The walk has a keyboard shortcut, `J` and `K` (see <<editor-keyboard>>).

A click on a row selects the thing as a click on its box or its link does, and shows it. On the Map the boxes around it are opened so that it is drawn, and the view is centred on it (what the *Center* button does); a pin or a hidden element of your own is not undone, and the panel says when the element is not on the map. A removed element opens the read-only panel described above, a relationship selects its link (in Elements, the element that declares it, and its link when that element's diagram is shown). The row of the selection is marked in the list, and the *Changes* tab brings the list back with that row in view.

[[editor-what-changed]]
==== What changed

The panel of a selected element, link or removed box adds a *What changed* block, first, so that a long description does not push it out of sight; it is the same on the page of a view in Stories.

- For a *changed* element, the natures in words, then each property that differs with its value _Before_ and _After_ (name, kind, description, qualifiers, tags, and the applications or `uses` of a node), then its relationships that are new, changed or removed, each a line with its chip. The *Before* and *After* lines carry a sign and a word, and the old value is struck, so nothing relies on colour.
- For a *new* element, "Whole element is new": its properties are the ones listed below the block, and the relationships it declares are listed as new.
- For a *removed* element, the read-only panel above, with "Whole element is removed"; it does not repeat what the panel already lists. A removed element that is only drawn as the place of what was inside it, and that other elements replace, adds _Replaced by_ and lists them.
- For a *moved*, *split* or *merged* element, one sentence that says which, then _Replaces_ with the name and the reference of each element it replaces (one that the other plateau does not have is flagged), then, if the element also changed, _Also changed_ and the properties _Before_ and _After_ as above.
- For a *link*, its state (all of the relationships it groups new, or removed, or changed, or a mixture, which reads _changed_) and, on each relationship it groups, its chip and, for one that changed, what it was and what it is.

[[editor-changes-only]]
==== Changes only and wording-only changes

*Everything | Changes only*, among the tools of a diagram and shown while a plateau is compared, sets what the diagrams draw: with *Changes only* a diagram keeps what is new, changed or removed, the boxes that enclose them and the ends of the changed links; the links that did not change are left out. The boxes kept only to place the changes, the _context_, are drawn faded, say no state and are told in the key ("context, faded: shown to place the changes", with their number). The focal element of a diagram always stays and is never faded. It works the same on the Map, on the diagrams of Elements and on the page of a view in Stories, and together with the pins, the hidden elements, a facet and *Hide unlinked*.

A change of the description alone is a change of wording, not of structure. The *Changes* panel counts them apart ("10 of the changed elements are changes of wording only") and keeps them visible by default; the checkbox *Hide wording-only changes* sets them aside in one click: those elements are drawn as unchanged (and a box that only they changed is no longer marked `contains-changes`, nor kept by *Changes only*), their rows leave the list, the counts of changed elements drop by their number and the panel says "10 wording-only changes hidden". Relationships are never set aside: a change of a label, a qualifier or a tag is structure.

Both choices are yours and not the workspace's: they are remembered per folder in this browser, like the plateau and the comparison, never written to `.archicode.yaml`, and they do nothing while nothing is compared.

[[editor-changes-address]]
==== The address and the trees

The address of the page carries a selection, so that a reload or a shared link reopens it: `ref=` for an element, `link-from=` and `link-to=` for a link, as before, and `removed=` for a removed element (`removed-layer=technology` for one of the technology layer). A removed element exists only next to its comparison, so the address then also carries `compare=` (`past`, the past that follows the plateau drawn; `future:<id>`, a plateau that overrides the plateau drawn; or `other:<id>`, any other plateau of the tree: a choice in the tree of *Compare with* is written as one of the three; `compare=future` with no id is read as the future to choose, and shown as the disabled entry _Choose a future plateau_ when several plateaus override the plateau drawn): opening such an address chooses that comparison, as if you had chosen it, even if this browser remembered another one. The other choices of the comparison are not in the address.

While a comparison is on, a row of the tree of Elements and of the tree of the Map has a small mark after its name: `+` for a new element, `~` for a changed one, `→`, `⋔` or `⊕` for a moved, split or merged one, and `±` for one that did not change but has a change below it. The word is in its tooltip and is read by a screen reader. A removed element is not in these trees (it is not an element of the plateau drawn, so it can be neither pinned nor hidden nor edited): the *Changes* panel lists it and opens its panel.

[[editor-replaces]]
==== Moved, split and merged elements, and candidates

An element of the plateau that is drawn can say which elements of the plateau it is compared with it replaces: the key `replaces` (see <<plateau-replaces>>). The editor draws the result with the three states above, counts them and lists them in the *Changes* panel, and the *What changed* block of a moved, split or merged element says what it replaces. A replaced element is not listed as removed. The *Changes only* filter keeps these elements like the others. The hand-written tags still follow the rule of <<plateau-state-tags>>: the look of `moved`, `split` or `merged` is only for a box the comparison says is in that state.

*Candidates.* Under the counts, the *Candidates* section lists the pairs the comparison takes for one thing said twice: an element only the other plateau has, and an element only this one has, of the same kind and the same layer, with the same name (when no other removed and no other new element of that kind has it), a close description, or as the only two of their kind under the same parent (not between two plateaus that are not one the past of the other, two alternatives), and each with its reasons. Unlike `archicode diff`, which lists them only for a plateau that already uses `replaces`, the editor proposes them for any comparison: it is how an author starts. A candidate is never applied by itself. Each pair has two buttons:

* *Write replaces* adds the reference of the removed element to the `replaces` of the new one, in the manifest that declares the new element, and nothing else. It is an edit like the others: the file is marked as modified and written by *Save all changes*, the lines you did not touch (comments, blank lines, quotes) stay as they are, a list that was there gets the reference appended once, and a list written on one line (`replaces: [shop.till]`) becomes a list on several lines. The comparison is made again at once, and the new element is now `moved`, `split` or `merged`.
* *Not related* sets the pair aside. It is remembered by the page only, until you close it: nothing is written and the comparison is not changed. The section says how many pairs are set aside, and *Show again* brings them back.

*Write replaces* writes the key on the element `to` of the _more recent_ plateau of the two compared, in the manifest that declares it, and nowhere else; when the plateau compared with is the past of the one drawn (or any plateau the plateau drawn stands on), the more recent plateau is the one drawn, and when it is a plateau above the one drawn, the more recent plateau is the one compared with, which the button writes all the same, since it is the one thing the *Changes* panel offers. It is refused, with the reason in the panel and nothing written, when the element belongs to the older plateau of the two (`replaces` goes on the more recent plateau: compare the other way round), when the more recent plateau only _inherits_ the element (it is declared in a plateau above, and the key would change that plateau and the ones that override it: compare that plateau with its past instead), when the replaced element is still in the more recent plateau, when it is not an element of the older one, or when the `replaces` of the element is not a list.

One more refusal belongs to comparisons several layers apart. `replaces` is read against the plateau a manifest builds on, so when a layer in between removed the replaced element, the entry would name nothing in the layer it is written in. The button is then off, and the sentence is its title and is written under the pair; a click that gets through anyway writes nothing and shows the same sentence, which names the element, the plateau the manifest builds on and what to do:

----
“a” is not an element of “p1”, the plateau “p2” builds on (a layer in between removed it): replaces is read against that plateau, so the entry would name nothing there. Compare “p2” with “p1”, or write the entry in the layer that still has “a” (a plateau that overrides one that holds it); nothing was written.
----

A split has one replaced element for all its pairs, so they are all refused or all allowed. A merge is written one pair at a time, so every pair is checked before the first is written, and the merge is refused as a whole: if the layer cannot hold one of the replaced elements, the buttons of all its pairs are off (the first pair keeps its own sentence above), and none is written, where the pair the layer could hold used to be written alone. The other pairs say why:

----
“n” would merge “a” and “b”, which is written one pair at a time, so every pair is checked before the first is written: “a” is not an element of “p1”, the plateau “p2” builds on (a layer in between removed it): replaces is read against that plateau, so the entry would name nothing there. Compare “p2” with “p1”, or write the entry in the layer that still has “a” (a plateau that overrides one that holds it). If a pair is no part of the merge, set it aside with “Not related”; nothing was written.
----

A pair set aside with *Not related* is no part of the merge, so the others are written as a lone pair is. A merge whose every replaced element the layer holds is written as it was, and a comparison one layer apart is not concerned.

*By hand.* The edit form of an element has a *Replaces* section as soon as the workspace has several plateaus: the list, each reference with a button to remove it, and a field to add one (it completes with the references of the plateau compared with). A reference the plateau compared with does not have is accepted and flagged ("not in “Base”", with the name of that plateau), because you may have written it for another baseline; one that the plateau drawn still has is flagged "still in this plateau: ignored". An element cannot replace itself, and a reference is listed once. *Add replaces* is checked as *Write replaces* is, when a comparison is on and the plateau drawn is the more recent of the two: if the comparison is several layers apart and a layer in between removed the reference, the field shows the sentence above under it, keeps what you typed, and nothing is written. With no comparison, or one layer apart, a reference is written as before. The details of an element that replaces something list it too.

*Warnings.* What the comparison ignored in the `replaces` it was given (see <<plateau-replaces-warnings>>) is a short list in the *Changes* panel, under the candidates, with the sentences of the command line; the editor does not write them to the console. A second list, *Hand-written states*, has one line per element and word for the state words you wrote by hand that the comparison does not give to the element (see <<plateau-state-tags>>), with the sentences of the command line, and the panel of such an element says it too, so that a box with a `new` badge that is not drawn as new tells why.

*Moving an element.* An element that you put under another parent in the editor has another reference, so it reads as a removed element and a new one when it is compared with the plateau it came from. *Move* does not write `replaces` (see <<editor-move>>): write it from *Write replaces* in the *Changes* panel, or in the form of the element.

[[editor-move]]
==== Moving an element under another parent

In the edit form of an element, *Move* (after *Delete*, before *Add child*) puts the element, and everything inside it, under another parent, or at the top level, without breaking a reference. It is offered when the dialog has at least one place to offer, the top level included: a solution, a person and an environment are at the top already and have nowhere else to go, so they have no *Move*; a system can go under a solution, and goes to the top level from there. It works on the plateau that is drawn, and never on the one it is compared with, which is read-only. In a plateau of layers it moves an element that plateau declares; an element it inherits is refused (a patch never changes a parent), and says which plateau declares it.

The dialog has a search field (it keeps the places that have every word you type in their name, id, reference or kind) and the list of the places the element can go, in the layer of the element: the _top level_, when it may be there, then the *valid parents*, each an element that may hold the kind of this one (the rules that create an element under a parent, see <<nesting>>). It says how many elements follow, how many references will be rewritten in how many files, and how many saved views follow. Under the places, a closed list says, for every element of the layer that is not offered, why not, with the same search: _N places are not offered, and why_. The reasons are the sentences that refuse a move: it is the element itself, it is inside the element, it is its parent already, its kind cannot be held by that parent (the sentence says what the parent holds), the parent already holds an element with that id, the parent is declared twice. A reason that stops every move (an element inside it is declared twice, a manifest cannot be read, a reference cannot be rewritten as text) is told once, at the top, as _No move can be written now_.

*The top level.* The kinds that may be at the top level are the ones the layer holds there: a person, a solution and a system in the application layer, an environment in the technology layer; for any other kind the list says so (_A container is never at the top level: the application layer holds only person, solution and system there._), and the editor neither offers it nor moves an element there. In practice the top level is offered for a system that sits in a solution.

*What follows.* The reference of the element becomes the reference of the new parent followed by its id (its id alone, at the top level), and so does the reference of each element inside it. Every reference to the element or to something inside it is rewritten, in every file of the workspace, by the machinery of an id change:

* the `destination` of the relationships of any element of the same layer;
* the `applications` of the nodes of the technology layer (for an application element) and the `uses` of the environments (for a technology element);
* the `header.parent` of the manifests attached to the element or below it;
* the saved views and the steps of stories of the workspace (the `element` of a view, the pinned, hidden and open elements of a Map view);
* what the page remembers: the selection, the unfolded rows, the modified marks, the pins of the Map.

The facets (their JSONPath is not a reference) are not read, as when an id changes. Nothing else of a file is touched.

*What is written.* There are four ways, chosen by how the manifest holds the element and where the new parent is declared, and the dialog says which one a place gets. The lines that are not part of the move stay as the author wrote them (comments, blank lines, quotes), as for any other edit:

* An element that has a manifest of its own (`app.shop.till.yaml`, with its `header.parent`) stays in its file: only the `header.parent` line changes (at the top level the line goes), and the manifests attached below it follow by their own `header.parent`. The new parent can be in any file.
* An element that is nested in the manifest of another one (in its `content.elements`) and goes under a parent of the same file is moved as a block inside that manifest: its lines, with the comments just above it and those at the end of its lines, go to the `elements` of the new parent, one level deeper or shallower, and the `elements` it leaves is dropped when it was the last element.
* A nested element that goes under a parent in another file (declared in another manifest of the plateau written) is cut out of its file and pasted in the other, with its comments and its quotes. Both files are marked as modified, and a refused write changes neither.
* A nested element that goes to the top level, or under a parent declared in a plateau whose files the plateau drawn does not write (a plateau it overrides), gets a manifest of its own, next to the file it leaves (`app.<reference>.yaml`, `env.` for the technology layer, and `-2` when the name is taken), with its `kind`, its `version`, the `plateau` drawn when it is not `baseline` and the `parent`, none at the top level. A group has no kind of manifest, so a group cannot go there: that is #114.

The comments on lines of their own inside the block go with it, and so do the comments after its last line when they belong to it. The rule is the indentation, nothing else (a comment cannot be asked whom it is about): a comment after the last line goes with the block when it is indented deeper than the item, as the keys of the block are, or when it is at the column of the item and nothing follows it in the list; a comment at the column of the item above a sibling is that sibling's introduction and stays, and so does one that is shallower than the item. The first comment that stays ends the scan, and the blank lines between the block and a comment that goes with it go with it. The rule is wrong in a few cases: a comment about the block written at the column of an item that has a sibling after it stays (it reads as the sibling's); a comment about the next element indented as the keys of the block goes with the block; a comment at the column of the item, last in its list, goes with the block even when you meant it for the next key of the parent; and a comment deeper than the item after a blank line goes with it, even as a section comment. It is the same for the block that moves to another file and for the one that becomes a manifest of its own. A file whose layout the move cannot follow (an anchor, an alias, a tag, a list written at the column of its key) is written by the ordinary save: the move works and the file says the right thing, and the comments of the block are the price. The end of line of each file is its own.

*What a move tells before it writes.* A move changes the reference of the element, so the views and the steps of the stories that name it follow it, in every plateau. A plateau beside the one drawn that still declares the old reference can then no longer draw those views, and *Move* says so as a rename does (see <<editor-rename>>): it opens a dialog that names those plateaus and the views and steps, and asks before it writes (_Back to Move_ or _Move anyway_). The plateaus it does not name are not concerned. Moving the element there too keeps the views whole.

*Replaces.* *Move* does not write `replaces`: the element that a move can reach is declared by the plateau that is drawn, and the plateaus it overrides do not have its old reference, so there is nothing for the key to name. In a workspace of plateaus the dialog says so in one line. To say that an element replaces one of another plateau, use *Write replaces* in the *Changes* panel, or the *Replaces* section of the form of the element (see <<editor-replaces>>).

*A move that is refused* changes nothing at all, and the reason is told in the dialog, which stays open with what you chose: the new parent is the element or something inside it, it is its parent already, it cannot hold that kind, it already holds an element with that id, the element or the parent is declared twice or belongs to the plateau compared with, a manifest cannot be read (a reference in it could not be rewritten), a reference is written as a number (`destination: 8080`) and cannot be rewritten as the reference of another element, or an id has a `/` or a `\` in it, which cannot be the name of a file (rename the element first).

Like every edit, a move is not written until *Save all changes*, and *Cancel* gives it up with the others (the folder is read again). The files it changed are marked as modified, and so is the moved element.

[[editor-theme]]
=== Theme

The editor is drawn in a light or a dark theme. By default it follows the one of the operating system. *Settings* has a section, *Appearance (this browser)*, to choose *Light*, *Dark* or *System* instead: the choice applies at once, to the page, the code editors and the SVG and PNG exported from a diagram.

The choice is a preference of the browser, not of the workspace. It is remembered by the browser alone (nothing is written to `.archicode.yaml` or to any file of the folder), it is not a change to save, and it does not light *Save all changes*. A browser that refuses to remember it keeps the choice until the page is closed.

[[editor-keyboard]]
=== Keyboard

.In a tree
- Up and Down move, Home and End jump to the first and last row.
- Right unfolds a branch or goes to its first child, Left folds it or goes to its parent.
- Enter and Space select the element (in the Map, they fold or unfold the branch).
- In the Map, `P` pins or unpins the element, and `H` hides it or shows it again.

.In the Changes panel
- `J` goes to the next change and `K` to the previous one: the walk of the buttons *Next change* and *Previous change*, in the same order, with the same filters, stopping at the ends. They work on every page that has the list (Plateaus, Applications, Environments, Elements, and Stories while a comparison is on), and from the *Selected* or *View* tab they bring the *Changes* tab back. They do nothing while you type in a field, an editor, a list or a text box, while a dialog, the help or the menu of the header is open, with Ctrl, Alt, Meta or Shift held, or where no list of changes is on the page. The buttons tell the key in their tooltips.

.In a diagram
- The arrow keys move between boxes, and Enter opens the one that has the focus.
- The boxes are one Tab stop, and so are the links: a diagram takes two presses of Tab to cross, however many boxes and links it has. The arrow keys move from the box that has the focus to the neighbouring box, which becomes the stop. On a link, the arrow keys move to the next and the previous link (not to the neighbouring box), Home and End jump to the first and the last, Enter or Space selects it, and `Esc` deselects it. Tab goes from the boxes to the links and on, and Shift+Tab comes back.
- `+`, `-` and `0` zoom in, out and back to the actual size. `F` fits the whole diagram and `C` goes back to how it opened.
- `M` maximizes the diagram, `Esc` brings the tree back. On the page of a view in Stories, `M` hides the list of views and the panel, and `Esc` restores them, or else deselects the box or the link (see <<editor-stories>>).

.In the story player
- Left and Right show the previous and the next step, `Home` and `End` the first and the last, `Esc` closes the window and gives the focus back to *Play*; Tab and Shift+Tab go round inside it. Up and Down are not taken. Nothing else happens while a field, a text box or a list has the focus (the list of the steps has its own arrows, and lets `Esc` through), while a dialog, the help or the menu of the header is open, or with Ctrl, Alt, Meta or Shift held; `J` and `K` do nothing in it (see <<editor-story-player>>).

== Commands

// - ArchiCode provides commands to generate views located in a workspace.
// - ArchiCode provides commands to query the data model of the workspace.

Within the realm of ArchiCode, an array of meticulously designed commands empowers you to wield precision and control over your architectural workspace. Let's delve into ArchiCode's command utilities, which enable you to seamlessly generate views and explore the intricacies of your workspace's data model:

- View Generation Commands: ArchiCode offers a range of commands dedicated to the seamless generation of views situated within your workspace. These commands enable you to customize the generation of views, providing a streamlined approach to shaping your architectural visualization.

- Data Model Querying Commands: ArchiCode equips you with specialized commands designed to query the intricate data model of your workspace. These commands serve as a window into your architecture's foundational elements, facilitating insightful exploration and analysis.
