The third step focused on the composition of containers within the "User Management" system, the establishment of relationships between them as well as the refinement of existing relationships.

===== Conclusion

// - The iterative process described in this example is just a backbone.
// - The iterative process must be tailored according to the architecture work.
// - The next steps may involve deeper decomposition of the other elements.
// - The next steps may involve the naming and qualification of the relationships.
// - The next steps may involve the naming, description and qualification of the elements.
// - The next steps may involve a customization of the styling and formatting to suite audience expectation.
// - The next steps may involve the design of the technology layer.

The iterative process outlined in this example serves as a foundational framework for designing and refining the architecture of an e-commerce platform using ArchiCode. It provides a structured approach to create a clear and organized representation of system elements, their relationships, and interactions. However, it is important to recognize that this process acts as a flexible backbone, adaptable to the unique needs and complexities of each architectural endeavor.

.Tailoring the Iterative Process:
Each architectural project requires a tailored approach to effectively address its specific requirements. Depending on the project's scope and goals, the iterative process may be customized to accommodate deeper levels of decomposition, refined naming and qualification of relationships, as well as more detailed descriptions and qualifications of individual elements.

.Deeper Decomposition and Naming:
As the design evolves, subsequent iterations may involve further decomposing other elements within the architecture, leading to a more comprehensive and intricate model. Additionally, refining the naming and qualification of relationships enhances clarity and ensures that the architecture accurately reflects the intended interactions.

.Customization for Audience Expectations:
To effectively communicate the architecture to diverse audiences, customization of styling and formatting becomes essential. Tailoring the visual representation to meet the expectations and preferences of stakeholders ensures that the architectural model is accessible and understandable to all parties involved.

.Incorporating the Technology Layer:
The final stages of the iterative process may encompass the design of the technology layer, where decisions about technologies, platforms, and frameworks are integrated into the architecture. This step bridges the gap between the high-level conceptual design and the practical implementation of the system.

In essence, ArchiCode empowers architects to iteratively design, refine, and communicate complex architectures. By customizing the iterative process and adapting it to the evolving project requirements, architects can create architecture models that effectively guide development decisions, facilitate communication, and lead to the successful realization of sophisticated systems. The collaborative and adaptable nature of ArchiCode empowers architects to navigate the complexities of modern software and system design with confidence.

[[facets]]
==== Tackling view clutter

===== Introduction

// - When the Archicode workspace deeply expresses a model, the views generated become disordered and difficult to understand.
// - This is particularly the case when systematic interactions such as authorization or observability are designed in.
// - As a result, views need to be refined to focus on specific concerns.
// - The facet concept provided by Archicode is a solution for refining views seamlessly.
// - The facet concept automates the generation of secondary workspaces.
// - Generation is based on transformation rules that remove elements and relationships based on JSON Path queries.
// - In this way, it is possible to generate secondary workspaces relevant to a specific subset of elements and relationships, enabling views focused on specific concerns.
// - For example, this makes it easy to generate views dedicated to authorization or observability, leaving the main views to concentrate on the essentials.

Navigating through complex architectural models often presents a challenge for professionals as intricate details and systematic interactions like authorization or observability make the generated views disordered and complex. As these views become overwhelming, there is an evident need for refined, focused, and organized presentation to enhance clarity and understanding. This is where Archicode steps in, offering an innovative solution to transform this complexity into a streamlined and comprehensible format.

Archicode introduces the facet concept, a feature engineered to automate the generation of secondary workspaces and refine views to focus on specific concerns. By employing transformation rules based on JSON Path queries, facets effectively remove certain elements and relationships, offering a cleaner, focused view of specific subsets. This feature serves as a bridge from the chaos of detailed expressions to an organized, easily understandable visualization, paving the way for enhanced insights into complex architectural models.

[NOTE]
.The paths of a facet are standard JSONPath
====
The `json-path` of an action is a JSONPath as standardized by https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9535[RFC 9535]. It selects nodes of the workspace, and the `REMOVE` operator deletes them. The same code evaluates it in the command line and in the editor, so a facet cannot mean one thing in each. Beyond comparisons, a filter can combine tests with `&&`, `||` and `!`, test that a field exists (`?(@.label)`), look inside a list (`?(@.qualifiers[?(@ == 'oidc')])`) and call `length()` or `count()`. The functions `match()` and `search()` (regular expressions) are not available.

The facets written for the previous engine (Jayway) are no longer read since 0.17. A path that uses `anyof`, `noneof`, `== null` or `!= null` is refused, by the command line and by the editor, with one sentence that names the standard form:

----
error: facet f: the facet path `$.application..elements[?(@.qualifiers anyof ['a'])]` uses `@.qualifiers anyof ['a']`, which is not JSONPath (RFC 9535) and is no longer read: write `@.qualifiers[?(@ == 'a')]`
----

Nothing is written, and the exit code is 1; the editor shows the same sentence as the error of the diagram, and of a saved view that names the facet, where it used to say that the facet removes the element (it said so for any facet that the kernel refuses, which hid the sentence). Rewrite the paths as follows:

[cols="1,1"]
|===
|Before |Write

|`@.qualifiers anyof ['a', 'b']`
|`@.qualifiers[?(@ == 'a' \|\| @ == 'b')]`

|`@.qualifiers noneof ['a']`
|`(@.qualifiers && !@.qualifiers[?(@ == 'a')])`

|`@.qualifiers == null`
|`!@.qualifiers`

|`@.qualifiers != null`
|`@.qualifiers`
|===

The keys of a facet are written in kebab-case: `global-enabled`, `directory-name-template`, `hide-unlinked` and `json-path`. The camel-case spelling is no longer read either, and the sentence says which key to write:

----
error: `.archicode.yaml` is not valid: unknown key `hideUnlinked`, write `hide-unlinked` (known: actions, hide-unlinked, name) at `settings.facets.customs[0].hideUnlinked` (line 5, column 27)
----

Relationships that have no tag, or no qualifier, simply do not have the field: `@.tags['k'] != 'v'` selects them, `@.tags['k'] == 'v'` does not.
====

[NOTE]
.The name of a facet is the name of a folder
====
The `name` of a facet goes, as it is written, into the name of the folders that hold its views: `views-oidc` in the steps below, and, next to each step of `archicode stories generate`, `<NN>-<view>-<facet>` (see <<stories-generate>>); `directory-name-template` only says where the name goes. It must therefore be one folder name, and one that every system takes, since a workspace is shared. A name is refused when it:

* is empty, or is `.` or `..`;
* holds `/`, `\`, a NUL or another control character;
* holds one of `< > : " | ? *`, or ends with a dot or a space (the names Windows refuses).

The reserved device names of Windows (`CON`, `NUL`, `COM1`) are not looked at. A name is never rewritten, because two facets could then write in the same folder. Every command that loads the workspace (`views generate`, `stories generate`, `query graph` and `diff`) refuses a name that is not a folder name before it reads a manifest or writes a file, with one line for each such facet, in the order of the file, exit code 1; the first reason found, from the left of the name, is the one told:

----
error: the facet name `a/b` cannot be the name of a folder: it holds `/`, which separates the folders of a path (write a plain word, such as `journey`)
----

`archicode plateau list` reads the tree of the plateaus only, and `archicode editor serve` loads nothing: neither looks at the facets. The editor refuses the workspace with the same sentence when it opens the folder, and *Add facet*, in *Settings* (see <<editor-facets>>), does not write such a name. A workspace that had a facet called `a/b` loaded in 0.18.0 and does not now: rename the facet, and its folder if you keep the old one (see <<upgrade-0-18>>). The facets of every example of this manual use plain words.
====

[NOTE]
.A facet can remove an element, and what points at it goes with it
====
`REMOVE` is not only for relationships: an action can select elements, such as `$.application..elements[?(@.id == 'catalog')]`. The element is removed with the elements inside it, and so is everything of the workspace that pointed at it or at one of them: the relationships whose `destination` is one of them, the entries of `uses` of an environment, and the entries of `applications` of a node. The views of the facet are then written without them; they do not stop with `dangling reference`. The facet says nothing when it does so, it is what it asked for. The workspace and its other facets are untouched.

[source,yaml]
----
settings:
  facets:
    customs:
      - name: "without-catalog"
        actions:
          - operator: REMOVE
            json-path: "$.application..elements[?(@.id == 'catalog')]"
----

With this facet, the view of `shop.web` in `views-without-catalog` no longer shows a `browses` link to `shop.catalog`, and no view of `shop.catalog` (the element, or a view of the manifests that is about it) is written for the facet. The same goes for a facet that selects on the state of a comparison (`$.application..elements[?(@.change == 'moved')]`). A reference that already pointed nowhere before the facet is not hidden by it: it is still reported as a `dangling reference`. A view of the manifests that is about the removed element, asked for with `views generate --view`, is an error that names the view and the facet (see <<plateau-views-lacking>>).
====

// - The relationships are qualified with the term _oidc_.

===== Step #3: Define the authorization facet

This facet, named _oidc_, filters out elements and relationships that aren't tagged with the term _oidc_. The result is a refined view where authorization concerns are not just visible but are the focal point, enabling a deeper, uncluttered insight into these specific interactions.

The `.archicode.yaml` file is enhanced with the definition of the _oidc_ facet. This inclusion is a significant stride towards achieving a tailored view that zeroes in on the authorization aspects of the architecture.

===== Step #4: Define the main facet

// - The forth step disable the default facet and define a new one for the main concern.
// - The task is the definition of the __main__ facet in the workspace configuration.
// - The name of the facet is __main__ so that the generated view folder will have the suffix __-main__.
// - The facet remove all relationships that are qualified with the term _oidc_.

In this step, we transition from the specific authorization focus, reintegrating into the broader view of the ecommerce platform. However, the approach is refined; we're disabling the default facet and defining a new one named _main_ to effectively manage the detailed views.

This newly defined _main_ facet is crucial; it's a tool that filters out all relationships tagged with the term _oidc_. The outcome is a holistic yet focused view of the ecommerce platform, excluding the specific authorization elements, for a balanced and comprehensive perspective.

The `.archicode.yaml` is updated to include the definition of the _main_ facet. This acts as a filter, excluding the _oidc_ qualified relationships, thus providing a clearer view of the primary architecture without the specific authorization elements.

It reveals a balanced view, where the specific authorization elements are excluded, ensuring that the primary architectural elements and relationships of the ecommerce platform are the focal point. It’s a testament to the adaptability and precision that the facet feature introduces to Archicode, affirming its role in enhancing the visualization and management of complex architectural designs.

===== Conclusion

// - The documentation has elucidated the process of utilizing facets in Archicode to streamline and organize complex workspace views.
// - The walkthrough exemplified the application of facets in refining views, particularly focusing on specific concerns like authorization and observability, without cluttering the main workspace.
// - By employing facets, practitioners can easily manage and visualize complex interactions in an organized manner, enhancing readability and understandability.

As we wrap up this insightful exploration, the potency and strategic advantage of utilizing facets in Archicode to streamline complex workspace views stand prominently unveiled. Each step of this guide, meticulously articulated, serves as a testament to the transformational impact of facets – a feature that elevates clarity, focus, and organization in the visualization of intricate architectural models.

.Reflecting on the Journey:
From the initiation of the workspace, where the complexity and detailed nature of architectural expressions were laid bare, to the seamless integration of specific concerns like authorization, each phase underscored a resonating need – the need for a tool that grants architects and designers the liberty to dive deep without drowning in complexity. The facet feature in Archicode emerges as this indispensable tool, offering a lifeline that transforms intricate views into organized, manageable, and insightful visual narratives.

.The Facet Advantage:
Through the practical application laid out in the steps, the prowess of facets in isolating and emphasizing specific concerns has been effectively illustrated. In the world of architectural design and modeling, where complexity is a constant companion, facets offer a bridge to clarity. They serve as a lens, focusing on distinct elements and relationships, making the comprehension of complex interactions not just possible but also intuitive.

.Looking Ahead:
The incorporation of facets in Archicode is not a mere enhancement; it is a pivotal evolution that underscores our commitment to empowering professionals to navigate the intricate landscapes of architectural design with confidence and precision. As we peer into the future, the continuous refinement and enhancement of this feature stand as a priority, promising users an ever-evolving tool that adapts and resonates with the dynamic nature of architectural design.

=== Source Code Organization

// - The best practices to organize the source code of a project using the tool ArchiCode.
// - ArchiCode works with workspace file.
// - The workspace centralizes the data, by default the name is `.archicode.yaml`.
// - As the content of workspace file grows, the management of the file becomes complicated.
// - Therefore, the manifest concept have been designed to solve big fat workspace files.
// - A manifest represents a single resource.
// - By default, ArchiCode discover manifests located in the folder "manifests" sibling to the workspace file.

Efficiently organizing your source code is a cornerstone of successful architectural endeavors, and ArchiCode is your companion in this journey. Let's delve into the best practices for structuring your source code using ArchiCode:

- Workspace Dynamics: The workspace file, named `.archicode.yaml` by default, serves as a centralized repository for your architectural data.

- Scaling Challenges: As your architectural vision flourishes, the workspace file's contents may expand, leading to management complexities. Recognizing the need for efficient management, ArchiCode introduces the concept of manifests. A manifest embodies a singular architectural resource, enabling focused and structured representation. With manifests, ArchiCode empowers you to break down complex elements into distinct resources, enhancing manageability and granularity. ArchiCode streamlines the process of manifest discovery by default. It effortlessly detects manifests residing in the "manifests" folder, positioned as a sibling to the workspace file.

.Example of an ArchiCode project directory
----
- project_dir/ <1>
    - manifests/ <2>
        - application/ <3>
            - <FILENAME>.{yaml,json,toml} <4>
            - ....
        - technology/ <5>
            - <FILENAME>.{yaml,json,toml}
            - ....
    - views/ <6>
        - <RENDERER NAME>/ <7>
            - application/ <8>
                - <VIEW_ID>.<EXTENSION> <9>
                - ....
            - technoloy/
                - <VIEW_ID>.<EXTENSION>
                - ....
    - .archicode.yaml <10>
----
<1> The directory of the project.
<2> The directory containing the manifests.
<3> An arbitrary directory containing the manifest related to the Application layer.
<4> A manifest file.
<5> An arbitrary directory containing the manifest related to the Technology layer.
<6> The directory containing the generated views.
<7> By convention, Render renders their views in subdirectory matching the Render's name.
<8> By convention, Render renders views in directory layers.
<9> By convention, the filename of rendered views is the view identifier. The extension depends on the Renderer.
<10> The workspace file is mandatory even if empty.

//-

* Manifest Diversity: ArchiCode efficiently handles multiple manifest directories, facilitating the seamless incorporation of manifests, including those from package managers like NPM. This versatility enables architects to integrate diverse architectural elements from various sources. This integration showcases ArchiCode's adaptability and its role in enhancing architectural endeavors.

.Example of manifest provided by NPM modules
----
- project_dir/ <1>
    - manifests/ <2>
    - node_modules/ <3>
        - <MODULE_NAME>/ <4>
    - .archicode.yaml <5>
----
<1> The directory of the project.
<2> The directory containing the manifests defined in the project.
<3> The directory containing the installed NPM modules.
<4> The directory containing the NPM module importing the other manifests.
<5> The `.archicode.yaml` must declare both directories `manifests` and `node_modules/<MODULE_NAME>` to be able to discover all manifests

== Appendix

=== Data Structures

==== Base Element

All elements share the attributes of the Base Element.

.Attributes of the Base Element
|===
|Name |Type |Comment

|id
|string
|The identifier of the element. Its format should match the variable name in Java or Javascript.

|name
|string
|The human name of the element.

|description
|string
|A short description of the element, usually between 25 and 75 words.

|qualifiers
|string
|A list of specific characteristics that helps define or categorize the element.
They are usually rendered in views.
It can be the name of the main framework used to implement a container: Spring Boot.
Or it can be the name of the RDMS for a database Container : PostgreSQL.

|tags
|string
|A list of key/value pairs that helps to drive the rendering of views.
For instance, to assign a predefined shape to the element or to categorize the element during a threat modeling process.
The tag `rendering-stereotypes` is a whitespace-separated list of stereotypes the styles apply to (see <<stereotypes-and-styles>>).

|relationships
|string
|The list relationships where the current element is the source.

|replaces
|a list of strings
|The full references of the elements of the plateau it is compared with that this element replaces (see <<plateau-replaces>>). Optional.

|===

==== Application Layer

.Attributes of the Application Layer
|===
|Name |Type |Comment

|elements
|an array of Solution or System elements
|A list of embedded objects.

|===

==== Solution

The Solution Element inherits the attributes of the Base Element.

.Attributes of the Solution Element
|===
|Name |Type |Comment

|elements
|an array of System elements
|A list of embedded objects.

|===

==== System

The System Element inherits the attributes of the Base Element.

.Attributes of the System Element
|===
|Name |Type |Comment

|elements
|an array of Container elements
|A list of embedded objects.

|===

==== Container

The Container Element inherits the attributes of the Base Element.

.Attributes of the Container Element
|===
|Name |Type |Comment

|elements
|an array of Component elements
|A list of embedded objects.

|===

==== Component

The Component Element inherits the attributes of the Base Element.

==== Technology Layer

.Attributes of the Technology Layer
|===
|Name |Type |Comment

|elements
|an array of Environment elements
|A list of embedded objects.

|===

==== Environment

The Environment Element inherits the attributes of the Base Element.

.Attributes of the Environment Element
|===
|Name |Type |Comment

|elements
|an array of Node elements
|A list of embedded objects.

|===

==== Node

The Node Element inherits the attributes of the Base Element.

.Attributes of the Node Element
|===
|Name |Type |Comment

|applications
|an array of references which refrence elements of the Application Layer
|The list of reference elements.

|elements
|an array of Node elements
|A list of embedded objects.

|===

==== Relationship

.Attributes of Relationship
|===
|Name |Type |Comment

|destination
|string
|The reference of the destination element within the same layer.

|label
|string
|A human-readable text defining the relationship.

|qualifiers
|a list of strings
|A list of specific characteristics that helps define or categorize the relationship.
They are usually rendered in views.
It can be the name of the protocol implementing the link.

|tags
|a list of ky/value pairs
|A list of key/value pairs that helps to drive the rendering of views.
For instance, to set TCP ports and IPs driving the rendering of Kubernetes Network Policy manifests.

|===

==== View

.Attributes of View
|===
|Name |Type |Comment

|id
|string
|The identifier of the view.
Its format should match the variable name in Java or Javascript.

|viewpoint
|string
|The code of the viewpoint identifying the viewpoint service.

|layer
|string
|The reference data model of the viewpoint: `application` or `technology`. Both tools read it in any case (`Technology` and `TECHNOLOGY` are the technology layer).

|name
|string
|The human name of the element.

|description
|string
|A short description of the element, usually between 25 and 75 words.

|properties
|any
|A custom object containing properties to drive the execution of the viewpoint and/or renderer service. The `map` viewpoint reads `pinned`, `hidden` and `opened` on the application layer, `links`, the relationships to draw (see <<map-links>>), `highlight`, the relationships to point at (see <<map-highlight>>), and, on the technology layer, the booleans `applications` and `relationships` (see <<map-viewpoint>>).

|===

==== Story

The content of a manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/story` (see <<stories>>).

.Attributes of Story
|===
|Name |Type |Comment

|id
|string
|Required. The identifier of the story, declared once in the workspace.

|name
|string
|The human name of the story.

|description
|string
|What the story is about, in a few words.

|steps
|a list of Step
|The steps, in the order they are told.

|===

==== Step

An item of the `steps` of a Story. A step names a view (`view`) or carries one (`viewpoint`, `layer`, `properties`).

.Attributes of Step
|===
|Name |Type |Comment

|view
|string
|The `id` of a view of the workspace.

|viewpoint, layer, properties, name
|as a View
|The view the step carries, when it names none.

|text
|string
|What to say about the view, as plain text.

|at
|string
|The `id` of the plateau the step is told at. Absent: the plateau that is drawn (see <<story-step-plateaus>>).

|against
|string
|The `id` of the plateau the step is compared with. Absent: no comparison.

|===

==== Plateau

The content of a manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau` (see <<plateau-manifest>>).

.Attributes of Plateau
|===
|Name |Type |Comment

|id
|string
|Required. The identifier of the plateau: lowercase letters, digits, `_` and `-`. `baseline` is the root.

|name
|string
|The human name of the plateau.

|horizon
|string
|A free label for when the plateau is meant to hold, such as `2027-Q1`.

|override
|string
|The id of the plateau it is a layer over; `baseline` when absent.

|patches
|a list of Patch
|What the plateau changes in what it inherits, applied in order.

|===

==== Patch

An item of the `patches` of a Plateau (see <<plateau-patches>>).

.Attributes of Patch
|===
|Name |Type |Comment

|operator
|string
|Required. `REMOVE`, `SET`, `ADD` or `MERGE`.

|target
|string
|The full reference of one element. Exclusive with `json-path`.

|layer
|string
|The layer of `target`: `application` (default) or `technology`.

|json-path
|string
|A JSONPath (RFC 9535) that selects the nodes to change. Exclusive with `target`.

|expect
|integer
|The number of nodes `json-path` must select, at least 1.

|field
|string
|The field of the element that is changed.

|where
|an object
|`destination` and `label`: finds one relationship of the element, for a `SET` of the field `relationships`.

|value
|any
|The new value (`SET`), the item or the list of items added (`ADD`), or the keys set (`MERGE`). `REMOVE` takes none.

|===
